Linear Realisability over nets: multiplicatives
Adrien Ragot, Thomas Seiller, Lorenzo Tortora de Falco

Abstract
We provide a new realisability model based on orthogonality for the multiplicative fragment of linear logic, both in presence of generalised axioms ( 𝖬𝖫𝖫 ✠ ) (\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}) and in the standard case ( 𝖬𝖫𝖫 ) (\mathsf{MLL}) . The novelty is the definition of cut elimination for generalised axioms. We prove that our model is adequate and complete both for 𝖬𝖫𝖫 ✠ \mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese} and 𝖬𝖫𝖫 \mathsf{MLL} .



stix@largesymbols”0E
stix@largesymbols”0F

Introduction

Since the inception of Linear Logic (
\mathsf{LL}
),
proofs are represented
as graphs that naturally live in a wider space of agents
called proof structures (
nets
in this paper) that can freely interact.
These nets
were introduced by J.Y. Girard in
[
7
]
,
together with the
desequentialisation
:
a simple process transforming proof trees from the sequent calculus
of
\mathsf{LL}
into nets.
However, not
every net is the desequentialisation of a proof:
it is
impossible
to extract a proof tree
from
a net that “contains” cycles or disconnections
[
5
]
.
Nets can therefore present forms of (what we call)
geometrical incorrectness
,
and geometrically correct nets are (representants of) proof trees of
\mathsf{LL}
.
More recently,
J.Y. Girard proposed
Ludics
,
an interpretation of
\mathsf{LL}

given in terms of “desseins”:
proof trees of the
\mathsf{LL}
sequent calculus with the addition of the daimon
(\maltese)
rule,
a generalised axiom allowing to prove any sequent.
Ludics introduces a new kind of incorrectness that we call
provability incorrectness
:
dessein are geometrically correct (they are proof trees)
but can be provably incorrect.
In the standard theory of proof nets
geometrical and provability correctness coincide;
it is the presence of daimons that
allows to distinguish
between provability correctness and geometrical correctness.

Understanding the relationship between correctness
and computational behavior
is (one of) the goal(s) of
realisability
,
which,
restricted to
LL
, will be our focus in this paper.
We briefly sum up
the existing works on
linear realisability
1
1

1

We use the expression linear realisability
in the sense of
[
20
]

i.e. realisability
models for
LL
.

by positioning them with respect to
Table 1
.
We also recall if these models enjoy completeness or not.
Two lines of research on realisability of
\mathsf{LL}

can be identified.

One
was initiated by V. De Paiva
Dialectica Interpretation

[
6
]

and led to
P. Oliva’s adequate and complete realisability model
of first order
\mathsf{LL}

[
14
]

where
realisers are
proof trees (with standard axioms)
from a decorated
sequent calculus of
\mathsf{LL}
.
As a consequence
realisers are typed and are “by construction”
geometrically and provably correct

(placing this model in the top left
corner of
Table 1
).

The other
originates in the work of J.Y. Girard:

Ludics

[
10
]
,
whose “desseins” are geometrically correct
but can be provably incorrect
(top right corner of
Table 1
),
which enjoys
completeness
.
E. Beffara proposed adequate models
in a concurrent
\pi
-calculus
[
2
]

and conjunctive structure
[
3
]
.
T. Seiller’s
interaction graphs

(inspired by Girard’s Geometry of Interaction
[
8
]
)
model various
LL
fragments adequately

[
15
,
18
,
19
,
16
,
17
]
.
Beffara’s and Seiller’s
approaches exhibit both geometrical and provability incorrectness
(bottom-right corner of
Table 1
),
but contain no completeness result.

We give the first complete realisability model
of the multiplicative fragment of
linear logic in terms of nets,
essentially the well–known untyped proof–structures
of
\mathsf{LL}

[
9
]

with
daimons
,
as in the work of P.L. Curien
[
4
]
:
this places us in the bottom–right corner of
Table 1
.
The main tool we use in our approach
to realisability is
\mathsf{LL}
cut elimination:
we interpret formulas as types, sets of nets closed under bi–orthogonality,
where the notion of orthogonality
is defined via the rewriting rules of nets induced by cut elimination.
We prove completeness for
\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}
, multiplicative
\mathsf{LL}

with generalised axioms,
meaning our model can capture

geometrical correctness
.
As a byproduct we obtain completeness
for the standard multiplicative fragment of linear logic (
\mathsf{MLL}
), thus capturing

provability correctness
.

Although not expressed in the terms of realisability,
a completeness result for
\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}

(in the atomic case)
using a notion of orthogonality
is already apparent in the work of P.L. Curien
[
4
]
,
where the partitions involved in the Danos Regnier criterion
[
5
]

are encoded using daimons.
More precisely,
one can test the geometrical correctness of a net
by confronting it against carefully chosen
opponents
(which as in the work of Béchet
[
1
]

are geometrically correct nets).
However the method in
[
4
]

does not allow to derive a completeness result for
\mathsf{MLL}
.
By contrast,
we use
geometrically incorrect
opponents to prove completeness for
\mathsf{MLL}
(
87
).

The novelty is the
cut elimination
of non-homogenous cuts
(a generalised axiom against a connective -- say a tensor):
unlike in Ludics

2
2

2

In Ludics, the daimon means the end of the game,
or the end of the proof search.
[
10
]

our daimon is the “perfect” opponent/evaluation context;
it never stops responding during computation
and
never prevents proof search to go on
(
Figure 4
and
24
).
These new cut elimination steps
are key to
interactively identify provability correctness
and so to
obtain our completeness result for
\mathsf{MLL}
(
86
).
The computational behavior of the daimon
also differs from Krivine’s continuations
involved in
classical realisability

[
12
]
:
they restore a previously stored context while
our daimon rather behaves like an adaptive evaluation context.

\mathsf{MLL}

\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}

Proof Nets

no incorrectness

provability incorrectness

Nets

geometrical incorrectness

geometrical and provability incorrectness

Table 1
:
Presence of incorrectness,
restricted to multiplicative linear logic,
for
realisability models.

The general aim is to understand
the computational content of proofs and of (incorrect) nets,
following a “purely interactive approach to logic” (to quote
[
10
]
).
We follow the approach initiated with Ludics,
we present a framework in which proofs and refutations
are objects of the same nature that can freely interact:
a proof–object proves a formula
A
whenever
it “defeats” all the refutations of
A
.
The correctness of an object
is evaluated using a dynamic criterion (we make an object interact with each of its refutations)
rather than a static one (such as a typing discipline).

Outline.

In
section 1
,
we give a detailed introduction of nets
that we define as ordered hypergraphs.
In
section 2
,
we recall the elementary notions
of multiplicative linear logic, we introduce the
\maltese
-links
and we formulate the criterion of Danos Regnier
[
5
]

in our setting.
In
section 3
, we define orthogonality between two nets as
“successful interaction” through cut elimination (Definition
42
); this leads to the notion of
type: a set of nets closed under bi–orthogonality. We then show how to perform the usual multiplicative constructions
in the framework of types.
In
section 4
, we define our realisability model
interpreting formulas as types and we prove its adequacy:
a net representing a proof of
A

is a realiser of
A
(Theorem
64
).
In
section 5
,
we relate correctness criteria with orthogonality.
The Danos-Regnier criterion applied to a cut-free net with conclusion
A

yields a set of nets called tests (Definition
74
).
We prove that the tests of
A
are proofs of
A^{\bot}
(Theorem
77
)
and that the interaction between a net
\pi
with conclusion
A

and its tests allows to determine whether or not
\pi
is indeed a proof:
we thus extend to our framework a result of Béchet
[
1
]
.
In
section 6
, we prove the completeness of our realisability model: if
a net
S
realises
A
(in every basis), then
S
is a proof of
A
in
\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}
(Theorem
85
).
Finally
we show that completeness of
\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}

implies that of
\mathsf{MLL}
(Theorem
88
).

1
Untyped nets

We introduce the
framework of
nets
in which
our construction takes place.
Nets are a special kind of
directed hypergraphs

together with an order of
some
of their vertices
which will come in play later on to define the notion of orthogonality.
These hypergraphs enjoy a natural notion of sum (
6
).
In subsection
1.2
,
we define our “realisers” that we call nets
and
their computational rules,
the cut elimination procedure
as known for multiplicative proof structures
[
9
]

but with a novelty: the generalised axiom or daimon–link
(\maltese)

which behave like an adaptative evaluation context.

1.1
Directed hypergraphs

Given a set
X
we will let
\mathcal{P}_{\leq}(X)

denote the set of
totally ordered finite subsets of
X
.
An element of
\mathcal{P}_{\leq}(X)
is equivalently
a finite sequence of elements of
X
but,

without repetitions
.

Definition 1
.

Suppose given a set
L
of
labels
.
A
directed (
L
-labelled) hypergraph
is
a tuple
(V,E,\mathrm{s},\mathrm{t},\ell)

where
V
is a finite set of
positions

and
E
is a finite set
of
links
,

\mathrm{s}:E\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{\leq}(V)
is the

source map
,

\mathrm{t}:E\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{\leq}(V)

is the
target map

and

\ell:E\rightarrow L
is the
labelling
map.

Given a link
e\in E
,
since the finite sets
\mathrm{t}(e)
and
\mathrm{s}(e)

are totally ordered,
to support readability
we will
represent them as sequences: they are respectively
called the
target
and the
source

sets of
e
.
A
source
(resp. target) of
e

is an element
of its source (resp. target) set
\mathrm{s}(e)
(resp.
\mathrm{t}(e)
).
The set of
targets and sources of
e

is the
domain
of the link
e
.
We will use superscripts
to denote sequences of positions
(
{\overline{p}},{\overline{q}},{\overline{u}},\ldots
).
A link is a
loop

when its target set and source set
are not disjoint.

Convention.

Along this work
we assume all the hypergraphs to be loop–free
i.e. containing only links which are not loops.

Given an hypergraph
\mathcal{H}

with
E
as its set of links,
we denote

\mathrm{s}(\mathcal{H})
(resp.
\mathrm{t}(\mathcal{H})
)
the set of all positions which
are
source (resp. target) of at least one link:

\mathrm{s}(\mathcal{H})=\bigcup_{e\in E}\mathrm{s}(e),\qquad\mathrm{t}(\mathcal{H})=\bigcup_{e\in E}\mathrm{t}(e).

A
conclusion/output
(resp. a
premise/input
)
of a directed hypergraph
\mathcal{H}

is a position which is the source (resp. target)
of no link in
\mathcal{H}
, i.e.
an element of
V\setminus\mathrm{s}(\mathcal{H})

(resp. of
V\setminus\mathrm{t}(\mathcal{H})
).
The set of conclusions (resp. premises)
of an hypergraph
\mathcal{H}

is denoted
\mathsf{out}(\mathcal{H})

(resp.
\mathsf{in}(\mathcal{H})
).
A position
p
is
isolated

in an hypergraph
\mathcal{H}

if
p
is both an output and
an input of
\mathcal{H}
,
i.e.

p\notin\mathrm{s}(\mathcal{H})\cup\mathrm{t}(\mathcal{H})
.
The
size
of a directed hypergraph
is the number of its links.
There is a unique empty hypergraph

\mathcal{H}=(V,E,\mathrm{s},\mathrm{t},\ell)

with

V=E=\mathrm{s}=\mathrm{t}=\emptyset
.

An
isomorphism

of hypergraphs

f:(V_{1},E_{1},\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathrm{t}_{1},\ell_{1})\rightarrow(V_{2},E_{2},\mathrm{s}_{2},\mathrm{t}_{2},\ell_{2})

is a pair of functions
(f_{V},f_{E})

such that
f_{V}:V_{1}\rightarrow V_{2}

and
f_{E}:E_{1}\rightarrow E_{2}
are bijections,

f_{E}
preserve labels i.e.

\ell(f_{E}(e))=\ell(e)
,
and
f_{E}
preserves the target and source of a link,
i.e.

\mathrm{s}_{2}(f_{E}(e))=f_{V}^{*}(\mathrm{s}_{1}(e))

and

\mathrm{t}_{2}(f_{E}(e))=f_{V}^{*}(\mathrm{t}_{1}(e))
,
where
f_{V}^{*}
is the natural extension of
f_{V}

to sequences of positions.
Along this work we work
with hypergraphs up to isomorphism.

Notation 2
.

We denote
\mathopen{\langle}\overline{u}\vartriangleright_{l}\overline{v}\mathclose{\rangle}

the hypergraph
(V,E,\mathrm{s},\mathrm{t},\ell)

such that
E=\{e\}
,

V=\mathrm{s}(e)\cup\mathrm{t}(e)
,

\mathrm{s}(e)=\overline{u}
,

\mathrm{t}(e)=\overline{v}

and
\ell(e)=l

(an example of such a single–link hypergraph
is found in
1(a)
).
In the sequel

\mathopen{\langle}\overline{u}\vartriangleright_{l}\overline{v}\mathclose{\rangle}

will denote both
the described hypergraph
and its unique link.

Notation 3
.

We write
u\cdot v
the concatenation of sequences.
Given
u=(u_{1},\dots,u_{n})
a sequence
of elements of a set
X
and an integer
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
,
we denote by
u_{<i}

(resp.
u_{>i}
)
the sequence
(u_{1},\dots,u_{i-1})

(resp.
(u_{i+1},\dots,u_{n})
).
Moreover, given two – potentially empty – sequences
u
and
v

we denote by

u[i\leftarrow v]

the sequence
u_{<i}\cdot v\cdot u_{>i}
.

A link is
initial
(resp.
final
)
when it has no input (resp. no output).
A position is
initial
(resp.
final
)
when it is an output (resp. input)
of an initial (resp. final) link.
In an hypergraph
\mathcal{H}
,
a link
e
is
terminal

when every target of
e
is a conclusion of
\mathcal{H}

– thus a final link is a terminal link.

Example 4
.

For instance a link
\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\ell}a,b,c\mathclose{\rangle}
is an initial link
and the positions
a,b
and
c
are initial,
on the other hand a link

\mathopen{\langle}a,b\vartriangleright_{\ell}c\mathclose{\rangle}

is not initial and neither are the positions
a,b
or
c
.

Hypergraphs
enjoy a natural notion of sum
based on the disjoint union of the set of links.

Notation 5
.

Given two sets
X_{0}
and
X_{1}

we denote
X_{0}\uplus X_{1}
the set
X_{0}\cup X_{1}

whenever
X_{0}
and
X_{1}
are disjoint.
Given two functions
f:X_{0}\rightarrow E

and
g:X_{1}\rightarrow E
with disjoint domains
we denote

f\uplus g

the function which takes an element
x

of

X_{0}\uplus X_{1}
,
and returns

f(x)
if
x\in X_{0}

and

g(x)
if
x\in X_{1}
.

Definition 6
.

Given two hypergraphs

\mathcal{H}_{1}=(V_{1},E_{1},\mathrm{t}_{1},\mathrm{s}_{1},\ell_{1})

and

\mathcal{H}_{2}=(V_{2},E_{2},\mathrm{t}_{2},\mathrm{s}_{2},\ell_{2})

such that
E_{1}\cap E_{2}=\emptyset
.
The sum of
\mathcal{H}_{1}

and
\mathcal{H}_{2}
is defined as:

\mathcal{H}_{1}+\mathcal{H}_{2}=(V_{1}\cup V_{2},E_{1}\uplus E_{2},\mathrm{t}_{1}\uplus\mathrm{t}_{2},\mathrm{s}_{1}\uplus\mathrm{s}_{2},\ell_{1}\uplus\ell_{2}).

d
a
b
c
e
\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\alpha

(a)

The representation
of the single–link hypergraph

\mathopen{\langle}a,b,c\vartriangleright_{\alpha}d,e\mathclose{\rangle}

(
2
).

d
a
b
c
e
\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\alpha
\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\beta
u
v

(b)

The sum of two single link
hypergraphs
\mathopen{\langle}a,b,c\vartriangleright_{\alpha}d,e\mathclose{\rangle}

and
\mathopen{\langle}b,u\vartriangleright_{\beta}v\mathclose{\rangle}

(
6
).

d
a
b
c
e
\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\alpha
\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\beta
u
v
b^{\prime}

(c)

The parallel sum of the two single link hypergraphs

\mathopen{\langle}a,b,c\vartriangleright_{\alpha}d,e\mathclose{\rangle}

and
\mathopen{\langle}b,u\vartriangleright_{\beta}v\mathclose{\rangle}
(
11
).

Figure 1
:
Hypergraphs can naturally be represented
in a graphical way,
we illustrate the notation of a hypergraph containing a single link,
the sum of hypergraphs and the parallel sum of hypergraphs.
In
1(c)

The position
b
is present in both hypergraphs
therefore we rename it in one of the two hypergraphs:
thus
\mathopen{\langle}a,b,c\vartriangleright_{\alpha}d,e\mathclose{\rangle}\parallel\mathopen{\langle}b,u\vartriangleright_{\beta}v\mathclose{\rangle}

equals
\mathopen{\langle}a,b,c\vartriangleright_{\alpha}d,e\mathclose{\rangle}\parallel\mathopen{\langle}b^{\prime},u\vartriangleright_{\beta}v\mathclose{\rangle}
(that is, upto isomorphism).

Remark 7
.

Whenever

\mathcal{H}_{1}=(V_{1},E_{1},\mathrm{t}_{1},\mathrm{s}_{1},\ell_{1})

and
\mathcal{H}_{2}=(V_{2},E_{2},\mathrm{t}_{2},\mathrm{s}_{2},\ell_{2})

are such that
E_{1}\cap E_{2}\neq\emptyset
,
we will
abusively write their sum as
\mathcal{H}_{1}+\mathcal{H}_{2}=(V_{1}\cup V_{2},E_{1}\uplus E_{2},\mathrm{t}_{1}\uplus\mathrm{t}_{2},\mathrm{s}_{1}\uplus\mathrm{s}_{2},\ell_{1}\uplus\ell_{2})
,
since up to renaming
the sets of links of two hypergraphs
can always be considered disjoint.

Remark 8
.

Vertices may overlap in a sum (as we take the union
of vertex sets rather than the disjoint union).
As a consequence,
a position may be input (or output) of several distinct
links (
1(b)
).
We can describe hypergraphs
as sums of simple hypergraphs;
namely those that contain only one link.
Indeed using
2
, an hypergraph consisting of two links

\mathopen{\langle}\overline{a}\vartriangleright_{\ell}\overline{b}\mathclose{\rangle}

and

\mathopen{\langle}\overline{c}\vartriangleright_{\ell^{\prime}}\overline{d}\mathclose{\rangle}

is in fact equal to the sum of the
single-link hypergraphs

\mathopen{\langle}\overline{a}\vartriangleright_{\ell}\overline{b}\mathclose{\rangle}
and

\mathopen{\langle}\overline{c}\vartriangleright_{\ell^{\prime}}\overline{d}\mathclose{\rangle}
.
By induction on the number of links,
this shows that any hypergraph
\mathcal{H}

without isolated positions can be written as
\mathcal{H}=\sum_{e\in E}\mathopen{\langle}\mathrm{s}(e)\vartriangleright_{\ell(e)}\mathrm{t}(e)\mathclose{\rangle}
.

Example 9
.

In the hypergraph

\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\ell 1}a,b,c\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}a\vartriangleright_{\ell 2}d\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\ell 3}e\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}e\vartriangleright_{\ell 4}\mathclose{\rangle}

the set of initial positions is
\{a,b,c,e\}
,
while
e
is the only final position of the hypergraph,
and it belongs to the domain of the unique final link
\mathopen{\langle}e\vartriangleright_{\ell 4}\mathclose{\rangle}
.

Remark 10
.

The sum of hypergraphs
enjoys the properties of an abelian monoid;
associativity,
commutativity,
and a neutral element
which is the empty hypergraph.

We will also
use extensively
the notion
of
parallel composition

or
parallel sum

of hypergraphs,
an analogue of the
union–graph

of two simple graphs.

Definition 11
.

Given

\mathcal{H}_{1}=(V_{1},E_{1},\mathrm{t}_{1},\mathrm{s}_{1},\ell_{1})

and

\mathcal{H}_{2}=(V_{2},E_{2},\mathrm{t}_{2},\mathrm{s}_{2},\ell_{2})
two hypergraphs
such that
V_{1}\cap V_{2}=E_{1}\cap E_{2}=\emptyset
,
we define their

parallel sum
as:

\mathcal{H}_{1}\parallel\mathcal{H}_{2}=(V_{1}\uplus V_{2},E_{1}\uplus E_{2},\mathrm{t}_{1}\uplus\mathrm{t}_{2},\mathrm{s}_{1}\uplus\mathrm{s}_{2},\ell_{1}\uplus\ell_{2})
.

Remark 12
.

The parallel sum of two hypergraphs

\mathcal{H}_{1}
and
\mathcal{H}_{2}

corresponds to a regular sum
whenever the sets of vertices
are disjoint.
Just like the sum,
parallel composition
can always be performed between two hypergraphs
(up to a renaming, see
1(c)
).

A hypergraph
\mathcal{H}=(V,E,\mathrm{t},\mathrm{s},\ell)
is:
(1)
target–surjective

whenever
\mathrm{t}(\mathcal{H})=V
,
(2)
source–disjoint

if the sets
\mathrm{s}(e)
for
e\in E

are pairwise disjoint,
(3)
target–disjoint

if the sets
\mathrm{t}(e)
for
e\in E

are pairwise disjoint (
Figure 2
).
A
module
is an hypergraph
which is target–disjoint and source–disjoint,
which means that for each position
p

there exists
at most
one link
e

such that
\mathrm{s}(e)
(resp.
\mathrm{t}(e)
) contains
p
.
Any single–link hypergraph is a module.
Uncarefully summing two modules does not necessarily
result in a module;
for instance
the single link hypergraphs

e=\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\ell}a\mathclose{\rangle}
and
e^{\prime}=\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\ell^{\prime}}a\mathclose{\rangle}

are both modules but their sum isn’t
as
a
is the target of the two links
e
and
e^{\prime}
.

d
a
b
c
e
\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\alpha
\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\beta
u
v

(a)

The sum of two single link hypergraphs

\mathopen{\langle}a,b,c\vartriangleright_{\alpha}d,e\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}b,u\vartriangleright_{\beta}v\mathclose{\rangle}
.
The hypergraph is target–disjoint, but
because
b
belongs to the source of both links
it is not source–disjoint,
it is also not target surjective.

d

c
e
\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\alpha
\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\beta

The sum of two single link hypergraphs

\mathopen{\langle}a,b,c\vartriangleright_{\alpha}d,e\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}e,u\vartriangleright_{\beta}v\mathclose{\rangle}
.
The hypergraph is target–disjoint and source–disjoint,
however it is not target surjective.

The sum of four single link hypergraphs

\mathopen{\langle}a,b,c\vartriangleright_{\alpha}d,e\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}e,u\vartriangleright_{\beta}v\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\gamma}a,b\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\delta}c,u\mathclose{\rangle}
.
The hypergraph is target–disjoint, source–disjoint,
and target surjective.

Figure 2
:
Properties of hypergraphs: source–disjoint, target–disjoint and target–surjective hypergraphs.

An
arrangement

of a directed hypergraph
\mathcal{H}

is a total order
<_{\mathbf{a}}
on its set of conclusions;
equivalently
the order may be identified
as a bijection

\mathbf{a}:\{1,\dots,card(\mathsf{out}(\mathcal{H}))\}\rightarrow\mathsf{out}(\mathcal{H})
.
An
ordered hypergraph
is a pair
(\mathcal{H},\mathbf{a})

of an hypergraph
\mathcal{H}
together with
an arrangement
\mathbf{a}
of
\mathcal{H}
.
Given an ordered hypergraph
(\mathcal{H},\mathbf{a})

with
n
conclusions
for an integer
1\leq i\leq n
,
we denote
\mathbf{a}(i)

by
\mathcal{H}(i)
whenever there is no ambiguity.
The arrangement
\mathbf{a}

is denoted
\mathbf{a}(\mathcal{H})
,
and we might refer
to
\mathcal{H}

as the
unordered hypergraph underlying

(\mathcal{H},\mathbf{a}).

For
n,m\in\mathbb{N}

we denote by
\mathbf{[}n;m\mathbf{]}

the set
of integers

i
such that
n\leq i\leq m
.
Given two functions

f:[1;n]\rightarrow E

and
g:[1;m]\rightarrow E

we denote

f\mathop{\sqcup\mkern-10.5mu{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}\mkern 2.5mug:[1;m+n]\rightarrow E

the function such that

f\mathop{\sqcup\mkern-10.5mu{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}\mkern 2.5mug(i)=f(i)
when
1\leq i\leq n

and
f\mathop{\sqcup\mkern-10.5mu{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}\mkern 2.5mug(i)=g(i-n)
when
n+1\leq i\leq n+m
.
This operation is not commutative.
The parallel sum of two ordered
hypergraph
(\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathbf{a}_{1})
and
(\mathcal{H}_{2},\mathbf{a}_{2})

naturally
yields an ordered hypergraph
as
(\mathcal{H}_{1}\parallel\mathcal{H}_{2},\mathbf{a}_{1}\mathop{\sqcup\mkern-10.5mu{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}\mkern 2.5mu\mathbf{a}_{2})

(note that however this is not a commutative operation).

1.2
Multiplicative nets

Up to this point we have allowed
any kind of link to occur in a hypergraph. We now
consider
untyped multiplicative nets
in which only some specific kinds of links occur.
We fix the set of labels
as the set made
of the
daimon
(
\maltese
)
the tensor (
\otimes
)
the par (
\invamp
)
and the cut (
\mathsf{cut}
) symbol.
Furthermore we fix
a family of links,
namely

\maltese
-labelled links that have no inputs (they are initial links),

\mathsf{cut}
-labelled links that have exactly two inputs and no outputs (they are final links),

\otimes
- and
\invamp
-labelled links that have exactly two inputs and one output.
As a consequence, the hypergraphs considered will closely resemble
to multiplicative linear logic proof structures, with two important points of divergence:
the absence of typing and the presence of generalised axioms,
a standard
\mathsf{MLL}
axiom link can be seen as daimon link
with two conclusions

3
3

3

To be precise one should say that an
atomic
standard
\mathsf{MLL}
axiom link

is
a daimon link
with two conclusions (
29
).
.

Formally we fix a countable set
\mathsf{Pos}
of positions
and a family of links
\mathcal{L}
defined as:

\mathcal{L}\triangleq\{\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},p_{2}\vartriangleright_{\otimes}p\mathclose{\rangle},~\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},p_{2}\vartriangleright_{\invamp}p\mathclose{\rangle},~\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},p_{2}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}\mid p_{1},p_{2},p\in\mathsf{Pos}\}\quad\cup\quad\{\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{1},\dots,p_{n}\mathclose{\rangle}\mid n\in\mathbb{N},p_{1},\dots,p_{n}\in\mathsf{Pos}\}.

Definition 13
.

A multiplicative module is
an ordered hypergraph
M=(\left|{M}\right|,\mathbf{a}(M))

where
\left|{M}\right|
is a sum of links of
\mathcal{L}
which is a module.

A multiplicative net
is a multiplicative module
S=(\left|{S}\right|,\mathbf{a}(S))

where
\left|{S}\right|
is target–surjective.

From now on we will omit the word
multiplicative

but a module (resp. net) will always be
a multiplicative module (resp. net).
For a module
M
(resp. a net
S
)
we refer to
\left|{M}\right|
(resp.
\left|{S}\right|
)
as the unordered hypergraph underlying
M
(resp.
S
).
An
unordered
module (resp. net)
is the unordered hypergraph
underlying a module (resp. net).

Remark 14
.

For two nets

S_{1}=(V_{1},E_{1},\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathrm{t}_{1},\ell_{1})

and

S_{2}=(V_{2},E_{2},\mathrm{s}_{2},\mathrm{t}_{2},\ell_{2})
,
if
S_{1}+S_{2}
remains a net
then

S_{1}+S_{2}=S_{1}\parallel S_{2}
.
Indeed,
by
6
,

E_{1}\cap E_{2}=\emptyset
.
Then,
by target–disjointness

\mathrm{t}(S_{1})\cap\mathrm{t}(S_{2})=\emptyset
;
and finally because
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
are target surjective
we have
V_{1}\cap V_{2}=\mathrm{t}(S_{1})\cap\mathrm{t}(S_{2})=\emptyset
,
so that
11

applies.

Notation 15
.

Given an integer
n

we denote by
\maltese_{n}

any multiplicative net
consisting of a single daimon link
with
n
outputs,
i.e. isomorphic
to
\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{1},\dots,p_{n}\mathclose{\rangle}
.

Definition 16
.

Given a multiplicative net

S

the
type
of a cut link
c=\mathopen{\langle}p,q\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

occurring in
S

is the multiset of the two labels
of the links of output
p
and
q
;
for readability
we write these multisets as ordered pairs.
Thus there are six

types
of cuts (up to symmetry).
More precisely, we distinguish:

multiplicative
cuts, of type
(\otimes/\invamp)
;

clash
cuts, of type
(\otimes/\otimes)
or
(\invamp/\invamp)
;

glueing
cuts, of type
(\maltese/\maltese)
;

non–homogeneous
cuts, of type
(\otimes/\maltese)

or
(\invamp/\maltese)
,
which are respectively called

reversible
and
irreversible
cuts.
In a net
S
,
a cut
\mathopen{\langle}p,q\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

is
cyclic

whenever
p
and
q
are targets of the same link.

Remark 17
.

Each cut link occurring in a net
S

has a type since
a net is target–surjective.
However in a module this isn’t true:
for instance in the module
\mathopen{\langle}p,q\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

consisting of a single cut link,
the type of the cut link is not defined.

Remark 18
.

The inputs of a cut link
\mathopen{\langle}p,q\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}
are ordered,
making the two links

\mathopen{\langle}p,q\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

and
\mathopen{\langle}q,p\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}
distinct.
However (up to isomorphism) this plays no role during cut elimination.

:
Rules
defining the non–homogeneous cut elimination.
In the elimination of the
(\invamp/\maltese)
cut - first row -

\overline{a}=(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})

and
\overline{b}=(b_{1},\dots,b_{m})

while

\sigma(\overline{a})=(a_{\sigma(1)},\dots,a_{\sigma(k)})

,

\sigma^{\prime}(\overline{a})=(a_{\sigma^{\prime}(1)},\dots,a_{\sigma^{\prime}(k^{\prime})})

,

\tau^{\prime}(\overline{b})=(b_{\tau^{\prime}(1)},\dots,b_{\tau^{\prime}(h^{\prime})})

(with
n=k+k^{\prime}
and
m=h+h^{\prime}
)
are sequences that define a partition of

\{a_{1},\dots,a_{n},b_{1},\dots,b_{n}\}

more precisely

\{a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\}=\{a_{\sigma(1)},\dots,a_{\sigma(k)},a_{\sigma^{\prime}(1)},\dots,a_{\sigma^{\prime}(k^{\prime})}\}

and

\{b_{1},\dots,b_{n}\}=\{b_{\tau(1)},\dots,b_{\tau(h)},b_{\tau^{\prime}(1)},\dots,b_{\tau^{\prime}(h^{\prime})}\}
,
and
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime},\tau,\tau^{\prime}
are permutations.
Furthermore

p^{1},p^{2},q^{1},q^{2}
are fresh positions.
The figure is slightly misleading:

q_{1}
and
q_{2}

may be elements of
\overline{a}

or
\overline{b}
(in the first row)
while
p_{1}
and
p_{2}

may be elements of
q_{1},\dots,q_{i-1},q_{i+1},\dots,q_{n}
(in the second row),
these cases are illustrated in
Figure 13
.
This has an important consequence: a cut can belong to a cycle
and still be reducible (
28
).

Multicative nets
comes with their notion of computation
called
cut elimination
:
it is a rewriting on nets
and more precisely it rewrites
a redex (that is a sub–net made of a single cut link
and two non–cut links)
into redexes or daimons (in the very specific case of glueing cuts).
Up to isomorphism,
how a redex is rewritten
depends solely on
its type (
16
).

Definition 19
.

The relation of
homogeneous cut elimination

on unordered nets
is denoted by
\rightarrow_{h}

and it is the rewriting relation
defined as the contextual closure (with respect to the sum) of
the relation defined in
Figure 3
.

Remark 20
.

The (homogeneous) cut elimination procedure
on unordered nets leave the conclusions unchanged.
As a consequence the homogeneous cut elimination
can be lifted from unordered nets to nets:
whenever two unordered nets are such that

S\rightarrow S^{\prime}
,
for any arrangement
\mathbf{a}
of
S

we have
(S,\mathbf{a})\rightarrow(S^{\prime},\mathbf{a})
.

The following result is easily established, in particular since the number of links strictly decreases
during homogeneous cut elimination.

Proposition 21
.

Homogeneous cut elimination is confluent and strongly normalizing.

Definition 22
.

The non homogeneous reduction
is denoted
\rightarrow_{nh}

and it
is defined on unordered nets
as the contextual closure
of the relation given in
Figure 4
.

Remark 23
.

The non–homogeneous reduction
preserves the conclusion
of the nets,
hence it can be lifted to ordered nets –
as in remark
20
.

Remark 24
.

In the framework of
Multiplicative Linear Logic (
section 2
,
6(c)
),
non homogeneous cut elimination simulates
proof search in the sequent calculus:

\;\scriptstyle\maltese

\Gamma,A\invamp B

\;\scriptstyle\maltese

A^{\bot},A

\;\scriptstyle\maltese

B^{\bot},B

\;\scriptstyle\otimes

A^{\bot}\otimes B^{\bot},A,B

\;\scriptstyle\invamp

A^{\bot}\otimes B^{\bot},A\invamp B

\;\scriptstyle\mathsf{cut}

\Gamma,A\invamp B

\rightarrow^{*}

\;\scriptstyle\maltese

\Gamma,A,B

\;\scriptstyle\invamp

\Gamma,A\invamp B

\;\scriptstyle\maltese

\Gamma,A\otimes B

\;\scriptstyle\maltese

A^{\bot},A

\;\scriptstyle\maltese

B^{\bot},B

\;\scriptstyle\otimes

A^{\bot},B^{\bot},A\otimes B

\;\scriptstyle\invamp

A^{\bot}\invamp B^{\bot},A\otimes B

\;\scriptstyle\mathsf{cut}

\Gamma,A\otimes B

\rightarrow^{*}

\;\scriptstyle\maltese

\Gamma_{1},A

\;\scriptstyle\maltese

\Gamma_{2},B

\;\scriptstyle\otimes

\Gamma,A\otimes B

This also illustrates the non determinism
of the
(\maltese/\invamp)
reduction step
which corresponds to proof search
on a formula of the form
A\otimes B
:
going from bottom to top
the

\otimes
–introduction rule splits the context
\Gamma
,
which is a non deterministic process.
A consequence of non determinism
is the loss of confluence for cut elimination (but not of strong normalisation,
30
);
since splitting the context is irreversible,
a net can have different normal forms,
like the second net
of figure
5(b)
(from left to right)
which coincides with the second net of figure
5(c)
:
this same net reduces, following the two figures,
to two different normal forms.

Remark 25
.

A cyclic cut is a glueing cut.
Indeed,
given a cyclic cut link
\mathopen{\langle}p,q\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}
in a net,
because
p
and
q
belong to the target of a same link
e

and the only links which may have several targets are daimon links
it follows that
e
is a daimon link.

Remark 26
.

The side condition
of
Figure 3

entails that a cyclic cut is not reducible:
for example
the net
\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p,q\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p,q\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

is a net in normal form.

Remark 27
.

A cut link
which is not reducible
is either a clashing cut
or a cyclic glueing cut.
Notice, however,
that while clashing cuts
never disappear during cut elimination,
cyclic cuts may disappear (see
10(b)
).

Remark 28
.

In the standard framework of
\mathsf{MLL}
proof structures
the cut elimination
of an axiom against a cut
is defined as the identification of the two extreme positions,
therefore eliminating such a cut may create
loops
(
section 1
).
To avoid loops from occurring during cut elimination
an ad hoc condition is usually added (see for example
[
13
]
).
In our framework,
this condition is
the rather natural side condition of
Figure 3
.

Remark 29
.

The rewriting rule, denoted

Given two binary relations

.

2
Multiplicative Linear Logic and proof nets

We define the
well–known notion of proof net
[
7
]

in our setting:
in the presence of the generalised axiom
(\maltese)
,
proof nets are similar to the
paraproof nets
of Curien
[
4
]

(which come from Girard Ludics
[
10
]
).
We then formulate the Danos–Regnier criterion
[
5
]
:
testing the acyclicity and connectedness
of (several) graphs allows to determine
whether a net is a (para)proof net or not
[
4
]
.

We fix a countable set

\mathsf{Var}

of
propositional variables
.
The set
\mathsf{Var}
comes with an (explicit) involution
(\cdot)^{\bot}
;
for each atomic variable

X
there exists its
dual
atomic variable

X^{\bot}
in
\mathsf{Var}
.
The set
\mathsf{Form}

of
formulas
of multiplicative linear logic
is defined by
the grammar in
6(a)
.
The involution
(\cdot)^{\bot}
is lifted
from
\mathsf{Var}
to
\mathsf{Form}

as in
6(b)
.
The set
\mathsf{Hseq}

of
hypersequents
is defined
by the grammar in
6(a)
,
a
sequent
is an hypersequent
without the
parallel
‘
\parallel
’
constructor.
The introduction of hypersequents is naturally suggested by the
constructions on types (
section 3
):
indeed
as the interpretation of the
\invamp
–connective
is based on the interpretation
of the “
,
”–connective,
the interpretation of the
\otimes
–connective
relies on that of the “
\parallel
”–connective
(
49
and
58
).
Technically hypersequents are necessary in
our
proof of the completeness theorem (
Theorem 88
).

A
proof
of
\mathsf{MLL}

(resp.
\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}
)
is a tree constructed
using the rules

(\mathsf{ax},\invamp,\otimes,\mathsf{cut},\mathsf{ex})

(resp.
(\maltese,\invamp,\otimes,\mathsf{cut},\mathsf{ex})
)
of
6(c)
.

Definition 31
.

A net
S

represents
6
6

6

In the standard Linear Logic terminology
\pi

is a sequentialisation of the proof net
S
.

a proof
\pi
of
\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}
,
denoted
\pi\equiv_{\mathcal{R}}S

or
S\equiv_{\mathcal{R}}\pi
,
whenever the relation
defined in
Figure 8
holds.
A net represents a proof of
\mathsf{MLL}

whenever it represents a proof of
\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}

where every sequent conclusion of a
(\maltese)
–rule
has shape
A,A^{\bot}
for
A\in\mathsf{Form}
.
A representation of a proof
\pi

is a net
S
which represents
\pi
.
A
proof net
of
\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}
(resp.
\mathsf{MLL}
)
is a net which represents
a proof of
\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}
(resp.
\mathsf{MLL}
):
we say that
S
is
correct
.
A net
S
is
correctly typeable
7
7

7

Notice
that with the expressions “correctly typeable” we mean here
that the net is both correct (it represents a proof)
and that we can label
its conclusions with the formulas of
\Gamma
.

by a sequent
\Gamma

whenever it represents a proof of
\Gamma

in
\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}
.

Notation 32
.

Let
\mathfrak{P}

denote
\mathsf{MLL}
or
\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}
and
let
S
be a net.
We write
S\vdash_{\mathfrak{P}}\Gamma

whenever there exists a proof

\pi
in
\mathfrak{P}

such that

S
is the representation of
\pi
.
Furthermore
we denote
\lBrace\Gamma:\mathfrak{P}\rBrace

the set of all the nets
S

such that
S\vdash_{\mathfrak{P}}\Gamma
.

A
substitution
is a map

\theta:\mathsf{Var}\rightarrow\mathsf{Form}

such that
\theta(X^{\bot})=\theta(X)^{\bot}
for each
X\in\mathsf{Var}
.
A substitution can be lifted
to formulas and hypersequents by induction:

\theta(A\otimes B)=\theta(A)\otimes\theta(B)
;

\theta(A\invamp B)=\theta(A)\invamp\theta(B)
;

\theta(A\parallel B)=\theta(A)\parallel\theta(B)
;

\theta(A,B)=\theta(A),\theta(B)
.
Given two hypersequents,
we denote
\Delta\leq\Gamma

whenever there exists a substitution
\theta

such that
\theta\Delta=\Gamma
.

Proposition 33
.

Let
\Gamma
and
\Delta
be two sequents
and suppose
\Delta\leq\Gamma
.
For any net
S
:
(1) if
S\vdash_{\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}}\Delta
then
S\vdash_{\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}}\Gamma

and
(2)
if
S\vdash_{\mathsf{MLL}}\Delta
then
S\vdash_{\mathsf{MLL}}\Gamma
.

Definition 34
.

The
switching
rewriting
is defined on unordered nets
as the contextual closure of
the rules in
Figure 7
.
A
switching
of a net
S

is a normal form of
S
for the switching rewriting:
we often denote it
\sigma S
.

Remark 35
.

The switching rewriting strongly normalizes
since every step reduces the number of links of the net.
The rewriting is also non-deterministic
and non-confluent,
every normal form is a par–free net.
The switching rewriting can be lifted to (ordered) nets;
with the notations of
Figure 7

whenever
an unordered net
\left|{S}\right|
with
n
conclusions
is such that
\left|{S}\right|\rightarrow_{\invamp}^{l}\left|{S^{\prime}}\right|

we define
(\left|{S}\right|,\mathbf{a})\rightarrow_{\invamp}^{l}(\left|{S^{\prime}}\right|,\mathbf{a}^{\prime})

where

\mathbf{a}^{\prime}(i)=\mathbf{a}(i)
for each
1\leq i\leq n

and
\mathbf{a}^{\prime}(n+1)=p_{2}

i.e. the new conclusion is made last conclusion
(similarly we can define it for the case
\rightarrow_{\invamp}^{r}
).

Definition 36
.

The
undirected multigraph
8
8

8

Recall that a multigraph is a graph where
two vertices may be connected by several edges (not to be confused with the notion of hypergraph
of
1
).
The function
\mathsf{brd}
maps each edge to its endpoints.
induced by
two partitions

P
and
Q
of a set
X

is

(V,E,\mathsf{brd})
denoted
\mathsf{G}(P,Q)
where:
(1)

V=\{1\}\times P\cup\{2\}\times Q

the vertices are the classes of
P
and
Q

(as a disjoint union);
(2)

E=X
;
(3)
For any edge
x
in
X
;

\mathsf{brd}(x)=\{(1,P_{x}),(2,Q_{x})\}

where
P_{x}\in P
is such that
x\in P_{x}

and
Q_{x}\in Q
is such
x\in Q_{x}
.

Two partitions
P

and
Q
of a set
X

are
orthogonal

if the multigraph
\mathsf{G}(P,Q)
is
acyclic and connected.

Definition 37
.

In a net
S

denote
p\geq_{S}q

the relation which holds
whenever there exists a link
e

such that
p\in\mathrm{s}(e)

and
q\in\mathrm{t}(e)
.
Denote
\geq^{*}_{S}
its reflexive and transitive closure;
a position
p
is

above
a position
q

whenever
p\geq^{*}_{S}q
.
Given a position
q

we denote
q\uparrow^{i}S

the set of
initial
positions which are above
q
in
S
.

Remark 38
.

Given a
cut–free
net
S
with conclusions
p_{1},\dots,p_{n}

the sets
p_{1}\uparrow^{i}S,\dots,p_{n}\uparrow^{i}S

form a partition of the initial positions of
S
.
We denote this partition
\uparrow^{i}S
.

Notation 39
.

Let
S
be a net
and let

\{d_{1},\dots,d_{n}\}
be the set of daimon links of
S
.
The partition

\{\mathrm{t}(d_{1}),\dots,\mathrm{t}(d_{n})\}

on the set of initial positions of
S

is denoted by
\mathsf{P}_{\maltese}(S)
.

Reformulated in the context
of hypergraphs we get the following
theorem from
[
5
]
.

Theorem 40

(​​
[
4
]
,
[
5
]
)
.

Given a cut–free net
S
,
the following assertions are equivalent:

1.

S
is a proof net of
\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}
;

2.

For every switching
\sigma S
of
S
,
the partitions

\mathsf{P}_{\maltese}(S)
and
\uparrow^{i}\sigma S

of the set of initial positions of
S

are orthogonal;

3.

Every switching
\sigma S
of
S

is acyclic and connected
9
9

9

We refer to the graph naturally induced
by the net
\sigma S
.

.

3
Interaction of nets, orthogonality, and types

We define how nets can
interact

and if the interaction of two nets leads to the

\maltese
-link with no outputs (
\maltese_{0}
) we say they are
orthogonal
.
This recalls classical realisability proposed
by J.-L. Krivine
[
12
]
,
where (the closure by antireduction of) the set

\{\maltese_{0}\}
will play the role of the
pole
.
Notice, however, that our setting is fully symmetrical:
both the elements of
truth values and
falsity values
are nets.

The notion of ordered hypergraph
and
arrangement
introduced in
section 1

will now explicitly come into play
as it is necessary for defining the interactions of nets (see
9(a)
).
We will denote by
\#S

the number of outputs of a net
S
.
Given a partial function
f:\mathbb{N}\rightarrow E

with a finite domain of cardinality
n

and ordered as
i_{1}<i_{2}<\dots<i_{n}
,
the
collapse
of
f
, denoted
f{\mkern-3.0mu\downarrow}
,
is
the total function
with domain
[1;n]

such that
f{\mkern-3.0mu\downarrow}(m)=f(i_{m})

for any integer
1\leq m\leq n
.

Definition 41
.

Let
S=(\left|{S}\right|,\mathbf{a}(S))
and
T=(\left|{T}\right|,\mathbf{a}(T))

be two nets
and
k=min(\#S,\#T)
,
we define their
interaction

S::T=(\left|{S::T}\right|,\mathbf{a}(S::T))
as:

\left|{S::T}\right|\;\triangleq\;\left|{S}\right|+\left|{T}\right|+\sum_{1\leq i\leq min(\#S,\#T)}\mathopen{\langle}S(i),T(i)\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}\qquad\mathbf{a}(S::T)\;\triangleq\;\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}\emptyset&\text{when }\#S=\#T\\
\mathbf{a}(S)\upharpoonright_{[k+1;\#S]}{\mkern-3.0mu\downarrow}&\text{when }\#S>\#T\\
\mathbf{a}(T)\upharpoonright_{[k+1;\#T]}{\mkern-3.0mu\downarrow}&\text{when }\#S<\#T\end{array}\right.

.

(b)

The cut elimination procedure applied to
S^{\prime}::T^{\prime}
leads to
\maltese_{0}
,
showing that
S^{\prime}\perp T^{\prime}
. In this figure
S^{\prime}=\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}q\mathclose{\rangle}
and
T^{\prime}=\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{1}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{2}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},p_{2}\vartriangleright_{\otimes}p\mathclose{\rangle}
.

Figure 9
:
The interaction of two nets (
41
)
and two orthogonal nets (
42
).

Definition 42
.

Two nets
S_{1}
and
S_{2}

are
orthogonal

if

S_{1}::S_{2}\rightarrow^{*}\maltese_{0}
10
10

10

Note that we require the
existence

of such a reduction, not all reductions
need to behave this way.
: when this holds we write

S_{1}\perp S_{2}
. For a net
S
and a set of nets
\Lambda
, if for every
\lambda\in\Lambda
we have
S\perp\lambda
we write
S\perp\Lambda
.

Remark 43
.

Since cut links are asymmetric,
namely
\mathopen{\langle}p,q\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

and
\mathopen{\langle}q,p\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}
are distinct nets,
the interactions

S::T
and
T::S

are not the same net.
However, this has no consequence on cut elimination
because the reduction steps
do not depend on the order
of the inputs of a cut link.
Thus

S::T
reduces to
\maltese_{0}

if and only if
T::S
does,
and as expected the relation of orthogonality is
symmetric.

Definition 44
.

Given a set
A
of multiplicative nets, we define
the
orthogonal
of

A
as

A^{\bot}=\{P\mid\forall R\in A,P\perp R\}
.
A
type

\mathbf{A}
is a set of multiplicative nets
such that
\mathbf{A}^{\bot\bot}=\mathbf{A}
.
11
11

11

Equivalently,
a type is a set
\mathbf{A}
such that
\mathbf{A}=B^{\bot}
for some set
B
,
see, for instance,
[
11
]
.

Remark 45
.

Since cut elimination
preserves the conclusions of a net
and
\maltese_{0}
has no output,
two orthogonal nets have
the same number of conclusions.
Thus,
for every type
\mathbf{A}
,
for every
R\in\mathbf{A}
and
for every
S\in\mathbf{A}^{\bot}
,
the nets
R
and
S
have the same number of conclusions: we
denote by
\#\mathbf{A}
the number of conclusions of the nets in
\mathbf{A}
.
Obviously

\#\mathbf{A}=\#\mathbf{A}^{\bot}
.

Remark 46
.

Clash cuts are preserved during cut elimination,
thus a net containing such a cut cannot reduce to
\maltese_{0}
.
Hence, there cannot be
two nets
S
and
S^{\prime}
respectively in

\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{A}^{\bot}

such that their
i
th conclusions
S(i)

and
S^{\prime}(i)

are both outputs of a
\invamp
–link (or
\otimes
–link):
their interaction
S::S^{\prime}

contains a clash cut
and thus the nets cannot be orthogonal.

Remark 47
.

A net
S
which is orthogonal to
the daimon link with a single output (i.e.
\maltese_{1}
)
has a single conclusion
which can be
the output of a daimon link,
a tensor link or a par link.
For instance the three cut–free nets

\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p\mathclose{\rangle}
,

\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{1}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{2}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},p_{2}\vartriangleright_{\otimes}p\mathclose{\rangle}
and

\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{1},p_{2}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},p_{2}\vartriangleright_{\invamp}p\mathclose{\rangle}

are all orthogonal to
\maltese_{1}

(one case is proved in
9(b)
).

The following proposition
is a key step for proving propositions

51
and
54
.

Proposition 48
.

Given three net
S
and
T
and
R

such that
\#S\geq\#T+\#R
:
the interaction
S::(T\parallel R)

is equal to
(S::T)::R
.

In the following definition
49

the side condition
\#S\geq\#\mathbf{A}

ensures that
whenever a net
S
in
A\Yright B

interacts with a net of
T\in\mathbf{A}^{\bot}

the remaining conclusions of
S::T

are conclusions of
S
,
this will allow to activate
48
.

Definition 49
.

Given two sets of nets
\mathbf{A}

and
\mathbf{B}

their
functional composition

denoted
\mathbf{A}\Yright\mathbf{B}
,
and their
parallel composition

denoted
\mathbf{A}\parallel\mathbf{B}

are defined as follows:

\mathbf{A}\Yright\mathbf{B}\;\triangleq\;\{S\mid\mbox{for any }T\in\mathbf{A}^{\bot},S::T\in\mathbf{B}\mbox{ and }\#S\geq\#\mathbf{A}\}

\mathbf{A}\parallel\mathbf{B}\;\triangleq\;\{S\parallel T\mid S\in\mathbf{A},T\in\mathbf{B}\}^{{}^{\bot\bot}}

Remark 50

(Density of the parallel composition)
.

For any two types
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
we have

(\mathbf{A}\parallel^{-}\mathbf{B})^{\bot}=(\mathbf{A}\parallel\mathbf{B})^{\bot}
, where

\mathbf{A}\parallel^{-}\mathbf{B}=\{S\parallel T\mid S\in\mathbf{A},T\in\mathbf{B}\}
.

Proposition 51

(Duality)
.

Given two types

\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
:

(\mathbf{A}\parallel\mathbf{B})^{\bot}=\mathbf{A}^{\bot}\Yright\mathbf{B}^{\bot}

and

(\mathbf{A}\Yright\mathbf{B})^{\bot}=\mathbf{A}^{\bot}\parallel\mathbf{B}^{\bot}
.

Remark 52
.

The duality of the constructions (
51
)
ensures that the set of types
is closed under the
\parallel

and
\Yright
operations.
Moreover, the intersection of two types
is still a type.
This is not the case for the union
which needs to be closed under bi–orthogonal.

Remark 53
.

For two types
\mathbf{A}

and
\mathbf{B}

the unordered nets of
\mathbf{A}\parallel\mathbf{B}

and of
\mathbf{B}\parallel\mathbf{A}

are the same,
so as the unordered nets of

\mathbf{A}\Yright\mathbf{B}

and
\mathbf{B}\Yright\mathbf{A}
.

Proposition 54
.

Given
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}
and
\mathbf{C}

three types;

(\mathbf{A}\Yright\mathbf{B})\Yright\mathbf{C}=\mathbf{A}\Yright(\mathbf{B}\Yright\mathbf{C})

and

(\mathbf{A}\parallel\mathbf{B})\parallel\mathbf{C}=\mathbf{A}\parallel(\mathbf{B}\parallel\mathbf{C})
.

Definition 55
.

Given
\mathbf{A}

and
\mathbf{B}

two types with one conclusion,
we define
their
tensor product
(denoted
\otimes
)
and their
compositional product
(denoted
\invamp
):

\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B}\,\triangleq\,\{S+\mathopen{\langle}S(1),S(2)\vartriangleright_{\otimes}p\mathclose{\rangle}\mid S\in\mathbf{A}\parallel\mathbf{B}\}^{{}^{\bot\bot}}\qquad\mathbf{A}\invamp\mathbf{B}\,\triangleq\,\{S+\mathopen{\langle}S(1),S(2)\vartriangleright_{\invamp}p\mathclose{\rangle}\mid S\in\mathbf{A}\Yright\mathbf{B}\}^{{}^{\bot\bot}}

where
p
denotes a fresh position.

Proposition 56

(Duality)
.

Given
\mathbf{A}

and
\mathbf{B}
two types
with one conclusion,

(\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B})^{\bot}=\mathbf{A}^{\bot}\invamp\mathbf{B}^{\bot}

and

(\mathbf{A}\invamp\mathbf{B})^{\bot}=\mathbf{A}^{\bot}\otimes\mathbf{B}^{\bot}
.

a
b
c
d
\scriptstyle\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\otimes
\scriptstyle\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\maltese

\rightarrow

a1

\rightarrow

a1

\scriptstyle\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\maltese
\scriptstyle\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\mathsf{cut}

(a)

The elimination of a
(\maltese/\otimes)
cut
preserves cycles in a net.

a

\scriptstyle\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\maltese

\rightarrow

a1

\rightarrow

c
\scriptstyle\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\maltese
\scriptstyle\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\maltese

\rightarrow

\maltese_{0}

(b)

The elimination of a
(\maltese/\invamp)
cut

may
break a cycle in a net.

a

a1

The elimination of a

:

4
Realisability Model: Adequacy

We introduce our realisability
model on untyped nets
and prove it is adequate.
We identify
a sufficient property
of interpretation bases to prove adequacy (Theorem
64
):
for any basis

\mathcal{B}
satisfying the property, a net
S
representing an
\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}
proof of a sequent
\Gamma

is a realiser of
\Gamma

i.e. it belongs to
\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}
.
This adequacy result immediately applies to
\mathsf{MLL}
,
since a net representing a proof of
\mathsf{MLL}

represents, in particular, a proof of
\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}
.

We start by giving
an interpretation of formulas and hypersequents of multiplicative linear logic.
We provide an interpretation of hypersequents instead of sequents
as it turns out
that handling hypersequents is more convenient
and proving a result on hypersequents proves it on sequents too.
However, do keep in mind
that the proof trees we defined using
6(c)

are constructed with sequents.

Definition 57
.

An
interpretation basis

\mathcal{B}

is a function
that associates with each atomic proposition

X
a type

\llbracket X\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}
, the
interpretation
of
X
,
such that:

•

Each net in
\llbracket X\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}
has one conclusion.

•

For any atomic proposition
X
,
we have
\llbracket X^{\bot}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}\subseteq\llbracket X\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}
.

Definition 58
.

Given an interpretation basis
\mathcal{B}
, the
interpretation
of
\mathsf{MLL}
formulas and of hypersequents of
\mathsf{MLL}
is defined
by induction:

\llbracket A\otimes B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

\,\triangleq\,

\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}\otimes\llbracket B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}
.

\llbracket A\invamp B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

\,\triangleq\,

\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}\invamp\llbracket B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}
.

\llbracket\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

\,\triangleq\,

\llbracket\mathcal{H}_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}\Yright\llbracket\mathcal{H}_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}
.

\llbracket\mathcal{H}_{1}\parallel\mathcal{H}_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

\,\triangleq\,

\llbracket\mathcal{H}_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}\parallel\llbracket\mathcal{H}_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}
.

Remark 59
.

Using duality of types (Proposition
56
) and the properties of orthogonality one proves that for an interpretation basis
\mathcal{B}
and an
\mathsf{MLL}
formula
A
we have

\llbracket A^{\bot}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}\subseteq\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}
.

Definition 60
.

A multiplicative net
realises

– with respect to an interpretation basis
\mathcal{B}
–
an hypersequent
\mathcal{H}

of
\mathsf{MLL}
formulas
whenever it belongs to

\llbracket\mathcal{H}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}
.

Notation 61
.

For a hypersequent
\mathcal{H}
, we will often write
S\Vdash_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{H}
instead of
S\in\llbracket\mathcal{H}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}
, and sometimes
S\Vdash\mathcal{H}
or
S\in\llbracket\mathcal{H}\rrbracket

when there is no ambiguity on the basis
\mathcal{B}
.

From the point of view of cut elimination,
a daimon link with
n
outputs
may be thought as the approximation
of a proof net with
n
outputs.
More precisely,
by iterating the process we have seen in
24
,
every cut–free proof
\pi
of a formula
C

can be obtained by applying the cut elimination
procedure to the daimon link
\maltese_{1}

(of conclusion
C
)
cut against the appropriate identities
of
C,C^{\bot}

(this generalises to a sequent
\Gamma
and
\maltese_{n}
).
Furthermore
daimon links and proof nets (with the same number of conclusions)
are interchangeable
with respect to geometrical correctness (
Table 1
):
in a correct (resp. incorrect) net
S
,
substituting a daimon link with
n
outputs
by
a proof net with
n
outputs
produces a correct (resp. incorrect) net.
However, proof nets and daimons (with the same number of conclusions)
differ
on realisability:
for instance a proof net ending with a tensor link
can never realise a formula of the form
A\invamp B

whereas a daimon link can (
Theorem 64
).
We will thus say that
a daimon link “approximates” a sequent:
this suggests Definition
62
.

Definition 62
.

A type
\mathbf{A}
is approximable
if and only if

\maltese_{1}\in\mathbf{A}
.
A basis
\mathcal{B}

is

approximable

if for each
X\in\mathsf{Var}
,
the type

\llbracket X\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}
is approximable.

Remark 63
.

Because inclusion
is preserved by bi–orthogonal closure,
a type
\mathbf{A}

is approximable
if and only if

\{\maltese_{1}\}^{{}^{\bot\bot}}\subseteq\mathbf{A}

which is equivalent
to the inclusion

\mathbf{A}^{\bot}\subseteq\{\maltese_{1}\}^{\bot}
.

Theorem 64

(Adequacy)
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be an approximable basis.
For any net
S
and sequent
\Gamma

S\vdash_{\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}}\Gamma\Rightarrow S\Vdash_{\mathcal{B}}\Gamma
.

Proof.

The technique is standard in the works on realisability (see
[
12
]
or
[
14
]
):
one proceeds by induction on the size of a proof
\pi
represented by
S
.
For the base case
one must show that
\maltese_{n}

realises any sequent
\Gamma
with
n
formulas.
To do so one first checks that, for any formula
A
,
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}
is approximable (
\maltese_{1}\in\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}
).
∎

Remark 65
.

An approximable basis yields adequacy, in particular, for
\mathsf{MLL}
. Notice, however, that there exist bases
yielding an interpretation that is adequate for
\mathsf{MLL}
but not for
\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}
.

5
Testability and tests

The partitions involved
in the Danos Regnier criterion (
Theorem 76
)
and their orthogonality with the daimons of a net
can be translated as
tests
;
so that for a formula
A
, a net
S
testable by
A
(definition
66
below)
and orthogonal to

\mathsf{tests}({A})

is
a correct net (
Theorem 76
).
We will show that these tests are proofs of
\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}
(
Theorem 77
).
This means that for realisers in an approximable basis,
testability (
66
)
and correct typeability (Definition
31
) coincide:
this is
82
.

Definition 66

((Atomic) testable cut–free nets)
.

A
formula labelling

of a cut–free net
S

is a function
\tau:V_{S}\rightarrow\mathsf{Form}
such that:

•

(Par)

When
\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},p_{2}\vartriangleright_{\invamp}p\mathclose{\rangle}
occurs in
S
:
if
\tau(p_{1})=A
and
\tau(p_{2})=B

then
\tau(p)=A\invamp B
.

•

(Tens)

When
\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},p_{2}\vartriangleright_{\otimes}p\mathclose{\rangle}
occurs in
S
:
if
\tau(p_{1})=A
and
\tau(p_{2})=B

then
\tau(p)=A\otimes B
.

A formula labelling of a cut–free net
S
is
atomic

when
for each daimon link
\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{1},\dots,p_{n}\mathclose{\rangle}

in
S
the formula
\tau(p_{i})
is a propositional variable.

A cut–free net
S
with
n
conclusions
is

testable
(resp.
atomic testable
)
by a sequent

\Gamma=A_{1},\dots,A_{n}
, which we denote
S\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq}\Gamma

(resp.
S\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.50003pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}\Gamma
),
if there exists a formula (resp. an atomic formula) labelling
\tau

of
S
such that
\tau(S(i))=A_{i}

for each
1\leq i\leq n
.

Remark 67
.

S\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq}\Gamma
iff

S\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.50003pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}\Delta
and
\Gamma=\theta\Delta

for some substitution
\theta
and sequent
\Delta
.

Remark 68
.

S\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.50003pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}\Gamma

iff

S
without its
\maltese
–links
is the syntactic forest of
(the formulas of)
\Gamma
.

Remark 69
.

A cut–free proof net
S\vdash_{\mathsf{MLL}}^{\maltese}\Gamma

is in particular testable by that sequent
i.e.

S\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq}\Gamma
.
However, a net
S\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq}\Gamma

which is testable by
\Gamma

may not be a proof net
because it could contain cycles or disconnections:
the testability condition only provides information
on the multiplicative links
constituting the net
S
.
When is
S
atomic testable by
A
,
orthogonality with the tests of
A

coincides with correctness (
75
).

Remark 70
.

Let
S\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.50003pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}A_{1},\dots,A_{n}
be a cut–free net.
For any nets
T_{1},\dots,T_{n}
cut–free and
atomically testable respectively by

{A_{1}}^{\bot},\dots,{A_{n}}^{\bot}

denoting
S_{0}

the normal form of
S::T_{1}\parallel\dots\parallel T_{n}
,

S_{0}
is obtained by homogeneous cut–elimination,
and we have
(1)
S_{0}
equals
\maltese_{0}

(2)
S_{0}
is equal to
the sum of
k\geq 2
daimon without conclusions (
S_{0}=\sum_{1\leq i\leq k}\maltese_{0}
)
or (3)

S_{0}
contains a cyclic cut
(
S_{0}=R+\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\vec{q},a,\vec{r},b,\vec{p}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}a,b\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}
).

Remark 71
.

Given a net
S=(\left|{S}\right|,\mathbf{a}(S))

we denote
S^{\maltese}=(\left|{S^{\maltese}}\right|,\mathbf{a}(S^{\maltese}))

the net such that

\left|{S^{\maltese}}\right|
is the hypergraph consisting
of the daimon links occurring in
S
.
The arrangement
\mathbf{a}(S^{\maltese})

is induced by
\mathbf{a}(S)

because above every conclusion of
S

there is binary tree:
each initial position
p

can be associated
with a sequence
\xi=\mathsf{adr}(p)
of
\{l,r\}^{*}

and an integer
i=\mathsf{root}(p)

so that going up from
S(i)
following the left/right instruction
of
\xi

one reaches the initial position
p
.
The initial positions of
S

are then ordered by the lexicographical order
of
(\mathsf{root}(p),\mathsf{adr}(p))

fixing
l\leq r
.

Notation 72
.

Given a net
S
with
n
initial positions,
and
P=\{C_{1},\dots,C_{k}\}
a partition of the initial positions of
S

we denote by
\mathbf{Nat}_{S}(P)

the partition

\{\mathbf{a}(S^{\maltese})^{-1}(C_{1}),\dots,\mathbf{a}(S^{\maltese})^{-1}(C_{k})\}

of
\{1,\dots,n\}
.
We might abusively write
\mathbf{Nat}(P)

for
\mathbf{Nat}_{S}(P)
.

Proposition 73
.

Let
A
be a formula,
given two
cut free nets

S\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.62222pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}A

and
T\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.62222pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}A^{\bot}

the assertions are equivalent:

1.

The nets
S
and
T
are orthogonal.

2.

The nets
S^{\maltese}
and
T^{\maltese}
are orthogonal.

3.

The partition

\mathbf{Nat}_{S}(\mathsf{P}_{\maltese}(S))
and
\mathbf{Nat}_{T}(\mathsf{P}_{\maltese}(T))
are orthogonal.

Definition 74
.

A cut-free net
T

is a
test
of a formula
A
if

T\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.50003pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}A^{\bot}

and there exists a net
S\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.50003pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}A

and a switching
\sigma S

such that
\mathbf{Nat}_{T}(\mathsf{P}_{\maltese}(T))=\mathbf{Nat}_{S}(\uparrow^{i}\sigma S)
.
We denote by

\mathsf{tests}({A})

the
set
\{S\mid S\mbox{ is a test of }A\}
.

Proposition 75
.

For
S
cut–free,
S\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.62222pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}A
, we have:

S\vdash_{\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}}A\Leftrightarrow S\perp\mathsf{tests}({A}).

A net
S
with
n

conclusion can always be transformed
in a net with
1
conclusion by
putting a bunch of par–links below its conclusions;
this allows to generalise the previous proposition.

Theorem 76

(Danos–Regnier Tests)
.

Given a cut–free net
S\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.62222pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}A_{1},\dots,A_{n}
;

S\vdash_{\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}}A_{1},\dots,A_{n}

if and only if

S
is orthogonal to

\mathsf{tests}({A_{1}})\parallel\dots\parallel\mathsf{tests}({A_{n}})
.

Theorem 77
.

Any test
T
of a formula
A

is correctly typeable by
A^{\bot}
,

T\vdash_{\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}}A^{\bot}
.

Proof.

Consider a test
T
of
A

then by
Theorem 76

any net
S\vdash_{\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}}A

is orthogonal to
T
.
By the counter–proof criterion
[
4
]

a net
N\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.50003pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}A^{\bot}
orthogonal to each proof of
A

is a proof; therefore it follows that

T
is a proof of
A^{\bot}
.
∎

Remark 78
.

Theorem 76
is a refinement of the counter–proof criterion
of P.L. Curien
[
4
]
:
if
S\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.50003pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}A
and
S\perp\mathsf{tests}({A})

then
S\vdash_{\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}}A

– and every element
of

\mathsf{tests}({A})
are proofs of
A^{\bot}
(
Theorem 77
),
but the converse does not hold.

From
Theorem 76
and
Theorem 77

one obtains an “interactive” criterion
for the nets of multiplicative linear logic (
\mathsf{MLL}
).
One takes a net of
S
of
\mathsf{MLL}

(i.e. a net with binary daimons)
and confronts it with the tests of
the according formulas (
74
).
A straightforward consequence of the
Theorem 76

is the reformulation of Béchet’s theorem in our framework.

Corollary 79
.

Let
S\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.62222pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}A_{1},\dots,A_{n}
be a cut–free net.
If
S
is not correct
then there exists
nets
T_{1}\in\mathsf{tests}({A_{1}}),\dots,T_{n}\in\mathsf{tests}({A_{n}})

such that
the normal form of
S::T_{1}\parallel\dots\parallel T_{n}

is not correct:
we are in case (2)
or (3) of
70
.

Remark 80
.

The
79

obviously applies to
\mathsf{MLL}
nets,
the main difference with Béchet’s original
result
is that his opponents are
\mathsf{MLL}
proof nets
(in our framework they are
\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}
proof nets).
However it is not difficult to adapt our techniques to obtain Béchet’s
result.

Remark 81
.

Consider an approximable basis
\mathcal{B}

and a sequent
\Gamma=A_{1},\dots,A_{n}

we have

\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}=\smash{(\llbracket A_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}\parallel\dots\parallel\llbracket A_{n}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot})^{\bot}}
.
By
Theorem 64
,
for any
A_{i}^{\bot}
we have
\smash{\lBrace A_{i}^{\bot}:\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}\rBrace\subseteq\llbracket A_{i}^{\bot}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}}

while
\mathsf{tests}({A_{i}})\subseteq\lBrace A_{i}^{\bot}:\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}\rBrace
(
Theorem 77
)
thus
\mathsf{tests}({A_{i}})\subseteq\llbracket A_{i}^{\bot}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}\subseteq\llbracket A_{i}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}

(
59
).
Because the
\parallel
–construction preserves inclusions
and orthogonality inverts inclusions
we derive that

\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}\subseteq\smash{(\mathsf{tests}({A_{1}})\parallel\dots\parallel\mathsf{tests}({A_{n}}))^{\bot}}
.

)
means that
for realisers in an approximable basis, testability and (correct) typeability
collapse.

Remark 86
.

The non homogeneous cut elimination
allows to distinguish the types

\llbracket X,X^{\bot}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

and
\llbracket X,Y\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

for a well chosen basis:
for instance for the basis, that we will denote
{\mathcal{B}}\langle\invamp\rangle
,
which maps positive propositional variables
to
\{\maltese_{\invamp}\}^{\bot}

and negative propositional variables
to
\{\maltese_{\invamp}\}^{{}^{\bot\bot}}
,
where
\maltese_{\invamp}
denotes the geometrically incorrect net

\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}a\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}b\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}a,b\vartriangleright_{\invamp}c\mathclose{\rangle}
.

In that case,
(1) because
\maltese_{2}
is not orthogonal to

\maltese_{\invamp}\parallel\maltese_{\invamp}
(
Figure 11
)
it follows that
\maltese_{2}\notin\llbracket X,X\rrbracket_{{\mathcal{B}}\langle\invamp\rangle}

and more generally
\maltese_{2}\notin\llbracket X,Y\rrbracket_{{\mathcal{B}}\langle\invamp\rangle}
;
(2) by the property expressed in
90
(and illustrated in
Figure 12
),

\maltese_{2}\in\llbracket X,X^{\bot}\rrbracket_{{\mathcal{B}}\langle\invamp\rangle}
;
(3) point (1) above is not in contradiction with the theorem of adequacy
(
Theorem 64
) because,
even though

\maltese_{2}\vdash_{\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}}X,Y
, the basis
{\mathcal{B}}\langle\invamp\rangle
is not approximable.

.

Remark 89
.

A result of adequacy for
\mathsf{MLL}
can also be stated:
given an interpretation basis
\mathcal{B}
(not necessarily approximable)
such that for each propositional variable
X

we have
\llbracket X^{\bot}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}=\llbracket X\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}
,
for any net
S
,
if
S\vdash_{\mathsf{MLL}}\Gamma
then
S\in\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}
.

Remark 90
.

The completeness result for
\mathsf{MLL}
(
Theorem 88
)
only identifies cut–free and
atomic
proofs
(i.e. where axioms introduce sequents of the form
X,X^{\bot}
).
This is because
for any atomic formulas
X
and
Y
,
and for any basis
\mathcal{B}
such that
\llbracket X^{\bot}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}=\llbracket X\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}
,

\maltese_{2}\in\llbracket X\invamp X^{\bot},Y\invamp Y^{\bot}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

while

X\invamp X^{\bot}
and
Y\invamp Y^{\bot}

are not dual formulas:
contrary to the atomic case
we cannot use
\maltese_{2}
to distinguish
\llbracket X\invamp X^{\bot},Y\invamp Y^{\bot}\rrbracket_{{\mathcal{B}}\langle\invamp\rangle}

from
\llbracket X\invamp X^{\bot},X^{\bot}\otimes X\rrbracket_{{\mathcal{B}}\langle\invamp\rangle}
.

The fact that
\maltese_{2}\in\llbracket X\invamp X^{\bot},Y\invamp Y^{\bot}\rrbracket_{{\mathcal{B}}\langle\invamp\rangle}

(and more generally for any basis
\mathcal{B}
such that
\llbracket X^{\bot}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}=\llbracket X\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}
)
is derived from the fact that,
for any integer
k

and for
any two orthogonal nets
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
with one conclusion,
the interaction
\maltese_{k+2}::(S_{1}\parallel S_{2})

has
at least one
reduction to
\maltese_{k}

by cut elimination (
Figure 12
).
We use this property for
k=2
and
k=4

to show that
\maltese_{2}\in\llbracket X\invamp X^{\bot},Y\invamp Y^{\bot}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}
.
More precisely, we prove that,

\maltese_{2}\perp\llbracket X\invamp X^{\bot}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}\parallel\llbracket Y\invamp Y^{\bot}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}
:
given
S,\overline{S}
and
R,\overline{R}

two pairs of orthogonal nets (with one conclusion),
when all nets
S,\overline{S},R,\overline{R}
have disjoint
sets of vertices, we can derive the following:

\begin{array}[]{r l r}&\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}a,b\mathclose{\rangle}::S+\overline{S}+\mathopen{\langle}S(1),\overline{S}(1)\vartriangleright_{\otimes}q\mathclose{\rangle}+R+\overline{R}+\mathopen{\langle}R(1),\overline{R}(1)\vartriangleright_{\otimes}r\mathclose{\rangle}\\
\rightarrow\cdot\rightarrow&\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}a_{1},a_{2},b_{1},b_{2}\mathclose{\rangle}::S+\overline{S}+R+\overline{R}\\
\rightarrow^{*}&\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}b_{1},b_{2}\mathclose{\rangle}::R+\overline{R}\\
\rightarrow^{*}&\maltese_{0}\end{array}

Figure 14
:

Relation of “dependency” between propositions
in this document (one can observe that this is an acyclic graph!).
The nodes of the graph are (some of) the propositions
