Appendix B
Complements to
section 1

We recall the notion of homomorphism and isomorphism between hypergraphs.

Definition 91

(Homomorphism and isomorphisms of hypergraphs)
.

An
homomorphism

between two (labelled) hypergraphs

\mathcal{H}_{1}=(V_{1},E_{1},\mathrm{s}_{1},\mathrm{t}_{1},\ell_{1})

and
\mathcal{H}_{2}=(V_{2},E_{2},\mathrm{s}_{2},\mathrm{t}_{2},\ell_{2})

is a pair of bijective maps
\langle f^{V},f^{E}\rangle
,
such that

•

(
commutation
)
For any edge
e_{1}
of
\mathcal{H}_{1}
,
\mathrm{s}_{2}(f^{E}(e_{1}))=f^{V}(\mathrm{s}_{1}(e_{1}))
,
i.e. the following diagram commutes:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{s}_{1}(e_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{s}_{2}(e_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{s}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{s}_{2}}

•

(
label-preserving
)
The function
f^{E}
preserves the labels
of the links,
i.e. for any link
e
in
\mathcal{H}_{1}
;

\ell(e)=\ell(f^{E}(e))

The homomorphism is an isomorphism if both
f^{V}
and
f^{E}
are bijective.

B.1
Rewriting properties of nets: Strong Normalisation

Proposition 92

(Strong normalisation
30
)
.

The cut elimination rewriting
\smash{\xrightarrow{}}

is strongly normalising.

Proof.

This is obtained by observing that an
appropriate measure on nets always decrease with cut elimination,
i.e. whenever
S\rightarrow S^{\prime}

then
m(S)>m(S^{\prime})
.
Such a measure
m
may be
the pairs
(\mathsf{connective}(S),\mathsf{cut}(S))

where
\mathsf{connective}(S)
is the number
of
\otimes
– and
\invamp
– links in
S
,
while
\mathsf{cut}(S)
is the number of cut links in
S
.
The order involved is then the lexicographical order.

Indeed we have in that case that
S\rightarrow S^{\prime}

implies
m(S)>m(S^{\prime})
:
the
(\otimes/\maltese)
,
(\invamp/\maltese)

and multiplicative steps
always decrease the number
\mathsf{connective}(S)
,
finally the glueing steps do not change
\mathsf{connective}(S)

however they make the numbe of cuts
\mathsf{cut}(S)
decrease.
∎

B.2
Rewriting properties of nets: unrelated cuts

Notation 93
.

Given two binary relations

R_{1}
and
R_{2}
on a set
X

we denote by
R_{1}\cdot R_{2}

the composition of the two relation,
i.e. for two
x,y\in X

xR_{1}\cdot R_{2}y

if and only if there exists
z

such that
xR_{1}z

and
zR_{2}y
.

Notation 94
.

Given a net
S
and cut link
c

we denote by
S\smash{\xrightarrow{c}}S^{\prime}
,
whenever
S\rightarrow S^{\prime}

by eliminating the cut
c
.
Note that whenever
S\rightarrow S^{\prime}

there exists a cut
c

such that
S\rightarrow cS^{\prime}
,
namely the unique cut which belong to
S

but not
S^{\prime}
.

Notation 95
.

We denote by
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{mult}}

the relation between nets such
that
S\rightarrow_{\mathsf{mult}}S^{\prime}

whenever
S\smash{\xrightarrow{c}}S^{\prime}

and
c
is multiplicative,
on the other hand we denote

S\rightarrow_{\neg\mathsf{mult}}S^{\prime}

whenever

S\smash{\xrightarrow{c}}S^{\prime}

and
c
is not a multiplicative cut.
Similarly one defined

\rightarrow_{\mathsf{glue}}
,
\rightarrow_{\neg\mathsf{glue}}
,

\rightarrow_{\mathsf{(\invamp/\maltese)}}
,
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{\neg(\invamp/\maltese)}}
,

\rightarrow_{\mathsf{(\otimes/\maltese)}}
and
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{\neg(\otimes/\maltese)}}
.

Definition 96
.

Given a net
S

two cuts
c_{1}
and
c_{2}

in
S
are
unrelated

whenever:

•

S
can be written as
C+R_{1}+R_{2}

where
R_{1}
is the redex of the cut
c_{1}

and
R_{2}
is the redex of the cut
c_{2}
.

•

By eliminating
c_{1}
,
S
reduces to
C+R_{1}^{\prime}+R_{2}
.

•

By eliminating
c_{2}
,
S
reduces to
C+R_{1}+R_{2}^{\prime}
.

Proposition 97

(Strong Confluence of unrelated cuts)
.

Given
S
some net
containing two reductible and unrelated cuts
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
.
The diagram below commutes:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{1}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{2}}

Where the dotted arrows are the existence of a reduction.

Proof.

Let us fix a net
S

containing (at least) two unrelated cuts
that we denote
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
.
This means that
one can write

S
has
C+R_{1}+R_{2}

where the
R_{i}
are the corresponding redexes.
Assuming (without loss of generality)
that
R_{i}\rightarrow R_{i}^{\prime}
:
by contextual closure, the elimination
of
c_{1}
rewrites
S

in
S_{1}=C+R_{1}^{\prime}+R_{2}

and the elimination of
c_{2}

rewrites
S
in
S_{2}=C+R_{1}+R_{2}^{\prime}

Using contextual closure once more we conclude that

S_{1}
reduces to
C+R_{1}^{\prime}+R_{2}^{\prime}

by eliminating
c_{2}

while
S_{2}
reduces to
C+R_{1}^{\prime}+R_{2}^{\prime}

by eliminating
c_{1}
.
∎

Remark 98
.

Consider a cut
c
which is not multiplicative in a net
S

if
c
is not related with a cut
c^{\prime}
in
S

then if
S\smash{\xrightarrow{c}}S^{\prime}

the cut produced by
c

are also unrelated with
c^{\prime}
.

Definition 99

(Strongly unrelated cuts)
.

We say that two cuts

c_{1}=\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},q_{1}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

and
c_{2}=\mathopen{\langle}p_{2},q_{2}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

are
strongly unrelated

whenever they are distinct
and the set
D_{1}
of daimon links
above
p_{1}
or
q_{1}

does not intersect with
the set
D_{2}
of daimon links
above
p_{2}
or
q_{2}
.

Remark 100
.

The notion of strongly unrelated
cuts
is to obtain the stability of unrelated cuts (as in
98
)
also for multiplicative cuts.
Given a multiplicative cut
c
of a net
S

and a cut
c^{\prime}
of
S
unrelated with
c
,
if
S\smash{\xrightarrow{c}}S^{\prime}

then the cut produced by the elimination of
c

remain unrelated with
c
in
S^{\prime}
.

Proposition 101
.

Given a net
S

and a partition of its set of cut links

\mathsf{cut}(S)=C_{1}\uplus C_{2}

such that each cuts of
C_{1}
and
C_{2}

are strongly unrelated
then the assertions are equivalent:

1.

S\rightarrow^{*}T

2.

S\rightarrow^{*}_{1}S^{\prime}\rightarrow^{*}_{2}T
,
where
\rightarrow_{1}
eliminates cut from

C_{1}
or the cut it produces
and
\rightarrow_{2}
eliminates cuts from
C_{2}

or the cut it produces.

Proof.

1\Rightarrow 2.

If two cut
c
and
d
are unrelated:
if
c
is not multiplicative, any cut created by the elimination of
c

will still be unrelated with
d
(
98
).
If
c
is multiplicative since
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
are strongly unrelated
and thus
c
and
d
are strongly unrelated cuts,
the non multiplicative cuts say
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
created by the elimination of
c

involve a different daimon link than that of
d

thus
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
are still unrelated with
d
(
100
).
Therefore, it follows that for each step of cut elimination

\smash{\xrightarrow{c}}_{1}

and
\smash{\xrightarrow{c^{\prime}}}_{2}

the cuts
c
and
c^{\prime}
are unrelated
thus using the property of confluence of unrelated cuts
(
97
)
one concludes.

2\Rightarrow 1.

indeed because
\rightarrow_{1}

and
\rightarrow_{2}

are special case of the cut elimination
\rightarrow
.
∎

B.3
Multiplicative Factorization of Cut Elimination
– Proof of
30

item 1

Remark 102
.

Given a cut
c
in a net
S

if
c
is a multiplicative cut
then for any other cut
c^{\prime}
of
S
:

c
and
c^{\prime}
are unrelated,
because the redexes of cuts only involve three links
made of the cut link and the two links above the input of that cut.
Therefore two cuts of two distinct multiplicative redexes cannot involve a same connective link
since otherwise it means
that these two cuts
have for input a same position
i.e. the target of the connective link
(this violates the property of
targget–disjointness
of nets).

Proposition 103

(General commutation of the Multiplicative cuts)
.

For any net
S

containing a multiplicative cut
c
,
for any sequence of cuts
\alpha^{*}
in
S

then the following diagrams commutes;
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{1}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}

Where the dotted arrows are the existence of a reduction.

Proof.

Because the multiplicative cut
c

is unrelated with any cut
d
of
S
(
102
)
we conclude using the confluence of unrelated cuts (
97
).
∎

Remark 104
.

If
S
reduces to
S^{\prime}

by eliminating a non multiplicative cut
then any multiplicative cut
c
occuring in
S^{\prime}

is a multiplicative cut occuring in
S
.

Remark 105
.

Given a net
S
containing a cut
c

and one of its reduction
S\rightarrow_{c}S^{\prime}
,
if a multiplicative or clash cut
c^{\prime}
occurs in
S^{\prime}
and not
S
:
then
c
was a multiplicative cut.

This is because (1) glueing cuts
make cuts disappear
(2)
(\otimes/\maltese)
and
(\invamp/\maltese)
cuts
create
two new cuts which can be of types
(\otimes/\maltese)
,

(\invamp/\maltese)
or
(\maltese/\maltese)

but these new cuts are never multiplicative cuts.

Proposition 106

(Cut Elimination Factorisation)
.

The relation induced by the cut–elimination rewriting

\rightarrow^{*}
of
22
and
19

is equal to

\rightarrow_{\mathsf{mult}}^{*}\cdot\rightarrow_{\neg{\mathsf{mult}}}^{*}
.

Proof.

The inclusion
of
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{mult}}^{*}\cdot\rightarrow_{\neg{\mathsf{mult}}}^{*}

in
\rightarrow^{*}
is obvious.

Let us now show the converse inclusion.
We do so by induction on the length of the reduction sequence.
If there is only one reduction this is trivial.

Let us treat the general case when
S\rightarrow^{*}S^{\prime}

doing a sequence of
n>1
steps.
Then this can be decomposed
as
S\rightarrow^{*}S_{0}\smash{\xrightarrow{c}}S^{\prime}
;
apply the induction hypothesis
yielding that there exists a net
S_{1}
such that

S\rightarrow_{\mathsf{mult}}^{*}S_{1}\rightarrow_{\neg\mathsf{mult}}^{*}S_{0}
.
Two case may occur depending on the type of the cut
c
.

•

If
c
is not multiplicative;
then
S_{0}\rightarrow_{\neg\mathsf{mult}}S^{\prime}

and from
S\rightarrow_{\mathsf{mult}}^{*}\cdot\rightarrow_{\neg\mathsf{mult}}^{*}S_{0}
.
we derive

S\rightarrow_{\mathsf{mult}}^{*}\cdot\rightarrow_{\neg\mathsf{mult}}^{*}S^{\prime}
.

•

If
c
is multiplicative;
note that whenever

T\rightarrow_{\neg\mathsf{mult}}^{*}T^{\prime}

a multiplicative cut
c
occuring in
T^{\prime}

will aslo occur in
T
and be also a multiplicative cut (
104
).
To conclude however it will be necessary to do an induction on the reduction sequence

S_{1}\rightarrow_{\neg\mathsf{mult}}^{*}S_{0}
:

–

If it contains only one step of reduction;
then the situation is the following

S_{1}\smash{\xrightarrow{c^{\prime}}}S_{0}\smash{\xrightarrow{c}}S^{\prime}

where
c^{\prime}
is not multiplicative
and
c
is a multiplicative cut.
Applying
104

non multiplicative cuts cannot create multiplicative cuts,
therefore the cut
c
also appears in
S_{1}

and thus both cuts
c
and
c^{\prime}
occur in
S_{1}
.
As a consequence since multiplicative
commute (
103
)
it follows that

S_{1}\smash{\xrightarrow{c}}S_{0}^{\prime}\smash{\xrightarrow{c^{\prime}}}S^{\prime}
.
Therefore
S_{1}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{mult}}^{*}\rightarrow_{\neg\mathsf{mult}}^{*}S^{\prime}
.

–

If the sequence of non multiplicative cut–elimination
contains
n
element.
Note that it may be factorized as

S_{1}\rightarrow_{\neg\mathsf{mult}}^{*}S_{2}\rightarrow_{\neg\mathsf{mult}}S_{0}

while
S_{0}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{mult}}S^{\prime}
.
Applying the previous result (the case when the
\rightarrow_{\neg\mathsf{mult}}
sequence is of length
1
)
we derive that

S_{1}\rightarrow_{\neg\mathsf{mult}}^{*}S_{2}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{mult}}S_{0}^{\prime}\rightarrow_{\neg\mathsf{mult}}S^{\prime}
.
Since the sequence
S_{1}\rightarrow_{\neg\mathsf{mult}}^{*}S_{2}

has a smaller length than that of

S_{1}\rightarrow_{\neg\mathsf{mult}}^{*}S_{2}\rightarrow_{\neg\mathsf{mult}}S_{0}

we call the induction hypothesis
and ensure

S_{1}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{mult}}^{*}\cdot\rightarrow_{\neg\mathsf{mult}}^{*}S_{0}^{\prime}

Finally since
S_{0}^{\prime}\rightarrow_{\neg\mathsf{mult}}S^{\prime}

we derive

S_{1}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{mult}}^{*}\cdot\rightarrow_{\neg\mathsf{mult}}^{*}S^{\prime}
.

Now we can conclude;
the situtation was

S\rightarrow_{\mathsf{mult}}^{*}S_{1}\rightarrow_{\neg\mathsf{mult}}^{*}S_{0}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{mult}}S^{\prime}

But we have shown that

S_{1}\rightarrow_{\neg\mathsf{mult}}^{*}S_{0}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{mult}}S^{\prime}

yields

S_{1}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{mult}}^{*}\cdot\rightarrow_{\neg\mathsf{mult}}^{*}S^{\prime}
.
This yields

S\rightarrow_{\mathsf{mult}}^{*}S_{1}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{mult}}^{*}\cdot\rightarrow_{\neg\mathsf{mult}}^{*}S^{\prime}

which means

S\rightarrow_{\mathsf{mult}}^{*}\cdot\rightarrow_{\neg\mathsf{mult}}^{*}S^{\prime}
.

∎

Remark 107
.

The previous proposition
is a novelty of the cut elimination with generalised axiom we have introduced.
The standard cut–elimination procedure of
\mathsf{MLL}
proof structure (
[
7
]
)
does not enjoy such a factorisation:
mainly because the
105

fails,
a non multiplicative cut (that is in that case an
\mathsf{(ax/cut)}
cut)
can be eliminated creating a single cut
that may be a multiplicative cut.

Remark 108
.

If
S\rightarrow_{\mathsf{mult}}^{*}S_{0}

i.e.
S
reduces to
S_{0}

by eliminating multiplicative cuts only,
then
S
and
S_{0}
have the same orthogonal.

B.4
Delaying Irreversible cuts elimination – proof of
30

item 2

Proposition 109

(Non–Deterministic non homogeneous cut elimination commutes to the right)
.

Given
S
some net
containing a non homogeneous cut link
c

of a daimon link against a
\invamp
–link.
The diagram below commutes,
for any cut kind of cut link
c^{\prime}
;
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{1}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}

Where the dotted arrows are the existence of a reduction.

Proof.

If
c^{\prime}
is a multiplicative cut
the commutation holds (
103
).
And more generally if
c^{\prime}
and
c
are unrelated then
the commutation also hold (
97
).
Thus
c^{\prime}
is a glueing or non homogeneous cut
involving the same daimon link as
c
.

Let us treat each cases
depending on the type of the cut
c^{\prime}
;

•

Assuming that
c^{\prime}
is a glueing cut,
and assuming (without loss of generality)
that the two first conclusions
of the daimon link are the premisses of the cut links
c
and
c^{\prime}
,
the reduction will be of the form:

\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{1},\dots,p_{n}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}r_{1},r_{2}\vartriangleright_{\invamp}r\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}q_{1},\dots,q_{k}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},r\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{2},q_{1}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

\rightarrow

\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{1}^{1},p_{2},A\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{1}^{2},B\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}q_{1},\dots,q_{k}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1}^{1},r_{2}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1}^{2},r_{2}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{2},q_{1}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

\rightarrow

\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{1}^{1},q_{2},\dots,q_{k},A\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{1}^{2},B\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1}^{1},r_{2}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1}^{2},r_{2}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

By consistently
chosing the partition during the elimination
of the cut
c

this can be matched by first starting with the elimination
of the glueing cut
c^{\prime}
.

\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{1},\dots,p_{n}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}r_{1},r_{2}\vartriangleright_{\invamp}r\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}q_{1},\dots,q_{k}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},r\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{2},q_{1}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

\rightarrow

\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{1},q_{2},\dots,q_{k},\dots,p_{n}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}r_{1},r_{2}\vartriangleright_{\invamp}r\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},r\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

\rightarrow

\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{1}^{1},q_{2},\dots,q_{k},A\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{1}^{2},B\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1}^{1},r_{1}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1}^{2},r_{2}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

•

In the first non homogeneous case the redex is of the following form

\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{1},\dots,p_{n}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}r_{1},r_{2}\vartriangleright_{\invamp}r\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}q_{1},q_{2}\vartriangleright_{\otimes}q\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},r\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{2},q\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}.

After one step of cut elimination this becomes
in all generality, given that
A=\{a_{1},\dots,a_{k}\}

and
B=\{b_{1},\dots,b_{l}\}
partition
p_{2},\dots,p_{n}
,

\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{1}^{1},p_{2},A^{\prime}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{1}^{2},B\mathclose{\rangle}++\mathopen{\langle}q_{1},q_{2}\vartriangleright_{\otimes}q\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1}^{1},r_{1}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1}^{2},r_{2}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{2},q\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}.

Without loss of generality assume that

p_{2}
occurs in the class
A=\{p_{2}\}\cup A^{\prime}
,
then after one step of cut elimination this becomes;

\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{1}^{1},p_{2}^{1},p_{2}^{2},A^{\prime}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{1}^{2},B\mathclose{\rangle}++\mathopen{\langle}p_{1}^{1},r_{1}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1}^{2},r_{2}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{2}^{1},q_{1}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{2}^{2},q_{2}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}.

Indeed one can obtain the same redex
by first eliminating
c^{\prime}
,
when eliminating the
\invamp
–link
we need to make a consistent choice
e.g.
the partition of
p_{2}^{1},p_{2}^{2},\dots,p_{n}

made of the two classes
B

and
A^{\prime}\cup\{p_{2}^{1},p_{2}^{2}\}
:

\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{1},\dots,p_{n}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}r_{1},r_{2}\vartriangleright_{\invamp}r\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}q_{1},q_{2}\vartriangleright_{\otimes}q\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},r\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{2},q\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

\rightarrow

\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{1},p_{2}^{1},p_{2}^{2}\dots,p_{n}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}r_{1},r_{2}\vartriangleright_{\invamp}r\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},r\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{2}^{1},q_{1}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{2}^{2},q_{2}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

\rightarrow

\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{1},p_{2}^{1},p_{2}^{2},\dots,p_{n}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}r_{1},r_{2}\vartriangleright_{\invamp}r\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},r\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{2}^{1},q_{1}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{2}^{2},q_{2}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

\rightarrow

\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{1}^{1},p_{2}^{1},p_{2}^{2},A^{\prime}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{1}^{2},B\mathclose{\rangle}++\mathopen{\langle}p_{1}^{1},r_{1}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1}^{2},r_{2}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{2}^{1},q_{1}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{2}^{2},q_{2}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}.

•

In the non homogeneous second case
both cuts are made of
\invamp
links
against a daimon thus
in all generality a reduction is of the
following form form;

\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{1},\dots,p_{n}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}r_{1},r_{2}\vartriangleright_{\invamp}r\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}q_{1},q_{2}\vartriangleright_{\invamp}q\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},r\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{2},q\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

\rightarrow

\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{1}^{1},p_{2},A\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{1}^{2},B\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}q_{1},q_{2}\vartriangleright_{\invamp}q\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{2},q\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1}^{1},r_{1}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1}^{2},r_{2}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

\rightarrow

\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{1}^{1},p_{2}^{1},A_{1}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{2}^{2},A_{2}^{2}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{1}^{2},B\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{2}^{1},q_{1}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{2}^{2},q_{2}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1}^{1},r_{1}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1}^{2},r_{2}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

Indeed starting by eliminating the cut

we can obtain the same redex
by making consistent choices in the partitions:

\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{1},\dots,p_{n}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}r_{1},r_{2}\vartriangleright_{\invamp}r\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}q_{1},q_{2}\vartriangleright_{\invamp}q\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},r\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{2},q\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

\rightarrow

\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{2}^{1},p_{1},A_{1}\cup B\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{2}^{2},A_{2}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}r_{1},r_{2}\vartriangleright_{\invamp}r\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},r\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{2}^{1},q_{1}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{2}^{2},q_{2}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

\rightarrow

\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{1}^{1},p_{2}^{1},A_{1}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{2}^{2},A_{2}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{1}^{2},B\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{2}^{1},q_{1}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{2}^{2},q_{2}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1}^{1},r_{1}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1}^{2},r_{2}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

∎

Proposition 110

(General right commutation,
30
item 2
)
.

For any net
S

containing an irreversible cut
c
,
and given
\alpha^{*}

a series of cut elimination
of cuts occuring in
S

then the following diagram commutes;
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{1}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}

Proof.

This follows from a simple induction
on
\alpha^{*}
.
If
\alpha^{*}
is made of only one cut
the proposition
109

gets us the conclusion.
Otherwise we decompose the sequence of reductions
as
\alpha^{*}=\beta\cdot\beta^{*}
and
by applying the proposition
109
we obtain
the following diagram.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{1}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta^{*}}

Calling the induction hypothesis on
\beta^{*}

we can complete the diagram as follows
and conclude.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{1}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{2}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}

∎

B.5
Reversible cuts can be anticipated – Proof of
30

item 3

Proposition 111

(Deterministic non homogeneous cut elimination commutes to the left)
.

Given
S
some net
containing a non homogeneous cut link
c

of a daimon link against a
\otimes
–link.
The diagram below commutes,
for any cut kind of cut link
c^{\prime}
;
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{1}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{\prime}}

Where the dotted arrows are the existence of a reduction.

Proof.

We reason as in the proof for proposition
109
,
if the two cuts involved different link of the net we
conclude.
Furthermore if
c^{\prime}
is an homogeneous cut the proposition hold.
Thus we assume that
c
and
c^{\prime}

are both non–homogeneous and involve the same daimon link.

•

If
c^{\prime}
is also a tensor link
its clear that the elimination of the two cuts commute.

•

If
c^{\prime}
is a
\invamp
link
we can call the previous proposition
109

on the cut
c^{\prime}
,
claiming that its elimination commutes on the right
with any step of cut elimination.
In particular any elimination of
c^{\prime}
followed by any elimination of
c

can be matched by an elimination of
c
followed by an elimination of
c^{\prime}
.
Thus we conclude.

∎

Proposition 112

(General left commutation)
.

For any net
S

containing an reversible cut
c
,
and given
\alpha^{*}

a series of cut elimination
of cuts occuring in
S

then the following diagram commutes;
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{1}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{*}}

Proof.

As for the previous proposition
it follows from a simple induction
on
\alpha^{*}
.
If
\alpha^{*}
is made of only one cut
the proposition
111

gets us to conclude.
Otherwise we decompose the sequence of reductions
as
\alpha^{*}=\beta^{*}\cdot\beta
and
by applying the proposition
111

together with the induction hypothesis
we obtain the following diagram.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{1}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

∎

Remark 113
.

Moreover, following a similar method, we can easily establish
that glueing cuts commute to the left
with any kind of cut.
The case with a
(\invamp/\maltese)
cut
is taken care of
since we know these cuts can be delayed (
30

item 2
).
For the other cases note that two related glueing cuts indeed commute,
furthermore by some simple calculations,
one can prove that a glueing cut and
(\otimes/\maltese)
cut
will also commute.
Indeed the result of commutation of two cuts may then be generalised to sequences
of cut elimination as in
112
.

Proposition 114

(
30

item 3
)
.

Given a net
S
containing a cut
c

that is not a
(\invamp/\maltese)
cut.
If
S\rightarrow^{*}\smash{\xrightarrow{c}}S^{\prime}

then
S\smash{\xrightarrow{c}}\rightarrow^{*}S^{\prime}
.

Proof.

This is a consequence
of
112

and
113
.
∎

Appendix C
Complements to
section 2

C.1
Multigraph

The notion of multigraph
is used several times:
(1) in order to define orthogonal partitions
one must compute a multigraph
and
(2) in all generality
the graph underlying an hypergraph
should also be a multigraph
– this will be defined in the next section of this appendix (
subsection C.2
).

Definition 115
.

An
(undirected) multigraph
is a tuple

G=(V_{G},E_{G},\mathsf{brd}_{G})

such that:

•

V_{G}
and
E_{G}

are two disjoint sets,
whose elements are respectively called
nodes

and
edges
.

•

The border function
\mathsf{brd}_{G}:E_{G}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}{(V_{G})}

maps edges to sets of nodes of cardinal
1
or
2
.
The intuition is that
whenever
\mathsf{brd}_{G}(e)=\{x,y\}

then
x
and
y
are the two endpoints of the edge
e
,
while if
\mathsf{brd}_{G}(e)=\{x\}

the edge
e
forms a
loop
.

C.2
Graph underlying an hypergraph

In
section 2

we informally refer to the notion of graph
underlying an hypergraph.
This induced graph can be formally defined
as in the following
116

Definition 116

(Underlying graph)
.

Given an hypergraph
\mathcal{H}=(V_{\mathcal{H}},E_{\mathcal{H}},\mathrm{t}_{\mathcal{H}},\mathrm{s}_{\mathcal{H}})

its
underlying directed graph

is
a directed graph denoted

\mathsf{G}(\mathcal{H})=(V_{G},E_{G},\mathrm{t}_{G},\mathrm{s}_{G})

obtained from
\mathcal{H}

as follow;

•

A vertex of
\mathsf{G}(\mathcal{H})

is either a position of the hypergraph
\mathcal{H}
,
or a link of
\mathcal{H}
,
i.e.
V_{G}=V_{\mathcal{H}}\uplus E_{\mathcal{H}}
.

•

An edge of
\mathsf{G}(\mathcal{H})

is a tuple
(e,p,\mathsf{in})

of a link
e\in E_{\mathcal{H}}

with
p\in\mathrm{s}(e)

or a tuple
(e,p,\mathsf{out})

with
p\in\mathrm{t}(e)
.

•

For an edge

(e,p,\mathsf{in})

we have

\mathrm{t}(e,p,\mathsf{in})=e

and
\mathrm{s}(e,p,\mathsf{in})=p
.
For an edge

(e,p,\mathsf{out})

we have

\mathrm{t}(e,p,\mathsf{out})=p

and
\mathrm{s}(e,p,\mathsf{out})=e
.

Definition 117

(Induced undirected graph)
.

Given a directed graph
G=(V_{G},E_{G},\mathrm{t}_{G},\mathrm{s}_{G})

its induced undirected graph
is the graph

G^{*}=(V_{G}^{*},E_{G}^{*},\mathsf{brd}_{G^{*}})

such that:

•

The set of vertices
V_{G}
and
V_{G}^{*}

are the same;

•

The set of edges
E_{G}
and
E_{G}^{*}

are the same;

•

For any edge
e

its border in
G^{*}

is given by

\mathsf{brd}_{G^{*}}(e)=\{\mathrm{s}_{G}(e),\mathrm{t}_{G}(e)\}
.

Remark 118
.

The paths of
the induced undirected graph
G^{*}

of a directed (multi)graph

G=(V_{G},E_{G},\mathrm{t}_{G},\mathrm{s}_{G})

are exactly the paths in
G

where we allow edges to be travelled
through in both direction
(source–target or target–source).

Definition 119
.

Given an hypergraph
\mathcal{H}

its
underlying graph

\mathsf{G}(\mathcal{H})^{*}

is the induced undirected graph of
\mathsf{G}(\mathcal{H})
.

The previous definition is the one involved in
in the theorem of Danos Regnier (
Theorem 40
):
given a switching
\sigma S
of a net
S

the acyclicity and connectedness of
\sigma S

is that of its underlying undirected graph
\mathsf{G}(\sigma S)^{*}
.

C.3
On substitutions – proof of
33

See
33

Proof.

This is because
if a sequent
\Delta
can be introduced
by a daimon rule (resp. an axiom rule for
\mathsf{MLL}
)
then for any substitution
\theta

the sequent
\theta\Delta

can be introduced by a daimon rule (resp. an axiom rule for
\mathsf{MLL}
).
Thus a proof by induction on the represented proof tree
allows us to conclude.
∎

Remark 120
.

The
33
implies that
whenever
A\leq B

the set

\lBrace A:\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}\rBrace

is contained in
\lBrace B:\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}\rBrace

but also that

\lBrace A:\mathsf{MLL}\rBrace

is contained in
\lBrace B:\mathsf{MLL}\rBrace

Appendix D
Complements to
section 3

D.1
On the notion of Interaction

D.1.1
Proof of
48

We have presented
a notion of interaction
S::T

between two net
S
and
T
in
41
,
this definition makes direct use of the order on the conclusions
of the nets (the arrangement of
section 1
).
An alternative can be to define
the interaction between two nets
S
and
T

using a notion of
interface

\sigma
,
a functional and injective relation between the conclusions
of
S
and
T
,
then
S::_{\sigma}T

is equal to

S+T+\sum_{(p,q)\in\sigma}\mathopen{\langle}S(p),T(p)\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}
.
This makes orthogonality more subtle to define
so we chose to work with ordered conclusions
which determines the interaction
S::T
,
in some sense the order on the conclusions
is used to define a canonical interface.

See
48

Proof.

Consider three net
S,T
and
R

such that
\#S\geq\#T+\#R
.
Note that
\#T\parallel\#R=\#T+\#R

thus
\#S\geq\#T\parallel\#R

and so

min(\#S,\#T\parallel\#R)=\#T\parallel\#R
.
As a consequence
S::(T\parallel R)

is equal to

S::(T\parallel R)=S+T+R+\sum_{1\leq i\leq\#T\parallel\#R}\mathopen{\langle}S(i),(T\parallel R)(i)\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

Note that
for any
1\leq i\leq\#T

we have
(T\parallel R)(i)=T(i)

thus:

S::(T\parallel R)=\left(S+T+\sum_{1\leq i\leq\#T}\mathopen{\langle}S(i),T(i)\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}\right)+R+\sum_{\#T+1\leq i\leq\#T\parallel R}\mathopen{\langle}S(i),(T\parallel R)(i)\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

Furthermore
note that for each
\#T+1\leq i\leq\#S

we have that
the conclusion
S(i)

is the conclusion
(S::T)(i-\#T)
of
S
.
Also for any
\#T+1\leq i\leq\#T+\#R

we have
(T\parallel R)(i)

equal to
R(i-\#T)
.
Thus we can rewrite the interaction
S::(T\parallel R)

as follows:

\displaystyle S::(T\parallel R)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(S+T+\sum_{1\leq i\leq\#T}\mathopen{\langle}S(i),T(i)\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}\right)+R+\sum_{\#T+1\leq i\leq\#T\parallel R}\mathopen{\langle}S(i),(T\parallel R)(i)\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(S+T+\sum_{1\leq i\leq\#T}\mathopen{\langle}S(i),T(i)\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}\right)+R+\sum_{\#T+1\leq i\leq\#T\parallel R}\mathopen{\langle}(S::T)(i-\#T),R(i-\#T)\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(S+T+\sum_{1\leq i\leq\#T}\mathopen{\langle}S(i),T(i)\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}\right)+R+\sum_{\#T+1-\#T\leq i\leq\#T\parallel R-\#T}\mathopen{\langle}(S::T)(i),R(i)\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(S+T+\sum_{1\leq i\leq\#T}\mathopen{\langle}S(i),T(i)\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}\right)+R+\sum_{1\leq i\leq R}\mathopen{\langle}(S::T)(i),R(i)\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle S::T+R+\sum_{1\leq i\leq R}\mathopen{\langle}(S::T)(i),R(i)\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(S::T)::R

∎

D.1.2
Interaction of nets and unrelated connective links

The following proposition
will be used in the proof of the result of adequacy
(
Theorem 64
) in the inductive cases.
In particular when one interprets
S
as the image of a proof

\pi_{0}
of
\Gamma,A,B
,
the link
e
represents a
\invamp
-link (say a link
\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},p_{2}\vartriangleright_{\invamp}p\mathclose{\rangle}
)
and
T
is an opponent from
\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}
.

Proposition 121
.

Given two nets
S
and
T

with
\#S\geq\#T

and a link
e
such that
the positions of
\mathrm{s}(e)

are included in the outputs of
S::T

and are the only conclusions of
S
.
Then we have:

(S::T)+e=(S+e)::T.

Proof.

Recall
the definition of the interaction

S::T=S+\sum_{1\leq i\leq\#T}\mathopen{\langle}S(i),T(i)\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+T

Thus we have that

(S::T)+e=\left(S+\sum_{1\leq i\leq\#T}\mathopen{\langle}S(i),T(i)\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+T\right)+e

Because each input of
e
is a conclusion of
S

in
S::T

then each input of
e
is a conclusions

S(j)
of
S
with
j\geq\#T
.
Furthermore say
S=(\left|{S}\right|,\mathbf{a}(S))

then
S+e=(\left|{S}\right|+e,(\mathbf{a}(S)\setminus{\mathrm{s}(e)})\cdot\mathrm{t}(e))

and for each
1\leq i\leq\#T

we have
S(i)=(S+e)(i)
.
It follows that:

\left(S+\sum_{1\leq i\leq\#T}\mathopen{\langle}S(i),T(i)\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+T\right)+e=\left(S+\sum_{1\leq i\leq\#T}\mathopen{\langle}(S+e)(i),T(i)\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+T\right)+e

Which itself is equal to

(S+e)+\sum_{1\leq i\leq\#T}\mathopen{\langle}(S+e)(i),T(i)\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+T=(S+e)::T

∎

D.2
On types, proving duality results and associativity

D.2.1
The Merge Operator

Definition 122

(Merge of two nets)
.

The
merge
of two daimon links
d=\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\overline{p}\mathclose{\rangle}

and
d^{\prime}=\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\overline{q}\mathclose{\rangle}

is the daimon link
d\bowtie d^{\prime}=\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\overline{p},\overline{q}\mathclose{\rangle}
.
Given
a net
S
containinng a daimon link
d

i.e.
S=d+S_{0}

and a net
S^{\prime}
containing a daimon link
d^{\prime}

i.e.
S^{\prime}=d^{\prime}+S^{\prime}_{0}
.
We denote by
S\bowtie^{(d,d^{\prime})}S^{\prime}

the net
S_{0}+S_{0}^{\prime}+(d\bowtie d^{\prime})
.

Proposition 123
.

Given two nets
S
and
S^{\prime}

respectively containing daimons
d
and
d^{\prime}

so that
S=S_{0}+d
and
S^{\prime}=S_{0}^{\prime}+d^{\prime}
:

S\bowtie^{(d,d^{\prime})}S^{\prime}

is equal to
S\bowtie^{(d,d^{\prime})}d^{\prime}+S_{0}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

One merely needs
to write the nets

S\bowtie^{(d,d^{\prime})}S^{\prime}

and
(S\bowtie^{(d,d^{\prime})}d^{\prime})+S_{0}^{\prime}

as sum of links
to conclude.
∎

Notation 124
.

We fix a convention
to denote the daimon links resulting from cut elimination steps
involving daimons (glueing and non homogeneous):

•

In a glueing step
say
d=\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\overline{p},a\mathclose{\rangle}

and
d^{\prime}=\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\overline{q},b\mathclose{\rangle}

the glueing redex
d+\mathopen{\langle}a,b\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+d^{\prime}

reducing to
\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\overline{p},\overline{q}\mathclose{\rangle}

we denote that resulting daimon link by
g_{a,b}(d,d^{\prime})
.

•

In a
(\otimes/\maltese)
step
say
d=\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\overline{p},a\mathclose{\rangle}

the reddex
d+\mathopen{\langle}a,b\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}b_{1},b_{2}\vartriangleright_{\otimes}b\mathclose{\rangle}

reducing to
\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\overline{p},a_{1},a_{2}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}a_{1},b_{1}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}a_{2},b_{2}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

we denote that resulting daimon link by
p_{a}(d)
.

•

In a
(\invamp/\maltese)
step
say
d=\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\overline{p},a\mathclose{\rangle}

the reddex
d+\mathopen{\langle}a,b\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}b_{1},b_{2}\vartriangleright_{\invamp}b\mathclose{\rangle}

reducing to
\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\overline{p}^{A},a_{1}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\overline{p}^{B},a_{2}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}a_{1},b_{1}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}a_{2},b_{2}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

for a partition
\overline{p}=\overline{p}^{A}\uplus\overline{p}^{B}

we denote that resulting daimon links by
t^{1}_{a,A}(d)

and
t^{2}_{a,B}(d)
.

Given a reduction step
S\rightarrow S^{\prime}

eliminating a cut
c

we denote
S\rightarrow^{c}S^{\prime}
.
Given an elimination step
S\rightarrow^{c}S^{\prime}

that isn’t multiplicative,
we denote by

d_{c}
the daimon involve in the redex
if it is unique,
otherwise we denote
the unordered pair of daimons
g(d_{c})

in the case of a glueing cut.

Proposition 125
.

Given a net
S
containing a cut
c=\mathopen{\langle}a,b\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

and daimon
d
of
S

if
S\rightarrow^{c}S^{\prime}
:

•

If
c
is multiplicative

S\bowtie^{d}\maltese_{1}\rightarrow^{c}S^{\prime}\bowtie^{d}\maltese_{1}
.

•

If
d
isn’t
d_{c}
or contained in
g(d_{c})

then

S\bowtie^{d}\maltese_{1}\rightarrow^{c}S^{\prime}\bowtie^{d}\maltese_{1}
.

•

If
c
is a glueing cut
and
d
belongs to
g(d_{c})

then
S\bowtie^{d}\maltese_{1}\rightarrow S^{\prime}\bowtie^{g_{\mathrm{s}(c)}(g(d_{c}))}\maltese_{1}

•

If
c
is a
(\otimes/\maltese)
cut
and
d=d_{c}

then
S\bowtie^{d}\maltese_{1}\rightarrow S^{\prime}\bowtie^{p_{a}(d)}\maltese_{1}

•

If
c
is a
(\invamp/\maltese)
cut
and
d=d_{c}

and
S\rightarrow^{c}S^{\prime}
by chosing a partition
A\uplus B
of the daimon
d=\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\overline{p}\mathclose{\rangle}

then
S\bowtie^{d}\maltese_{1}\rightarrow S^{\prime}\bowtie^{t^{1}_{a,A}(d)}\maltese_{1}

and
S\bowtie^{d}\maltese_{1}\rightarrow S^{\prime}\bowtie^{t^{2}_{a,B}(d)}\maltese_{1}
.

Proof.

If the reduction
S\rightarrow S^{\prime}

is a multiplicative step,
then it does not affects the daimon of
S

and therefore we conclude.
In the other cases one (or two for the glueing cut) daimon
are involved in the cut–elimination,
if these daimons don’t contain
d

then by contextual closure one can ensure the proposition.

Now let us treat the three cases where the daimon
d

is involved.

•

Say the
reduction in
S

is that of a glueing cut:

\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\overline{p},a\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}a,b\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\overline{q},b\mathclose{\rangle}\rightarrow\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\overline{p},\overline{q}\mathclose{\rangle}.

Say (without loss of generality) that
d=\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\overline{p},a\mathclose{\rangle}

then in
S\bowtie^{d}\maltese_{1}

the daimon
d
is isomorphic to
\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\overline{p},a,u\mathclose{\rangle}

and the redex becomes:

\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\overline{p},a,u\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}a,b\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\overline{q},b\mathclose{\rangle}\rightarrow\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\overline{p},u,\overline{q}\mathclose{\rangle}.

Indeed the resulting daimon
is
g_{a,b}(d_{1},d_{2})\bowtie\maltese_{1}

and therefore the redex of
S\bowtie^{d}\maltese_{1}

is
S^{\prime}\bowtie^{g_{a,b}(d_{1},d_{2})}\maltese_{1}
.

•

Say the reduction is that of
a
(\otimes,\maltese)
cut:

\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\overline{p},a\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}a,b\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}b_{1},b_{2}\vartriangleright_{\otimes}b\mathclose{\rangle}\rightarrow\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\overline{p},a_{1},a_{2}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}a_{1},b_{1}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}a_{2},b_{2}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}.

It follows that:

\displaystyle\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\overline{p},a\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}a,b\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}b_{1},b_{2}\vartriangleright_{\otimes}b\mathclose{\rangle}\bowtie\maltese_{1}

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\overline{p},a,u\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}a,b\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}b_{1},b_{2}\vartriangleright_{\otimes}b\mathclose{\rangle}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\quad

\displaystyle\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\overline{p},a_{1},a_{2},u\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}a_{1},b_{1}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}a_{2},b_{2}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\overline{p},a_{1},a_{2}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}a_{1},b_{1}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}a_{2},b_{2}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}\bowtie\maltese_{1}

Thus we have shown that
in
S\rightarrow S^{\prime}

the daimon
d
became

p_{a}(d)

and we have that

S\bowtie^{d}\maltese_{1}

rewrites to

S^{\prime}\bowtie^{p_{a}(d)}\maltese_{1}
.

•

Say the reduction is that of
a
(\invamp,\maltese)
cut:

\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\overline{p},a\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}a,b\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}b_{1},b_{2}\vartriangleright_{\invamp}b\mathclose{\rangle}\rightarrow\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\overline{p}_{A},a_{1}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\overline{p}_{B},a_{2}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}a_{1},b_{1}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}a_{2},b_{2}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}.

It follows that:

\displaystyle\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\overline{p},a\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}a,b\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}b_{1},b_{2}\vartriangleright_{\invamp}b\mathclose{\rangle}\bowtie\maltese_{1}

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\overline{p},a,u\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}a,b\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}b_{1},b_{2}\vartriangleright_{\invamp}b\mathclose{\rangle}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\quad

\displaystyle\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\overline{p}_{A},a_{1},u\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\overline{p}_{B},a_{2}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}a_{1},b_{1}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}a_{2},b_{2}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\overline{p}_{A},a_{1}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\overline{p}_{B},a_{2}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}a_{1},b_{1}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}a_{2},b_{2}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}\bowtie\maltese_{1}

Thus we have shown that
in
S\rightarrow S^{\prime}

the daimon
d
can rewrites in two daimons

t^{1}_{a,A}(d)

or
t^{2}_{b,B}(d)

for any choice of partition
A
and
B
of
\overline{p}
.
As a consequence

S\bowtie^{d}\maltese_{1}

rewrites to

S^{\prime}\bowtie^{t^{1}_{a,A}(d)}\maltese_{1}

or to
S^{\prime}\bowtie^{t^{2}_{a,B}(d)}\maltese_{1}

still for any choice of partition.

∎

Remark 126
.

The previous proposition
ensures the (less precise) statement:
given a net
S
with a daimon link
d

if
S\rightarrow S^{\prime}

then there exists a daimon
d^{\prime}

in
S^{\prime}
so that
S\bowtie^{d}\maltese_{1}\rightarrow S^{\prime}\bowtie^{d^{\prime}}\maltese_{1}
.
By a simple induction, this can be generalised to multiple steps of cut elimination.

Proposition 127
.

Given
S
and
T
two nets such that
S::T\rightarrow\maltese_{k}
,

d
a daimon of
S
,
and
d_{0}=\maltese_{n}
some daimon link:
the net
(S\bowtie^{d}\maltese_{n})::T

reduces to
\maltese_{n+k}
.

Proof.

Assume that
S
and
T
are orthogonal,
then
S::T\rightarrow\maltese_{k}
.
Consider
d
some daimon of
S

then in particular it is a daimon of
S::T

and
S\bowtie^{d}d_{0}::T

is equal to
S::T\bowtie^{d}d_{0}
.
Because
S::T\rightarrow\maltese_{k}

it follows that

S::T\bowtie^{d}\maltese_{n}\rightarrow\maltese_{k}\bowtie^{d^{\prime}}d_{0}

where
d^{\prime}
is a daimon link of
\maltese_{k}

(
126
).
Since
\maltese_{k}
is a net made of a single daimon link,
necessarily
d^{\prime}=\maltese_{k}

and thus
since
d_{0}=\maltese_{n}

\maltese_{k}\bowtie^{d^{\prime}}d_{0}=\maltese_{n+k}

Therefore we conclude

S::T\bowtie^{d}d_{0}\rightarrow\maltese_{n+k}
.
∎

Proposition 128
.

Given two nets
S
and
T
are orthogonal.

S^{\prime}
a net obtained from
S

by adding
n
conclusions to some (eventually distinct) daimons of
S

then
S^{\prime}::T
reduces to
\maltese_{n}
.

Proof.

One inductively uses the previous proposition(
127
).
∎

D.2.2
Behavior of Identity cut–nets

Definition 129
.

We call a cut
c

occuring in a net
S

irreversible
whenever it is of type
(\maltese/\invamp)

(
16
).
An
identity cut–net

whenever it is of the following form

\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\vec{a},p_{1},\vec{b},p_{2},\vec{c}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},p_{2}\vartriangleright_{\invamp}p\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p,q\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\vec{d},q,\vec{e}\mathclose{\rangle}

Namely, the
\invamp
–involved above the input
p
of the
cut link
is such that both its inputs are outputs of the same daimon .

Proposition 130

(Normal Form of Identity cut–nets)
.

An identity cut–net

S
with
n
conclusions
has a unique normal form
which is (up to isomorphism)

\maltese_{n}
.

Proof.

Note that the conclusions of an identity
cut–net are all outputs of
(one of the two) daimon links.
Writing
S
as the following:

S::=\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\vec{a},p_{1},\vec{b},p_{2},\vec{c}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},p_{2}\vartriangleright_{\invamp}p\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p,q\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\vec{d},q,\vec{e}\mathclose{\rangle}

The
n
conclusions of
S

correspond
to the set obtained from the vector

\vec{a},\vec{b},\vec{c},\vec{d},\vec{e}

(note that this vetor contains no repetition).

Fixing a choice for the partition
of the context
\vec{d},\vec{e}

of the daimon containing the position
q

we obtain a reduction:

\displaystyle\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\vec{a},p_{1},\vec{b},p_{2},\vec{c}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},p_{2}\vartriangleright_{\invamp}p\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p,q\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\vec{d},q,\vec{e}\mathclose{\rangle}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\quad

\displaystyle\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\vec{a},p_{1},\vec{b},p_{2},\vec{c}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},q_{1}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{2},q_{2}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\vec{d}_{l},q_{1},\vec{e}_{l}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\vec{d}_{r},q_{2},\vec{e}_{r}\mathclose{\rangle}

At this point two cuts occur thus two reductions are possible,
both lead to the same net.
Eliminating first
\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},q_{1}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}
:

\displaystyle\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\vec{a},p_{1},\vec{b},p_{2},\vec{c}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},q_{1}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{2},q_{2}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\vec{d}_{l},q_{1},\vec{e}_{l}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\vec{d}_{r},q_{2},\vec{e}_{r}\mathclose{\rangle}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\quad

\displaystyle\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\vec{a},\vec{b},p_{2},\vec{c},\vec{d}_{l},\vec{e}_{l}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{2},q_{2}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\vec{d}_{r},q_{2},\vec{e}_{r}\mathclose{\rangle}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\quad

\displaystyle\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\vec{a},\vec{b},\vec{d}_{r},\vec{e}_{r},\vec{c},\vec{d}_{l},\vec{e}_{l}\mathclose{\rangle}

\displaystyle\equiv\quad

\displaystyle\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\vec{a},\vec{b},\vec{c},\vec{d},\vec{e}\mathclose{\rangle}

\displaystyle\equiv\quad

\displaystyle\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}S(1),\dots,S(n)\mathclose{\rangle}

Eliminating first
\mathopen{\langle}p_{2},q_{2}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}
:

\displaystyle\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\vec{a},p_{1},\vec{b},p_{2},\vec{c}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},q_{1}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{2},q_{2}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\vec{d}_{l},q_{1},\vec{e}_{l}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\vec{d}_{r},q_{2},\vec{e}_{r}\mathclose{\rangle}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\quad

\displaystyle\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\vec{a},p_{1},\vec{b},\vec{d}_{r},\vec{e}_{r},\vec{c}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},q_{1}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\vec{d}_{l},q_{1},\vec{e}_{l}\mathclose{\rangle}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\quad

\displaystyle\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\vec{a},\vec{d}_{l},\vec{e}_{l},\vec{b},\vec{d}_{r},\vec{e}_{r},\vec{c}\mathclose{\rangle}

\displaystyle\equiv\quad

\displaystyle\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}\vec{a},\vec{b},\vec{c},\vec{d},\vec{e}\mathclose{\rangle}

\displaystyle\equiv\quad

\displaystyle\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}S(1),\dots,S(n)\mathclose{\rangle}

This is true for any choice of partition
during the first
(\maltese/\invamp)

step,
therefore

S
has a unique normal
which is (upto iso)
\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}S(1),\dots,S(n)\mathclose{\rangle}
.
∎

Remark 131
.

From
130

one derive that an identity cut–net without
conclusions always reduces to

\maltese_{0}
.

Remark 132
.

A net which is cut–free and has no conclusions
is a sum of daimon links without conclusions:
such a net cannot contain connective
otherwise to have no conclusion
a cut needs to be added,
similarly its daimon links cannot have outputs
otherwise to have no conclusions a cut must be present in the net.

Proposition 133

(Identity cut–net behavior)
.

Consider an identity cut
S
without conclusions
and denote its two daimons
d_{1}
and
d_{2}
.
Let
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
be two nets without conclusions,
and
d_{1}^{\prime}
a daimon of
T_{1}
while
d_{2}^{\prime}
denotes a daimon of
T_{2}
,
the assertions are equivalent:

1.

T_{1}\rightarrow^{*}\maltese_{0}
and
T_{2}\rightarrow^{*}\maltese_{0}
.

2.

S\bowtie^{d_{1},d_{1}^{\prime}}T_{1}\bowtie^{d_{2},d_{2}^{\prime}}T_{2}

reduces to
\maltese_{0}

Proof.

1\Rightarrow 2.
This is the easy implication.

T_{2}\rightarrow^{*}\maltese_{0}
thus by applying
127

S\bowtie^{d_{1},d_{1}^{\prime}}T_{1}\bowtie^{d_{2},d_{2}^{\prime}}T_{2}

reduces to

S\bowtie^{d_{1},d_{1}^{\prime}}T_{1}\bowtie^{d_{2},f(d_{2}^{\prime})}\maltese_{0}

that is
S\bowtie^{d_{1},d_{1}^{\prime}}T_{1}

again applying
127

with
T_{1}\rightarrow^{*}\maltese_{0}

yields that

S\bowtie^{d_{1},d_{1}^{\prime}}T_{1}

reduces to

S\bowtie^{d_{1}}\maltese_{0}

that is
S
.
We conclude since
S\rightarrow^{*}\maltese_{0}

(
131
).

2\Rightarrow 1.

The other direction requires the use of
30
(
item 2
)
and
30
(
item 3
).
The cut that we will denote
c
occuring in
S

is a
(\invamp/\maltese)
cut
thus it can be performed last (
30
item 2
).
Thus if
S\bowtie^{d_{1},d_{1}^{\prime}}T_{1}\bowtie^{d_{2},d_{2}^{\prime}}T_{2}

reduces to
\maltese_{0}

one can factorize the reduction as

S\bowtie^{d_{1},d_{1}^{\prime}}T_{1}\bowtie^{d_{2},d_{2}^{\prime}}T_{2}\rightarrow^{*}U

with
U\rightarrow_{c}\maltese_{0}
,
one can verify that this implies that
U
is (isomorphic to) the following net

\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{1},p_{2}\mathclose{\rangle}{p}+\mathopen{\langle}p,q\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}q\mathclose{\rangle}

That is,
U
and
S
are isomorphic nets.

In that case note that
the cuts of
T_{1}
and
T_{2}

in the net
S\bowtie^{d_{1},d_{1}^{\prime}}T_{1}\bowtie^{d_{2},d_{2}^{\prime}}T_{2}

are unrelated
and thus applying
97
,
one can factorize the reduction leading to
U
as follows:

S\bowtie^{d_{1},d_{1}^{\prime}}T_{1}\bowtie^{d_{2},d_{2}^{\prime}}T_{2}\rightarrow^{*}_{2}S_{1}\rightarrow^{*}_{1}S\rightarrow_{c}\rightarrow^{*}\maltese_{0}

(1)

where
\rightarrow_{1}
eliminates cuts occuring in
T_{1}

or its reducts only
and
\rightarrow_{2}
eliminates cuts occuring in
T_{2}
or its reducts only.
As a consequence using
127

one may rewrite the first step of the reduction:

(S\bowtie^{d_{1},d_{1}^{\prime}}T_{1})\bowtie^{d_{2},d_{2}^{\prime}}T_{2}\rightarrow^{*}_{2}(S\bowtie^{d_{1},d_{1}^{\prime}}T_{1})\bowtie^{d_{2},f(d_{2}^{\prime})}T_{2}^{\prime}

Necessarily because
c
(and the cut it produces) is a cut that is not in
T_{2}

or any of its redexes,
and because the cuts of
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
also are disjoint
the reduction
Equation 1

implies that
T_{2}^{\prime}
must be cut–free
and thus in particular a normal form of
T_{2}
.

Similarly
because

(S\bowtie^{d_{1},d_{1}^{\prime}}T_{1})\bowtie^{d_{2},f(d_{2}^{\prime})}T_{2}^{\prime}

reduces to

S
by
\rightarrow^{*}_{1}

applying
127

one obtains that

S_{1}=(S\bowtie^{d_{1},d_{1}^{\prime}}T_{1})\bowtie^{d_{2},f(d_{2}^{\prime})}T_{2}^{\prime}

reduces to
S

in the following way:
there is a reduction
T_{1}\rightarrow T_{1}^{\prime}

and
S
equals

(S\bowtie^{d_{1},d_{1}^{\prime}}T_{1}^{\prime})\bowtie^{d_{2},f(d_{2}^{\prime})}T_{2}^{\prime}
.
Again
because
c
(and the cut it produces) is a cut that is not in
T_{1}

or any of its redexes,
and because the cuts of
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
also are disjoint
the reduction
Equation 1

implies that
T_{1}^{\prime}
must be cut–free
and thus in particular a normal form of
T_{1}
.

From this argument one shows that

S=(S\bowtie^{d_{1},d_{1}^{\prime}}T_{1}^{\prime})\bowtie^{d_{2},f(d_{2}^{\prime})}T_{2}^{\prime}.

(2)

Furthermore
T_{1}^{\prime}
and
T_{2}^{\prime}

are both cut–free and without conclusions
they are therefore sums of
\maltese_{0}
links
132
.
If
T_{1}^{\prime}
(resp
T_{2}^{\prime}
)
is
\sum_{1\leq i\leq n}\maltese_{0}
(resp.
\sum_{1\leq i\leq k}\maltese_{0}
)
then

S=(S\bowtie^{d_{1},d_{1}^{\prime}}\sum_{1\leq i\leq n}\maltese_{0})\bowtie^{d_{2},f(d_{2}^{\prime})}T_{2}^{\prime}

equals

(S+\sum_{1\leq i\leq n-1}\maltese_{0})\bowtie^{d_{2},f(d_{2}^{\prime})}T_{2}^{\prime}

that will be

(S+\sum_{1\leq i\leq n-1}\maltese_{0})\bowtie^{d_{2},f(d_{2}^{\prime})}\sum_{1\leq i\leq k}\maltese_{0}

that is

S+\sum_{1\leq i\leq n-1}\maltese_{0}+\sum_{1\leq i\leq k-1}\maltese_{0}
.

From
Equation 2

this means that necessarily

n-1=0
and
k-1=0

i.e.

T_{1}^{\prime}
and
T_{2}^{\prime}
equal
\maltese_{0}
.
Therefore we conclude that

T_{1}\rightarrow^{*}\maltese_{0}

and

T_{2}\rightarrow^{*}\maltese_{0}

∎

D.2.3
Proofs of section
section 3
–

51
,
56
and
54

See
51

Proof.

Consider a net
S

orthogonal to
\mathbf{A}\parallel\mathbf{B}
. For any
a\in\mathbf{A}
and
b\in\mathbf{B}

the net
S::(a\parallel b)

reduces to
\maltese_{0}
. Since
S::(a\parallel b)=S::a::b
(
48
)
and since the orthogonality holds for any pair
(a,b)
, in particular for any net
b\in\mathbf{B}
the net

(S::a)::b
reduces to
\maltese_{0}
.
This means that, for any
a\in\mathbf{A}
,
S::a
is orthogonal to
\mathbf{B}
i.e.
S::a\in\mathbf{B}^{\bot}
.
Since
\mathbf{A}=(\mathbf{A}^{\bot})^{\bot}
, this means that
S\in\mathbf{A}^{\bot}\Yright\mathbf{B}^{\bot}
.

On the other hand, consider a net
S\in\mathbf{A}^{\bot}\Yright\mathbf{B}^{\bot}
:
for any
a\in\mathbf{A}=(\mathbf{A}^{\bot})^{\bot}
the net
S::a
belongs to
\mathbf{B}^{\bot}
and so for any
b\in\mathbf{B}

the net
S::a::b
reduces to
\maltese_{0}
.
Since
S::a::b=S::(a\parallel b)
(
48
), we have that
S
is orthogonal to
\mathbf{A}\parallel\mathbf{B}
(using Remark
50
).

Hence we showed
(\mathbf{A}\parallel\mathbf{B})^{\bot}=\mathbf{A}^{\bot}\Yright\mathbf{B}^{\bot}
. As a consequence
(\mathbf{A}\Yright\mathbf{B})^{\bot}=(\mathbf{A}^{{}^{\bot\bot}}\Yright\mathbf{B}^{{}^{\bot\bot}})^{\bot}=(\mathbf{A}^{\bot}\parallel\mathbf{B}^{\bot})^{{}^{\bot\bot}}=\mathbf{A}^{\bot}\parallel\mathbf{B}^{\bot}
.
∎

Lemma 134
.

Given a net with no conclusions
R

and two types with one conclusion
\mathbf{A}

and
\mathbf{B}
:
If

R\bowtie^{d}\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{1},p_{2}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},p_{2}\vartriangleright_{\invamp}p\mathclose{\rangle}
belongs to
\mathbf{A}^{\bot}\invamp\mathbf{B}^{\bot}

then

R\bowtie^{d}\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p\mathclose{\rangle}
belongs to
\mathbf{A}^{\bot}\invamp\mathbf{B}^{\bot}

Proof.

To do so one shows that

R\bowtie^{d}\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p\mathclose{\rangle}
is orthogonal to any net
orthogonal to
\mathbf{A}^{\bot}\invamp\mathbf{B}^{\bot}
.
Consider therefore an opponent

U

orthogonal to
\mathbf{A}^{\bot}\invamp\mathbf{B}^{\bot}
.
We distinguish two cases:

•

If
U=U^{\prime}+\mathopen{\langle}U^{\prime}(1),U^{\prime}(2)\vartriangleright_{\otimes}q\mathclose{\rangle}

has a terminal tensor link.
Note that, by assumption:

R\bowtie^{d}\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{1},p_{2}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},p_{2}\vartriangleright_{\invamp}p\mathclose{\rangle}::U\rightarrow^{*}\maltese_{0}

Since multiplicative cuts can be performed first
and

R\bowtie^{d}\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{1},p_{2}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},p_{2}\vartriangleright_{\invamp}p\mathclose{\rangle}::U

reduces to

R\bowtie^{d}\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{1},p_{2}\mathclose{\rangle}::U^{\prime}
by eliminating a multiplicative cut
hence we derive the following (
106
)

R\bowtie^{d}\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{1},p_{2}\mathclose{\rangle}::U^{\prime}\rightarrow^{*}\maltese_{0}.

In the meanwhile we have the following reduction:

\displaystyle R\bowtie^{d}\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p\mathclose{\rangle}::U

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle R\bowtie^{d}\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p\mathclose{\rangle}::U^{\prime}+\mathopen{\langle}U^{\prime}(1),U^{\prime}(2)\vartriangleright_{\otimes}q\mathclose{\rangle}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\quad

\displaystyle R\bowtie^{d}\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{1},p_{2}\mathclose{\rangle}::U^{\prime}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\quad

\displaystyle\maltese_{0}\mbox{(previous argument)}

•

If
U
has a terminal daimon link
outputing its only conclusion
q
.
Then
U
may written as
as
\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}q\mathclose{\rangle}\bowtie^{d^{\prime}}R^{\prime}
.
Therefore one can identify an identity cut–net
V
in the interaction:

\displaystyle R\bowtie^{d}\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{1},p_{2}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},p_{2}\vartriangleright_{\invamp}p\mathclose{\rangle}::U

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle R\bowtie^{d}\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{1},p_{2}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},p_{2}\vartriangleright_{\invamp}p\mathclose{\rangle}::\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}q\mathclose{\rangle}\bowtie^{d^{\prime}}R^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle(R\bowtie^{d}\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{1},p_{2}\mathclose{\rangle})+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},p_{2}\vartriangleright_{\invamp}p\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p,q\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+(\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}q\mathclose{\rangle}\bowtie^{d^{\prime}}R^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle[\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{1},p_{2}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},p_{2}\vartriangleright_{\invamp}p\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p,q\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+(\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}q\mathclose{\rangle}\bowtie^{d^{\prime}}R^{\prime})]\bowtie^{d}R

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle[\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{1},p_{2}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},p_{2}\vartriangleright_{\invamp}p\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p,q\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}q\mathclose{\rangle}]\bowtie^{d^{\prime}}R^{\prime}\bowtie^{d}R

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle[V]\bowtie^{d^{\prime}}R^{\prime}\bowtie^{d}R

Now since
V
is a cut net without conclusions
while
R^{\prime}
and
R
both have no conclusion
while

[V]\bowtie^{d^{\prime}}R^{\prime}\bowtie^{d}R\rightarrow^{*}\maltese_{0}

(because by assumption
R\bowtie^{d}\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p\mathclose{\rangle}::U\rightarrow\maltese_{0}
)
we derive that
R\rightarrow^{*}\maltese_{0}

and
R^{\prime}\rightarrow\maltese_{0}
(
133
).

We then derive the following

\displaystyle R\bowtie^{d}\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p\mathclose{\rangle}::U

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle R\bowtie^{d}\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p\mathclose{\rangle}::\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}q\mathclose{\rangle}\bowtie^{d^{\prime}}R^{\prime}

\displaystyle\rightarrow^{*}\quad

\displaystyle\maltese_{0}\bowtie^{d}\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p\mathclose{\rangle}::\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}q\mathclose{\rangle}\bowtie^{d^{\prime}}R^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p\mathclose{\rangle}::\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}q\mathclose{\rangle}\bowtie^{d^{\prime}}R^{\prime}

\displaystyle\rightarrow^{*}\quad

\displaystyle\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p\mathclose{\rangle}::\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}q\mathclose{\rangle}\bowtie^{d^{\prime}}\maltese_{0}

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p\mathclose{\rangle}::\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}q\mathclose{\rangle}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\quad

\displaystyle\maltese_{0}

in the end we have shown that

R\bowtie^{d}\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p\mathclose{\rangle}
belongs to
(\mathbf{A}^{\bot}\invamp\mathbf{B}^{\bot})^{{}^{\bot\bot}}

which is
\mathbf{A}^{\bot}\invamp\mathbf{B}^{\bot}
and conclude.

∎

See
56

Proof.

One equality implies the other: say that
(\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B})^{\bot}=\mathbf{A}^{\bot}\invamp\mathbf{B}^{\bot}

holds for any pair of types. Then
\mathbf{A}^{\bot}\otimes\mathbf{B}^{\bot}=(\mathbf{A}^{\bot}\otimes\mathbf{B}^{\bot})^{{}^{\bot\bot}}=(\mathbf{A}^{{}^{\bot\bot}}\invamp\mathbf{B}^{{}^{\bot\bot}})^{\bot}=(\mathbf{A}\invamp\mathbf{B})^{\bot}
.

Let us now prove
\mathbf{A}^{\bot}\invamp\mathbf{B}^{\bot}=(\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B})^{\bot}
. As for

\mathbf{A}^{\bot}\invamp\mathbf{B}^{\bot}\subseteq(\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B})^{\bot}
, we prove

\{S+\mathopen{\langle}S(1),S(2)\vartriangleright_{\otimes}p\mathclose{\rangle}\mid S\in\mathbf{A}\parallel\mathbf{B}\}\subseteq\{T+\mathopen{\langle}T(1),T(2)\vartriangleright_{\invamp}p\mathclose{\rangle}\mid T\in\mathbf{A}^{\bot}\Yright\mathbf{B}^{\bot}\}^{\bot}

which is enough to conclude because the previous inclusion yields (since bi–orthogonality preserves inclusion)

\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B}\subseteq\{T+\mathopen{\langle}T(1),T(2)\vartriangleright_{\invamp}p\mathclose{\rangle}\mid T\in\mathbf{A}^{\bot}\Yright\mathbf{B}^{\bot}\}^{\bot}

that is (since the tri–orthogonal of a set is the orthogonal of this set):

\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B}\subseteq(\mathbf{A}^{\bot}\invamp\mathbf{B}^{\bot})^{\bot}.

Which
implies
(\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B})^{\bot}\supseteq(\mathbf{A}^{\bot}\invamp\mathbf{B}^{\bot})
(Orthogonality inverts inclusion).

So let

S_{0}\in\{S+\mathopen{\langle}S(1),S(2)\vartriangleright_{\otimes}p\mathclose{\rangle}\mid S\in\mathbf{A}\parallel\mathbf{B}\}

and

T_{0}\in\{T+\mathopen{\langle}T(1),T(2)\vartriangleright_{\invamp}p\mathclose{\rangle}\mid T\in\mathbf{A}^{\bot}\Yright\mathbf{B}^{\bot}\}
.
One easily sees that
S_{0}\bot T_{0}
: after eliminating the multiplicative cut we obtain a net
S::T
,
where
S\in\mathbf{A}^{\bot}\Yright\mathbf{B}^{\bot}
and
T\in\mathbf{A}\parallel\mathbf{B}
:
these constructions are orthogonal (Proposition
51
).

As for
(\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B})^{\bot}\subseteq\mathbf{A}^{\bot}\invamp\mathbf{B}^{\bot}
,
let
T\in(\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B})^{\bot}
i.e.

T\bot\{S+\mathopen{\langle}S(1),S(2)\vartriangleright_{\otimes}p\mathclose{\rangle}\mid S\in\mathbf{A}\parallel\mathbf{B}\}^{\bot\bot}
;
we can deduce that the unique terminal link of
T
is either a
\invamp
–link or a
\maltese
–link:

•

if
T=T_{0}+l

has a terminal
\invamp
–link
l
then,
for every
S_{0}\in\mathbf{A}\parallel\mathbf{B}

the net
S::=S_{0}+\mathopen{\langle}S(1),S(2)\vartriangleright_{\otimes}p\mathclose{\rangle}
belongs
\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B}
.
In a single multiplicative step of cut elimination
T::S
reduces to

T_{0}::S_{0}
and since
S::T\rightarrow^{*}\maltese_{0}

it follows that
T_{0}::S_{0}\rightarrow^{*}\maltese_{0}
, using
30

item 3
.
As a consequence
T_{0}\bot\ \mathbf{A}\parallel\mathbf{B}

i.e.
T_{0}\in\mathbf{A}^{\bot}\Yright\mathbf{B}^{\bot}
and
T\in\mathbf{A}^{\bot}\invamp\mathbf{B}^{\bot}
;

•

if
T
has for terminal link a daimon link then

T
may be written as
R\bowtie^{d}\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p\mathclose{\rangle}

where
R
is a net without conclusions.
Let us start an argument
from the assumption that
T
belongs to
(\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B})^{\bot}
:

\displaystyle T\perp\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad

\displaystyle T\perp\{S_{a}\parallel S_{b}\mid S_{a}\in\mathbf{A},S_{b}\in\mathbf{B}\}^{{}^{\bot\bot}}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad

\displaystyle T\perp\{S_{a}\parallel S_{b}+\mathopen{\langle}S_{a}(1),S_{b}(1)\vartriangleright_{\otimes}p\mathclose{\rangle}\mid S_{a}\in\mathbf{A},S_{b}\in\mathbf{B}\}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad

\displaystyle T::S_{a}\parallel S_{b}+\mathopen{\langle}S_{a}(1),S_{b}(1)\vartriangleright_{\otimes}p\mathclose{\rangle}\rightarrow^{*}\maltese_{0}\quad(\mbox{for any }S_{a}\in\mathbf{A},S_{b}\in\mathbf{B})

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad

\displaystyle R\bowtie^{d}\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p\mathclose{\rangle}::S_{a}\parallel S_{b}+\mathopen{\langle}S_{a}(1),S_{b}(1)\vartriangleright_{\otimes}p\mathclose{\rangle}\rightarrow^{*}\maltese_{0}\quad(\mbox{for any }S_{a}\in\mathbf{A},S_{b}\in\mathbf{B})

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad

\displaystyle R\bowtie^{d}\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{1},p_{2}\mathclose{\rangle}::S_{a}\parallel S_{b}\rightarrow^{*}\maltese_{0}

\displaystyle(\mbox{\autoref{prop:SN}\autoref{item:earlyNonPar}})

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad

\displaystyle R\bowtie^{d}\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{1},p_{2}\mathclose{\rangle}\perp\{S_{a}\parallel S_{b}\mid S_{a}\in\mathbf{A},S_{b}\in\mathbf{B}\}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad

\displaystyle R\bowtie^{d}\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{1},p_{2}\mathclose{\rangle}\perp\mathbf{A}\parallel\mathbf{B}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad

\displaystyle R\bowtie^{d}\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{1},p_{2}\mathclose{\rangle}\in\mathbf{A}^{\bot}\Yright\mathbf{B}^{\bot}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\quad

\displaystyle R\bowtie^{d}\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{1},p_{2}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},p_{2}\vartriangleright_{\invamp}p\mathclose{\rangle}\in\mathbf{A}^{\bot}\invamp\mathbf{B}^{\bot}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\quad

\displaystyle R\bowtie^{d}\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p\mathclose{\rangle}\in\mathbf{A}^{\bot}\invamp\mathbf{B}^{\bot}

\displaystyle(\mbox{\autoref{lem:parrDualityHardCase}})

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad

\displaystyle T\in\mathbf{A}^{\bot}\invamp\mathbf{B}^{\bot}

∎

See
54

Proof.

The result is a consequence of the two following properties:

1.

the density of the parallel composition (
50
);

2.

48
:
more precisely,
for any nets
S_{1},S_{2},S_{3},S_{4}
such that
\#S_{1}\geq\#S_{2}+\#S_{3}+\#S_{4}
, we have

(S_{1}::(S_{2}\parallel S_{3}))::S_{4}=((S_{1}::S_{2})::S_{3})::S_{4}=(S_{1}::S_{2})::(S_{3}\parallel S_{4}).

By
51
it is enough
to show that one of the two constructions
is associative. Let us do it for the parallel composition: more precisely, using property
1
we prove that
(A\parallel(B\parallel C))^{\bot}=(A\parallel^{-}(B^{-}\parallel C^{-}))^{\bot}
and
((A\parallel B)\parallel C)^{\bot}=((A\parallel^{-}B)\parallel^{-}C)^{\bot}
, and using property
2
that
(A\parallel^{-}(B^{-}\parallel C^{-}))^{\bot}=((A\parallel^{-}B)\parallel^{-}C)^{\bot}
. From the previous equalities one deduces

A\parallel(B\parallel C)=(A\parallel(B\parallel C))^{\bot\bot}=((A\parallel B)\parallel C)^{\bot\bot}=(A\parallel B)\parallel C
.

To prove the equality

(A\parallel(B\parallel C))^{\bot}=(A\parallel^{-}(B\parallel^{-}C))^{\bot}
one proves that, for every net
S
, the following equivalence holds:
S\perp A\parallel(B\parallel C)\iff S\perp A\parallel^{-}(B^{-}\parallel C^{-})
. Indeed:

\displaystyle S\perp A\parallel(B\parallel C)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle S\perp A\parallel^{-}(B\parallel C)

(by property
1
)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\forall a\in A,\forall x\in B\parallel C,S::(a\parallel x)\rightarrow\maltese_{0}

(by definition)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\forall a\in A,\forall x\in B\parallel C,(S::a)::x\rightarrow\maltese_{0}

(by
48
)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\forall a\in A,S::a\perp(B\parallel C)

(by definition)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\forall a\in A,S::a\perp(B\parallel^{-}C)

(by property
1
)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\forall a\in A,\forall x\in(B\parallel^{-}C),(S::a)::x\rightarrow\maltese_{0}

(by definition)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\forall a\in A,\forall x\in(B\parallel^{-}C),S::(a\parallel x)\rightarrow\maltese_{0}

(by
48
)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle S\perp A\parallel^{-}(B\parallel^{-}C)

(by definition).

To prove the equality
((A\parallel B)\parallel C)^{\bot}=((A\parallel^{-}B)\parallel^{-}C)^{\bot}
, we need to express the (obvious) fact that, given three nets
S,T,U
such that
\#S=\#T+\#U
, once the list of
S
’s conclusions that one decides to cut with the list of conclusions of
T
(resp.
U
) is chosen, the interaction between
S
on the one hand and
T
and
U
on the other hand is uniquely determined. With the conventions of definition
41
, when we write
(S::T)::U
we cut the first part (“the head”) of the sequence
a(S)
with
a(T)
and the last part (“the tail”) of the sequence
a(S)
with
a(U)
. We denote by
ex_{U}(S)
the net
S
where we have swapped the tail and the head of
a(S)
: the previous fact can then be expressed by the equality
(S::T)::U=(ex_{U}(S)::U)::T
.

Like for the previous equality, we prove that, for every net
S
, the following equivalence holds:
S\perp(A\parallel B)\parallel C\iff S\perp(A\parallel^{-}B)\parallel^{-}C
. Indeed:

\displaystyle S\perp(A\parallel B)\parallel C

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle S\perp(A\parallel B)\parallel^{-}C

(by property
1
)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\forall x\in A\parallel B,\forall c\in C,S::(x\parallel c)\rightarrow\maltese_{0}

(by definition)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\forall x\in A\parallel B,\forall c\in C,(S::x)::c\rightarrow\maltese_{0}

(by
48
)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\forall x\in A\parallel B,\forall c\in C,(ex_{c}(S)::c)::x\rightarrow\maltese_{0}

(by the obvious fact above)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\forall c\in C,ex_{c}(S)::c\in(A\parallel B)^{\bot}

(by definition)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\forall c\in C,ex_{c}(S)::c\in(A\parallel^{-}B)^{\bot}

(by property
1
)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\forall a\in A,\forall b\in B,\forall c\in C,(ex_{c}(S)::c)::(a\parallel b)\rightarrow\maltese_{0}

(by definition)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\forall a\in A,\forall b\in B,\forall c\in C,((ex_{c}(S)::c)::a)::b\rightarrow\maltese_{0}

(by
48
)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\forall a\in A,\forall b\in B,\forall c\in C,((S::a)::b)::c\rightarrow\maltese_{0}

(by the obvious fact above)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\forall a\in A,\forall b\in B,\forall c\in C,(S::(a\parallel b))::c\rightarrow\maltese_{0}

(by
48
)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\forall a\in A,\forall b\in B,\forall c\in C,S::((a\parallel b)\parallel c)\rightarrow\maltese_{0}

(by
48
)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle S\perp(A\parallel^{-}B)\parallel^{-}C

(by definition).

To prove the last equality
(A\parallel^{-}(B^{-}\parallel C^{-}))^{\bot}=((A\parallel^{-}B)\parallel^{-}C)^{\bot}
, we proceed like before and we show that, for every net
S
, the following equivalence holds:
S\perp A\parallel^{-}(B^{-}\parallel C^{-})\iff S\perp(A\parallel^{-}B)\parallel^{-}C
. Indeed:

\displaystyle S\perp A\parallel^{-}(B^{-}\parallel C^{-})

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\forall a\in A,\forall b\in B,\forall c\in C,S::(a\parallel(b\parallel c))\rightarrow\maltese_{0}

(by definition)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\forall a\in A,\forall b\in B,\forall c\in C,(S::a)::(b\parallel c)\rightarrow\maltese_{0}

(by
48
)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\forall a\in A,\forall b\in B,\forall c\in C,((S::a)::b)::c\rightarrow\maltese_{0}

(by
48
)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\forall a\in A,\forall b\in B,\forall c\in C,(S::(a\parallel b))::c\rightarrow\maltese_{0}

(by property
2
)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow
