\displaystyle\forall a\in A,\forall b\in B,\forall c\in C,S::((a\parallel b)\parallel c)\rightarrow\maltese_{0}

(by
48
)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle S\perp(A\parallel^{-}B)\parallel^{-}C

(by definition).

∎

Appendix E
Complements to
section 4

E.1
Proving
59

Proposition 135

(Constructions preserve inclusion)
.

Consider four types

\mathbf{A}_{0}\subseteq\mathbf{A}

and
\mathbf{B}_{0}\subseteq\mathbf{B}

then:

1.

\mathbf{A}_{0}\parallel\mathbf{B}_{0}\subseteq\mathbf{A}\parallel\mathbf{B}

2.

\mathbf{A}_{0}\Yright\mathbf{B}_{0}\subseteq\mathbf{A}\Yright\mathbf{B}

3.

\mathbf{A}_{0}\otimes\mathbf{B}_{0}\subseteq\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B}

4.

\mathbf{A}_{0}\invamp\mathbf{B}_{0}\subseteq\mathbf{A}\invamp\mathbf{B}

Proof.

We treat each point independently.

1.

Consider
x
an element of

\mathbf{A}_{0}\parallel^{-}\mathbf{B}_{0}

then
x
is of the form

a_{0}\parallel b_{0}

with
a_{0}\in\mathbf{A}_{0}

and
b_{0}\in\mathbf{B}_{0}
.
Because we have the inclusion

\mathbf{A}_{0}\subseteq\mathbf{A}

and
\mathbf{B}_{0}\subseteq\mathbf{B}

it follow then that

x=a_{0}\parallel b_{0}

belongs to
\mathbf{A}\parallel^{-}\mathbf{B}

and thus to
\mathbf{A}\parallel\mathbf{B}
.

As a consequence

\mathbf{A}_{0}\parallel^{-}\mathbf{B}_{0}\subseteq\mathbf{A}\parallel\mathbf{B}

thus, because bi orthogonality preserves inclusion,
it follows that

(\mathbf{A}_{0}\parallel^{-}\mathbf{B}_{0})^{{}^{\bot\bot}}\subseteq(\mathbf{A}\parallel\mathbf{B})^{{}^{\bot\bot}}

therefore

\mathbf{A}_{0}\parallel\mathbf{B}_{0}\subseteq\mathbf{A}\parallel\mathbf{B}

by density (
50
)

2.

If
\mathbf{A}_{0}\subseteq\mathbf{A}

and
\mathbf{B}_{0}\subseteq\mathbf{B}

we equivalently have the inclusions

\mathbf{A}_{0}^{\bot}\supseteq\mathbf{A}^{\bot}

and
\mathbf{B}_{0}^{\bot}\supseteq\mathbf{B}^{\bot}
.
Using the previous demonstrated fact
it follows that

\mathbf{A}_{0}^{\bot}\parallel\mathbf{B}_{0}^{\bot}

contains
\mathbf{A}^{\bot}\parallel\mathbf{B}^{\bot}
.
Again using the fact that orthoganility invert inclusions
we then derive that

(\mathbf{A}_{0}^{\bot}\parallel\mathbf{B}_{0}^{\bot})^{\bot}

is included in
(\mathbf{A}^{\bot}\parallel\mathbf{B}^{\bot})^{\bot}
.
By duality
this means

\mathbf{A}_{0}\Yright\mathbf{B}_{0}\subseteq\mathbf{A}\Yright\mathbf{B}
(
51
).

3.

In the
\otimes
–case
we reason similarly to the
\parallel
case.

4.

For the
\invamp
–case
we can reason by duality
using the
\otimes
–case.

∎

Proposition 136

(
59
)
.

Given a formula
A

and a basis
\mathcal{B}
,
we have
\llbracket A^{\bot}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}\subseteq\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}
.

Proof.

By induction on the formula.
If
A=X
is an atomic formula this is trivial.
If
A=B\otimes C

then:

\displaystyle\llbracket(B\otimes C)^{\bot}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

\displaystyle=\llbracket B^{\bot}\invamp C^{\bot}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

\displaystyle=\quad\llbracket B^{\bot}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}\invamp\llbracket C^{\bot}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

\displaystyle\subseteq\quad\llbracket B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}\invamp\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}

\displaystyle(\mbox{\autoref{prop:inclusionPreserve} and induction hypothesis})

\displaystyle=\quad(\llbracket B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}\otimes\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}})^{\bot}

\displaystyle(\mbox{\autoref{prop:dualC}})

\displaystyle=\quad(\llbracket B\otimes C\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}})^{\bot}

\displaystyle(\mbox{\autoref{prop:dualC}})

Similarly, if
A=B\invamp C
:

\displaystyle\llbracket(B\invamp C)^{\bot}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

\displaystyle=\llbracket B^{\bot}\otimes C^{\bot}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

\displaystyle=\quad\llbracket B^{\bot}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}\otimes\llbracket C^{\bot}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

\displaystyle\subseteq\quad\llbracket B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}\otimes\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}

\displaystyle(\mbox{\autoref{prop:inclusionPreserve} and induction hypothesis})

\displaystyle=\quad(\llbracket B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}\invamp\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}})^{\bot}

\displaystyle(\mbox{\autoref{prop:dualC}})

\displaystyle=\quad(\llbracket B\invamp C\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}})^{\bot}

\displaystyle(\mbox{\autoref{prop:dualC}})

∎

E.2
On approximable basis

Remark 137
.

Given two nets
S
and
T

the net

S::T
is equal to the net
T::S
,
that is, up to ismorphism of the cut links
mapping each
\mathopen{\langle}S(i),T(i)\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

to a cut link
\mathopen{\langle}T(i),S(i)\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}
.

Proposition 138
.

Given
\mathcal{B}
an approximable basis
for any formula
A
of
\mathsf{MLL}

the type
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

is approximable.

Proof.

By induction on
A
.
In the case where
A
is atomic this follows from the definition.

•

For a formula
A\otimes B
.
Let us show
\maltese_{1}
belongs to
\llbracket A\otimes B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

by showing that

\maltese_{1}

is orthogonal to
\llbracket A\otimes B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}
.

The interpretation
\llbracket A\otimes B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}

is equal to
(\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}\otimes\llbracket B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}})^{\bot}

and so to
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}\invamp\llbracket B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}
.
To be orthogonal to
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}\invamp\llbracket B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}

is to be orthogonal
to the nets
S+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},p_{2}\vartriangleright_{\invamp}p\mathclose{\rangle}

where
S
belongs to
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}\Yright\llbracket B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}
.
Consider
S
such a net, then:

\displaystyle\maltese_{1}::S+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},p_{2}\vartriangleright_{\invamp}p\mathclose{\rangle}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\quad

\displaystyle\maltese_{1}\parallel\maltese_{1}::S

(
22
)

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle S::(\maltese_{1}\parallel\maltese_{1})

(
137
)

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle(S::\maltese_{1})::\maltese_{1}

(
48
)

By induction

\maltese_{1}
belongs to
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot\bot}

and
S
belongs to
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}\Yright\llbracket B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}

we conclude that

S::\maltese_{1}
belongs to
\llbracket B^{\bot}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}
.
Again by induction

\maltese_{1}
belongs to

\llbracket B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{{}^{\bot\bot}}

and so in particular it belongs to
in
\llbracket B^{\bot}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}
.
To conclude:
we have shown that

S::\maltese_{1}
belongs to
\llbracket B^{\bot}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}
,
hence it is orthogonal to
\llbracket B^{\bot}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}

so in particular
it is orthogonal to
\maltese_{1}
.
As a consequence
S::\maltese_{1}::\maltese_{1}

reduces to
\maltese_{0}
.

•

Now consider a formula of the form
A\invamp B
.
Again let us show that

\maltese_{1}

is orthogonal to
\llbracket A\invamp B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}

i.e. to
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}\otimes\llbracket B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}
.
To be orthogonal to that type
is to be orthogonal to the nets

\overline{a}\parallel\overline{b}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},p_{2}\vartriangleright_{\otimes}p\mathclose{\rangle}
.
Consider such a net, then:

\displaystyle\maltese_{1}::\overline{a}\parallel\overline{b}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},p_{2}\vartriangleright_{\otimes}p\mathclose{\rangle}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\quad

\displaystyle\maltese_{2}::\overline{a}\parallel\overline{b}

(
22
)

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle\maltese_{1}\bowtie\maltese_{1}::\overline{a}\parallel\overline{b}

(
122
)

By induction both

\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}
and
\llbracket B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}
contains
\maltese_{1}

thus
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}\Yright\llbracket B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

contains
\maltese_{2}
(
178
).
Equivalently this means that
\maltese_{2}

is orthogonal to
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}\parallel\llbracket B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}
,
hence
\maltese_{2}\perp\overline{a}\parallel\overline{b}
.
This concludes to show that
\maltese_{1}
belongs to

\llbracket A\invamp B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}
.

∎

E.3
Adequacy – Proof of
Theorem 64

See
64

Proof.

We proceed by induction
on the proof
\pi

which is represented by the net
S
.

Base case.

In the base the proof
\pi
consists of
a proof tree using a single
\maltese
–rule introducing
any sequent
\Gamma=A_{1},\dots,A_{n}
of length
n
.
Then any net
S
representing
\pi

is isomorphic to
\maltese_{n}
,
we must therefore show that
\maltese_{n}

belongs to
\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}
.

Because

\mathcal{B}
is approximable
any interpretation
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

is approximable (
138
): for every
1\leq i\leq n
we have
\maltese_{1}\in\llbracket A_{i}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}
i.e.
u\bot\maltese_{1}
for every
u\in\llbracket A_{i}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}
.
We want to show that

\maltese_{n}\in\llbracket A_{1},\dots,A_{n}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

or equivalently that

\maltese_{n}
is orthogonal to
\llbracket A_{1},\dots,A_{n}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}=(\llbracket A_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}\Yright\ldots\Yright\llbracket A_{n}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}})^{\bot}=\llbracket A_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}\parallel\ldots\parallel\llbracket A_{n}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}
.
By density (Remark
50
) to prove
\maltese_{n}\bot\ \llbracket A_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}\parallel\ldots\parallel\llbracket A_{n}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}
it is enough to prove that
u_{1}\parallel\dots\parallel u_{n}\bot\maltese_{n}

for every
u_{i}\in\llbracket A_{i}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}
. We proceed by induction on
n
:

\displaystyle\maltese_{n}::(u_{1}\parallel\dots\parallel u_{n})

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle(\ldots(\maltese_{n}::u_{1})::\dots)::u_{n}

\displaystyle(\mbox{\autoref{prop:interAsAction}})

\displaystyle\rightarrow^{*}\quad

\displaystyle(\ldots(\maltese_{n-1}::u_{2})::\dots)::u_{n}

\displaystyle(\mbox{\autoref{prop:addConcluReduction} and $u_{1}\bot\maltese_{1}$})

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle\maltese_{n-1}::(u_{2}\parallel\dots\parallel u_{n})

\displaystyle(\mbox{\autoref{prop:interAsAction}})

\displaystyle\rightarrow^{*}\quad

\displaystyle\maltese_{0}

\displaystyle(\mbox{Induction hypothesis})

As a consequence

\maltese_{n}

is contained in the type

\llbracket A_{1},\dots,A_{n}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}
,
i.e.
\maltese_{n}

realises the sequent
\Gamma
.

To conclude observe that
any proof
\pi
of conclusion
\Gamma=A_{1},\dots,A_{n}

which uses only one inference rule
in
\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}
, must use a daimon inference rule.
Then any such proof
\pi

is mapped by
\llbracket\pi\rrbracket

to the daimon link
with
n
outputs
\maltese_{n}
.
Hence we have showed the base case of the induction,
where the induction is
performed on the size (its number of inference rules) of the proof
\pi
.

Inductive cases.

We look at the last rule of
\pi

which may be a
\otimes
,
\invamp

or
\mathsf{cut}
–rule:

ind
\cdot 1

Assume that the last rule in the
represented proof is a
\invamp
–rule with main conclusion
A\invamp B
.
Thus the sequent is of the form
\Gamma,A\invamp B
,
and by assumption

S\vdash_{\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}}\Gamma,A\invamp B
.
Say the conclusions of
S

are ordered as
q_{1},\dots,q_{n},p

then since
p
is given the type
A\invamp B
the net
S
is of the form

S^{\prime}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},p_{2}\vartriangleright_{\invamp}p\mathclose{\rangle}
where
S^{\prime}\vdash_{\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}}\Gamma,A,B
.

Calling the induction hypothesis we can deduce
S^{\prime}\Vdash_{\mathcal{B}}\Gamma,A,B
.
By definition, this menas that
for any
\gamma
in
\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}
,
we have

S^{\prime}::\gamma\Vdash_{\mathcal{B}}A,B
, and thus

S^{\prime}::\gamma+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},p_{2}\vartriangleright_{\invamp}p\mathclose{\rangle}\Vdash_{\mathcal{B}}A\invamp B
.

Furthermore interaction and sum commute (
121
)
hence,

S^{\prime}::\gamma+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},p_{2}\vartriangleright_{\invamp}p\mathclose{\rangle}=S^{\prime}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},p_{2}\vartriangleright_{\invamp}p\mathclose{\rangle}::\gamma
so that
S::\gamma\Vdash_{\mathcal{B}}A\invamp B
for any
\gamma\in\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}
:
this allows us to conclude that
S\Vdash_{\mathcal{B}}\Gamma,A\invamp B
.

ind
\cdot 2

Assume that the last rule in the
represented proof is a
\otimes
–rule with main conclusion
A\otimes B
.
Thus the sequent is of the form
\Gamma,\Delta,A\otimes B
,
and
S
is of the form
S_{1}\otimes S_{2}

where

S_{1}\vdash_{\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}}\Gamma,A

and

S_{2}\vdash_{\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}}\Delta,B
.

Calling the induction
hypothesis
we obtain

S_{1}\Vdash_{\mathcal{B}}\Gamma,A

and

S_{2}\Vdash_{\mathcal{B}}\Delta,B
.
Thus for any
\gamma\in\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}

and
\delta\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}

S_{1}::\gamma\Vdash_{\mathcal{B}}A

and

S_{2}::\delta\Vdash_{\mathcal{B}}B
.
In particular this means that
the tensor union of the two nets

S_{1}::\gamma+S_{2}::\delta+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},p_{2}\vartriangleright_{\otimes}p\mathclose{\rangle}

is in
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}\otimes\llbracket B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}=\llbracket A\otimes B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}
.

Note that

S_{1}::\gamma+S_{2}::\delta+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},p_{2}\vartriangleright_{\otimes}p\mathclose{\rangle}=S_{1}+S_{2}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},p_{2}\vartriangleright_{\otimes}p\mathclose{\rangle}::\gamma\parallel\delta
(
48
and
121
).
Thus
S_{1}+S_{2}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},p_{2}\vartriangleright_{\otimes}p\mathclose{\rangle}::\gamma\parallel\delta\Vdash_{\mathcal{B}}A\otimes B
.
Since this hold for any
\gamma\in\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}

and
\delta\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}

we conclude that

S_{1}+S_{2}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},p_{2}\vartriangleright_{\otimes}p\mathclose{\rangle}\Vdash_{\mathcal{B}}\Gamma,\Delta,A\otimes B

i.e.

S\Vdash_{\mathcal{B}}\Gamma,\Delta,A\otimes B
.

ind
\cdot 3

Assume that the last rule in
\pi

is a cut rule
between two subproofs
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}

of respective conclusions
\Gamma,A

and
\Delta,A^{\bot}
.
Then a net
S
which represents

\pi
is of the form

S=S_{1}+S_{2}+\mathopen{\langle}S_{1}(\#S_{1}),S_{2}(\#S_{2})\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

where

S_{1}
(resp.
S_{2}
)
is a representation of the proof
\pi_{1}
(resp.
\pi_{2}
).

Applying the induction hypothesis
we derive

S_{1}
belongs to
\llbracket\Gamma,A\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

and
S_{2}
belongs to
\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}
.
Hence for any
u\in\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}

and
v\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}

we have

S_{1}::u\in\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

and

S_{2}::v\in\llbracket A^{\bot}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}
.
Because

\llbracket A^{\bot}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}\subset\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}
(Remark
59
)
it follows that

(S_{1}::u)\bot(S_{2}::v)
.
Let us rewrite this net to conclude:

\displaystyle S_{1}::u\quad::\quad S_{2}::v

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle S_{1}::u\quad+\mathopen{\langle}(S_{1}::u)(1),(S_{2}::v)(1)\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\quad S_{2}::v

(
41
)

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle S_{1}::u\quad+\mathopen{\langle}S_{1}(\#S_{1}),S_{2}(\#S_{2})\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\quad S_{2}::v

(Identity)

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle(S_{1}+\mathopen{\langle}S_{1}(\#S_{1}),S_{2}(\#S_{2})\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle})::u+S_{2}::v

(
121
)

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle(S_{1}+\mathopen{\langle}S_{1}(\#S_{1}),S_{2}(\#S_{2})\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+S_{2})::u::v

(
121
)

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle S::u::v

\displaystyle(\mbox{Identify }S)

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle S::(u\parallel v)

\displaystyle(\mbox{\autoref{prop:interAsAction}})

This shows that
S

is orthogonal to any

u\parallel v

when
u\in\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}

and
v\in\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}
.
In other words

S
is orthogonal to

\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}\parallel^{-}\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}

and therefore to

\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}\parallel\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}
(
50
)
thus by duality it belongs to

\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}\Yright\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}
(
51
)
i.e.
by definition to

\llbracket\Gamma,\Delta\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}
.

∎

Appendix F
Complements to
section 5

F.1
On orthogonality of Paritions

F.1.1
Orthogonal Partitions and bijections

Definition 139

(image of a partition)
.

Given a partition
P=\{C_{1},\dots,C_{n}\}
of a set
X

and a function
f:X\rightarrow Y

the
image

of
P
by
f

is the set
\{f(C_{1}),\dots,f(C_{n})\}
,
it is denoted
f(P)
.

Proposition 140

(Bijections preserve partitions)
.

Given
f:X\rightarrow Y
a bijection between two sets.
For any partition
P
of
X

the image
f(P)
is a partition of
Y
.

Definition 141
.

An isomoprhisms

f:G\rightarrow H
between two undirected multigraph

G=(V_{G},E_{G},\mathsf{brd}_{G})

and
H=(V_{H},E_{H},\mathsf{brd}_{H})

is a pair of functions
(f_{V},f_{E})
such that:

•

f_{V}:V_{G}\rightarrow V_{H}
is a bijection.

•

f_{E}:E_{G}\rightarrow E_{H}
is a bijection.

•

For any edge
e
of
E_{G}

we have that
f_{V}(\mathsf{brd}_{G}{e})

equals
\mathsf{brd}_{H}{f(e)}
.

Proposition 142
.

Given
f:X\rightarrow Y
a bijection

P
and
Q
two partitions of
X
.
The assertions are equivalent:

•

The partitions
P
and
Q
are orthogonal.

•

The partitions
f(P)
and
f(Q)
are orthogonal.

Proof.

We show that the induced graph
by
P
and
Q

and by
f(P)
and
f(Q)
are isomorphic,
i.e. that
G_{1}::=\mathsf{G}(P,Q)
and
G_{2}::=\mathsf{G(f(P),f(Q))}

are isomorphic.
The bijection between the set of edges is given by
f:X\rightarrow Y

while the bijection between the vertex–sets

{V}_{G_{1}}
and
{V}_{G_{2}}

is given by the map
associating with a subset of
X
its image under
f

i.e.
F:\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}\rightarrow\{f(x_{1}),\dots,f(x_{n})\}
.

Finally, one can check that the
border function
are coherent: for
A\in P

the image
f(\mathsf{brd}_{\mathsf{G}(P,Q)}(A))

is
\mathsf{brd}_{\mathsf{G}(f(P),f(Q))}(f(A))
.
∎

F.1.2
Orthogonal Partitions and their representations as nets

Definition 143
.

A
natural partition
is a partition
of a subset of
\mathbb{N}
.
A natural partition of size
n

is a partition of the set of integers
\{1,\dots,n\}
.

Remark 144
.

Given a natural partition
P
of size
n
,
any partition to
Q
which is orthogonal to
P

is also a natural partition of size
n
.

Definition 145
.

A net
S=(\left|{S}\right|,\mathbf{a}(S))

containing only daimon links

represents a natural partition

P
of size
n

(which we denote
S\equiv P
)
whenever:

•

For each class
\{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}\}
in
P

there exists a daimon link

\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}S(i_{1}),\dots,S(i_{k})\mathclose{\rangle}
in
S
.

•

For each daimon link
\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}S(a_{1}),\dots,S(a_{m})\mathclose{\rangle}

contained in
S

the set of integers
\{a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\}

is a class of the partition
P
.

Remark 146
.

Given a natural partition
P
of size
n

there exists (infinitely) many nets
S

which represent
P
,
however all these nets are isomorphic.

Notation 147
.

Given a natural partition
P
of size
n

we abusively denote by
P^{\maltese}

a representation of
P
.

Proposition 148
.

Given
P
and
Q

two natural partition of size
n
,
and two nets
S
and
T

such that

S\equiv P
while
T\equiv Q
,
the assertions are equivalent:

•

The partitions
P
and
Q
are orthogonal.

•

The nets
S
and
T
are orthogonal.

Proof.

Observe that
\mathsf{G}(P,Q)
is acyclic and connected
if and only if
S::T
is acyclic and connected.
Then we can prove each implication:

1\Rightarrow 2.

Assuming that
\mathsf{G}(P,Q)

then
S::T
is acyclic and connected.
Because
S
and
T
contain only daimon links,

S::T
contains only glueing cuts.
Furthermore the elimination of glueing cuts preserve
acyclicity and connectedness
while
S::T
is a net without conclusion
(and cut–elimination preserve the conclusion of a net).
The only cut free, acyclic–connected
net with no conclusions is
\maltese_{0}

thus we conclude
S::T\rightarrow\maltese_{0}

meaning that
S
and
T
are orthogonal.

2\Rightarrow 2.

Assume that
S::T\rightarrow\maltese_{0}

note that the reduction step of glueing cuts
preserve acyclicity and connectedness

in both directions
,
as a consequence since
S::T
reduces to
\maltese_{0}

by eliminating only glueing cuts
and since
\maltese_{0}
is acyclic and connected
we conclude that
S::T
is also acyclic and connected.
This means equivalently that
\mathsf{G}(P,Q)
is acyclic and connected.

∎

F.2
Proving
75

F.2.1
Elimination of Multiplicative cuts and addresses

Definition 149
.

An
address
is a sequence
of elements of
\{l,r\}
.
The element at position
l
(resp.
r
)
relatively to
p
in a net
S

is as follows:

\mathsf{Find}(p;l)

\triangleq

\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}p_{1}\mbox{ if }p\in\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},p_{2}\vartriangleright_{\square}p\mathclose{\rangle}\mbox{ belongs to }S\\
\mbox{undefined otherwise.}\end{array}\right.
.

\mathsf{Find}(p;r)

\triangleq

\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}p_{2}\mbox{ if }p\in\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},p_{2}\vartriangleright_{\square}p\mathclose{\rangle}\mbox{ belongs to }S\\
\mbox{undefined otherwise.}\end{array}\right.
.

The element at address
\xi

relatively to
p
in a net
S

is defined inductively:

\mathsf{Find}(p;\epsilon)=p\quad;\quad\mathsf{Find}(p;l\cdot\xi)=\mathsf{Find}(\mathsf{Find}(p;l);\xi)\quad;\quad\mathsf{Find}(p;r\cdot\xi)=\mathsf{Find}(\mathsf{Find}(p;r);\xi)

Remark 150
.

For each position
p

of a net
S=(\left|{S}\right|,\mathbf{a}(S))

there exists an address
\xi

and an integer
i

so that

p=\mathsf{Find}(S(i);\xi)

(This can be shown by induction on the number of links in
S
).
For a position

p
we denote that index
i

by
\mathsf{root}(p)

and
\xi
by
\mathsf{adr}(p)
.

Remark 151
.

Adresses are ordered
in the following way:
we set
l\leq r

and lift to adresses using the lexicographical order.

Definition 152

(cut–free initial order)
.

Given a cut–free net
S=(\left|{S}\right|,\mathbf{a}(S))

we can order the initial positions of
S
:
A position
p
is smaller than
q

if

\mathsf{root}(p)<\mathsf{root}(q)

or if

\mathsf{root}(p)=\mathsf{root}(q)

while
\mathsf{adr}(p)\leq\mathsf{adr}(q)

(for the lexicographical order).
This induced order is denoted
\leq^{\mathbf{a}(S)}
.

Given a conclusion
p
of a net
S

an address
\xi
is
defined
for
p

is
\mathsf{Find}(p;\xi)
is defined in
S
.
An address is
maximal
for a position
p

if
\xi
is defined for
p

and no address that is defined for
p

has
\xi
for strict prefix.
We denote
\mathsf{max}(p)

the maximal addresses of a position
p
.

Proposition 153
.

Given a net
S
,
p
a conclusion of
S

and
\xi
an address.
The assertions are equivalent:

•

\xi
is maximal for
p
.

•

\mathsf{Find}(p;\xi)
is an initial position of
S
.

Corollary 154
.

Given a net
S=(\left|{S}\right|,\mathbf{a}(S))

the set of initial positions of
S

is equal to

\bigcup_{1\leq i\leq\#S}\mathsf{Find}(S(i);\mathsf{max}(S(i))).

Notation 155
.

Given a position
p
in a net
S

we denote

\mathsf{link}_{S}(p)

the set of connective links above
p

in
S
.

Given an (unordered) net

S=(V,E,\mathrm{s},\mathrm{t})
and a set of link
E_{0}
of
S

we denote
by
S\setminus E_{0}
the net

(V\cap\bigcup_{e\in E\setminus E_{0}}\mathrm{t}(e)\cup\mathrm{s}(e),E\setminus E_{0},\mathrm{s},\mathrm{t})
.

Proposition 156
.

Given
S
and
T

two nets such that

S\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.62222pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}A_{1},\dots,A_{n}

while
T\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.62222pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}A_{1}^{\bot},\dots,A_{n}^{\bot}

then by eliminating only multiplicative cuts
the interaction
S::T
reduces
to

S^{\maltese}+T^{\maltese}+\sum_{1\leq i\leq n}\sum_{\xi\in\{l,r\}^{N_{i}}}\mathopen{\langle}\mathsf{Find}(S(i);\xi)_{S},\mathsf{Find}(T(i);\xi)_{T}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

Proof.

Assume that

S
and
T
contain connective links
(i.e.
\otimes
– and
\invamp
–links)
and more precisely,
without loss of generality assume that their first conclusion
is the output of a connective link.
As a consequence
since
S\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.50003pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}A_{1},\dots,A_{n}
and
T\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.50003pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}A_{1}^{\bot},\dots,A_{n}^{\bot}
witness dual sequents
it follows that
A_{1}=B_{1}\otimes B_{2}

while
A_{1}^{\bot}=B_{1}\invamp B_{2}
.

First note that

S::T

is equal to

S+T+\sum_{1\leq i\leq n}\mathopen{\langle}S(i),T(i)\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}=S+T+\mathopen{\langle}S(1),T(1)\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\sum_{2\leq i\leq n}\mathopen{\langle}S(i),T(i)\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

Furthermore since

A_{1}=B_{1}\otimes B_{2}

while
A_{1}^{\bot}=B_{1}\invamp B_{2}

we can ensure that
a link
\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},p_{2}\vartriangleright_{\otimes}S(1)\mathclose{\rangle}

occurs in
S
and is terminal
while a link

\mathopen{\langle}q_{1},q_{2}\vartriangleright_{\invamp}S(1)\mathclose{\rangle}

occurs in
T
and is terminal.

As a consequence
\mathopen{\langle}S(1),T(1)\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

is a multiplicative cut in
S::T

and its elimination produces the following reduction:

\displaystyle S+T+\mathopen{\langle}S(1),T(1)\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\;

\displaystyle S\setminus\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},p_{2}\vartriangleright_{\otimes}S(1)\mathclose{\rangle}+T\setminus\mathopen{\langle}q_{1},q_{2}\vartriangleright_{\invamp}T(1)\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},q_{1}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{2},q_{2}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle S\setminus\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},p_{2}\vartriangleright_{\otimes}S(1)\mathclose{\rangle}+T\setminus\mathopen{\langle}q_{1},q_{2}\vartriangleright_{\invamp}T(1)\mathclose{\rangle}

\displaystyle\quad+\mathopen{\langle}\mathsf{Find}(S(1);l),\mathsf{Find}(T(1);l)\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}\mathsf{Find}(S(1);r),\mathsf{Find}(T(1);r)\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

By contextual closure of the cut elimination procedure
we derive the following reduction:

\displaystyle S::T

\displaystyle=S+T+\sum_{1\leq i\leq n}\mathopen{\langle}S(i),T(i)\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

\displaystyle=S+T+\mathopen{\langle}S(1),T(1)\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\sum_{2\leq i\leq n}\mathopen{\langle}S(i),T(i)\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

\displaystyle\rightarrow S\setminus\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},p_{2}\vartriangleright_{\otimes}S(1)\mathclose{\rangle}+T\setminus\mathopen{\langle}q_{1},q_{2}\vartriangleright_{\invamp}T(1)\mathclose{\rangle}

\displaystyle\quad+\mathopen{\langle}\mathsf{Find}(S(1);l),\mathsf{Find}(T(1);l)\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}\mathsf{Find}(S(1);r),\mathsf{Find}(T(1);r)\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\sum_{2\leq i\leq n}\mathopen{\langle}S(i),T(i)\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

Now consider the two nets

S_{0}=(S\setminus\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},p_{2}\vartriangleright_{\otimes}S(1)\mathclose{\rangle},\mathsf{Find}(S(1);l)\cdot\mathsf{Find}(S(1);r)\cdot\mathbf{a}(S))

and

T_{0}=(T\setminus\mathopen{\langle}q_{1},q_{2}\vartriangleright_{\invamp}T(1)\mathclose{\rangle},\mathsf{Find}(T(1);l)\cdot\mathsf{Find}(T(1);r)\cdot\mathbf{a}(2))
.
Observe that
because

S\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.50003pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}A_{1},\dots,A_{n}

it follows that

S_{0}\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.50003pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}B_{1},B_{2},A_{2},\dots,A_{n}

while because

T\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.50003pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}A_{1}^{\bot},\dots,A_{n}^{\bot}

it follows that

T_{0}\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.50003pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}B_{1}^{\bot},B_{2}^{\bot},A_{2}^{\bot},\dots,A_{n}^{\bot}
.
Because
S_{0}
and
T_{0}
have less connective links than
S
and
T

we can apply the induction hypothesis.
Furthermore we observe that

\displaystyle S\setminus\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},p_{2}\vartriangleright_{\otimes}S(1)\mathclose{\rangle}+T\setminus\mathopen{\langle}q_{1},q_{2}\vartriangleright_{\invamp}T(1)\mathclose{\rangle}

\displaystyle\quad+\mathopen{\langle}\mathsf{Find}(S(1);l),\mathsf{Find}(T(1);l)\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}\mathsf{Find}(S(1);r),\mathsf{Find}(T(1);r)\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\sum_{2\leq i\leq n}\mathopen{\langle}S(i),T(i)\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle S_{0}+T_{0}+\mathopen{\langle}\mathsf{Find}(S(1);l),\mathsf{Find}(T(1);l)\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}\mathsf{Find}(S(1);r),\mathsf{Find}(T(1);r)\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\sum_{2\leq i\leq n}\mathopen{\langle}S(i),T(i)\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle S_{0}+T_{0}+\mathopen{\langle}S_{0}(1),T_{0}(1)\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}S_{0}(2),T_{0}(2)\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\sum_{2\leq i\leq n}\mathopen{\langle}S(i),T(i)\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle S_{0}+T_{0}+\mathopen{\langle}S_{0}(1),T_{0}(1)\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}S_{0}(2),T_{0}(2)\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\sum_{3\leq i\leq n+1}\mathopen{\langle}{S_{0}}(i),{T_{0}}(i)\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle S_{0}+T_{0}+\sum_{1\leq i\leq n+1}\mathopen{\langle}{S_{0}}(i),{T_{0}}(i)\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle S_{0}::T_{0}

By calling the induction hypothesis on
S_{0}::T_{0}

we derive the reduction:

S_{0}::T_{0}\rightarrow^{*}S_{0}^{\maltese}+T_{0}^{\maltese}+\sum_{1\leq i\leq\#S_{0}}\sum_{\xi\in\mathsf{max}(S_{0}(i))}\mathopen{\langle}\mathsf{Find}(S_{0}(i);\xi)_{S},\mathsf{Find}(T_{0}(i);\xi)_{T}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

First observe

S_{0}^{\maltese}=S^{\maltese}

and
T_{0}^{\maltese}=T^{\maltese}
.
Now note that
any for any
3\leq i\leq\#S_{0}

we have
S_{0}(i)=S(i-1)

while
T_{0}(i)=T(i-1)

thus the sum of cut links can be rewritten:

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq i\leq\#S_{0}}\sum_{\xi\in\mathsf{max}(S_{0}(i))}\mathopen{\langle}\mathsf{Find}(S_{0}(i);\xi)_{S_{0}},\mathsf{Find}(T_{0}(i);\xi)_{T_{0}}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq i\leq 2}\sum_{\xi\in\mathsf{max}(S_{0}(i))}\mathopen{\langle}\mathsf{Find}(S_{0}(i);\xi)_{S_{0}},\mathsf{Find}(T_{0}(i);\xi)_{T_{0}}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\sum_{3\leq i\leq\#S_{0}}\sum_{\xi\in\mathsf{max}(S_{0}(i))}\mathopen{\langle}\mathsf{Find}(S_{0}(i);\xi)_{S_{0}},\mathsf{Find}(T_{0}(i);\xi)_{T_{0}}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq i\leq 2}\sum_{\xi\in\mathsf{max}(S_{0}(i))}\mathopen{\langle}\mathsf{Find}(S_{0}(i);\xi)_{S_{0}},\mathsf{Find}(T_{0}(i);\xi)_{T_{0}}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\sum_{3\leq i\leq\#S_{0}}\sum_{\xi\in\mathsf{max}(S(i-1))}\mathopen{\langle}\mathsf{Find}(S(i-1);\xi)_{S},\mathsf{Find}(T(i-1);\xi)_{T}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq i\leq 2}\sum_{\xi\in\mathsf{max}(S_{0}(i))}\mathopen{\langle}\mathsf{Find}(S_{0}(i);\xi)_{S_{0}},\mathsf{Find}(T_{0}(i);\xi)_{T_{0}}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\sum_{2\leq i\leq\#S}\sum_{\xi\in\mathsf{max}(S(i))}\mathopen{\langle}\mathsf{Find}(S(i);\xi)_{S},\mathsf{Find}(T(i);\xi)_{T}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

To conclude
observe that

\mathsf{max}(S_{0}(i))

is equal
to
\{s\mid\exists\mathsf{i}\in\{l,r\}\mathsf{i}\cdot s\in\mathsf{max}(S(i))\}

Furthermore

\mathsf{Find}(T(1);l)

is equal to

T_{0}(1)
.
Thus we derive the following:

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq i\leq 2}\;\;\sum_{\xi\in\mathsf{max}(S_{0}(i))}\mathopen{\langle}\mathsf{Find}(S_{0}(i);\xi)_{S_{0}},\mathsf{Find}(T_{0}(i);\xi)_{T_{0}}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathsf{i}\in\{l,r\}}\;\;\sum_{\xi\in\mathsf{max}(\mathsf{Find}(S(1);\mathsf{i})_{S})}\mathopen{\langle}\mathsf{Find}(X;\xi)_{S_{0}},\mathsf{Find}(X;\xi)_{T_{0}}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathsf{i}\in\{l,r\}}\;\;\sum_{\xi\in\mathsf{max}(\mathsf{Find}(S(1);\mathsf{i})_{S})}\mathopen{\langle}\mathsf{Find}(S(1);\mathsf{i}\xi)_{S},\mathsf{Find}(T(1);\mathsf{i}\xi)_{T}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\xi\in\mathsf{max}(S(1))}\mathopen{\langle}\mathsf{Find}(S(1);\xi)_{S},\mathsf{Find}(T(1);\xi)_{T}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

Putting all together the sum of cut links can be written as:

\displaystyle\sum_{\xi\in\mathsf{max}(S(1))}\mathopen{\langle}\mathsf{Find}(S(1);\xi)_{S},\mathsf{Find}(T(1);\xi)_{T}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}+\sum_{2\leq i\leq\#S}\;\;\sum_{\xi\in\mathsf{max}(S(i))}\mathopen{\langle}\mathsf{Find}(S(i);\xi)_{S},\mathsf{Find}(T(i);\xi)_{T}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq i\leq\#S}\;\;\sum_{\xi\in\mathsf{max}(S(i))}\mathopen{\langle}\mathsf{Find}(S(i);\xi)_{S},\mathsf{Find}(T(i);\xi)_{T}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

As a consequence we conclude that

S_{0}::T_{0}
reduces to

S^{\maltese}+T^{\maltese}+\sum_{1\leq i\leq\#S}\;\;\sum_{\xi\in\mathsf{max}(S(i))}\mathopen{\langle}\mathsf{Find}(S(i);\xi)_{S},\mathsf{Find}(T(i);\xi)_{T}\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

Finally, because
S::T\rightarrow S_{0}::T_{0}

we conclude.
∎

F.2.2
Proof of
75

Remark 157
.

Given a net
S=(\left|{S}\right|,\mathbf{a}(S))

the net induced by its daimon
S^{\maltese}

comes with the natural order induced
by
\mathbf{a}(S)
and the lexicographical order on address.
More specifically
given
p
and
q
two points of
S^{\maltese}

both can be written as
\mathsf{Find}(a;\xi)

where
a=\mathsf{root}(p)_{S}
is a conclusion of
S

while
\xi=\mathsf{adr}(p)_{S}
.
Then
p\leq q

if and only if
(\mathsf{root}(p),\mathsf{adr}(p))\leq(\mathsf{root}(q),\mathsf{adr}(q))

for the lexicographical order.

Proposition 158
.

Given two cut free nets
S

and
T

atomically testable respectively

A_{1},\dots,A_{n}

and
A_{1}^{\bot},\dots,A_{n}^{\bot}
.
The assertions are equivalent;

•

S
and
T
are orthogonal.

•

S^{\maltese}
and
T^{\maltese}
are orthogonal.

Proof.

Consider
S
and
T

two nets (atomically) testable by dual sequents
so that
\#S=\#T=n
.
The following reduction can be derived
(
156
):

S::T\rightarrow_{\mathtt{mult}}S^{\maltese}+T^{\maltese}+\sum_{1\leq i\leq n}\sum_{\xi\in\mathsf{max}(S(i))}\mathopen{\langle}\mathsf{Find}(S(i);\xi),\mathsf{Find}(T(i);\xi)\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

But observe
that the outputs of
S^{\maltese}

and
T^{\maltese}

are ordered according to the lexicographical order
on the pairs
(\mathsf{root}(p),\mathsf{adr}(p))
.
As a consequence two conclusion have the same index
if and only if
their respective roots are
S(i)
and
T(i)
for some
i

and their address is the same.
We derive the following

S^{\maltese}+T^{\maltese}+\sum_{1\leq i\leq n}\sum_{\xi\in\mathsf{max}(S(i))}\mathopen{\langle}\mathsf{Find}(S(i);\xi),\mathsf{Find}(T(i);\xi)\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}=S^{\maltese}+T^{\maltese}+\sum_{1\leq i\leq N}\mathopen{\langle}S^{\maltese}(i),T^{\maltese}(i)\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}

1\Rightarrow 2.

By assumptioni
S::T\rightarrow\maltese_{0}
,
because the multiplicative cuts
get eliminated and can always be performed first
due to the commutation result (
103
),
this ensures that
S::T\rightarrow S^{\maltese}::T^{\maltese}

and since
S::T\rightarrow\maltese_{0}

it follows that
S^{\maltese}::T^{\maltese}\rightarrow\maltese_{0}
.

2\Rightarrow 1.

We have shown with the previous equality that

S::T\rightarrow S^{\maltese}::T^{\maltese}

thus we immediately conclude.
∎

Proposition 159

(Orthogonality of nets and of natural partitions)
.

Given
S^{\maltese}
and
T^{\maltese}
two nets
made only of daimon links,
the assertions are equivalent:

1.

S^{\maltese}
and
T^{\maltese}
are orthogonal.

2.

\mathbf{Nat}(\mathsf{P}_{\maltese}(S))

and
\mathbf{Nat}(\mathsf{P}_{\maltese}(T))

are orthogonal.

Proof.

Assume that

S^{\maltese}::T^{\maltese}
reduces to
\maltese_{0}
.
In other words the following net reduces to
\maltese_{0}
:

S^{\maltese}+T^{\maltese}+\sum_{1\leq i\leq n}\mathopen{\langle}S^{\maltese}(i),T^{\maltese}(i)\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}.

Equivalently this means that

S^{\maltese}::T^{\maltese}
is an acyclic and connected graph.
In particular if we contract each cut link
\mathopen{\langle}S^{\maltese}(i),T^{\maltese}(i)\vartriangleright_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathclose{\rangle}
in a single point
that we call
i

the acyclicity and connectedness is preserved.
And in that case the resulting graph is

\mathsf{G}(\mathbf{Nat}(\mathsf{P}_{\maltese}(S)),\mathbf{Nat}(\mathsf{P}_{\maltese}(T)))

showning that these two partitions are orthogonal.
The same argument in the other direction hold.
∎

See
73

Proof.

This follows from the two previous proposition,

159

and
158
.

\mathbf{1\Leftrightarrow 2.}

As for
1\Rightarrow 2
,
we need to establish
a property of the rewriting of nets:
if
N\rightarrow_{\mathsf{mult}}N^{\prime}

and
N\smash{\xrightarrow{}}^{*}\maltese_{0}

then
N^{\prime}\smash{\xrightarrow{}}^{*}\maltese_{0}
.
As for
2\Rightarrow 1
,
it follows from

S::T\rightarrow^{*}S^{\maltese}::T^{\maltese}
.

\mathbf{2\Leftrightarrow 3.}

One first proves that

S^{\maltese}::T^{\maltese}

is acyclic and connected (
ACC
)
if and only if
\mathsf{G}(\mathbf{Nat}(\mathsf{P}_{\maltese}(S)),\mathbf{Nat}(\mathsf{P}_{\maltese}(T)))
is.

As for
3\Rightarrow 2
,
since no cycle occurs in
S^{\maltese}::T^{\maltese}

all (glueing) cuts are acyclic
and thus all cuts
can be eliminated,
furthermore the elimination of a glueing cut
preserves
ACC
.
The net
S^{\maltese}::T^{\maltese}
contains
no connective link, thus its normal form
does not contain clash cuts.
Hence because the
only net without conclusion that is
normal,
ACC
and contains no clash cuts
is
\maltese_{0}

we conclude

S^{\maltese}::T^{\maltese}\rightarrow\maltese_{0}
.
As for
2\Rightarrow 3
,
note that
anti–steps of cut elimination for glueing cuts
also preserve the
ACC
property
hence if
S^{\maltese}::T^{\maltese}\rightarrow\maltese_{0}

it must be that
S^{\maltese}::T^{\maltese}
is
ACC
.

∎

F.3
Description of tests

The object of this section
is to show that the
tests
of a formula
A

(
74
)
may be defined relatively to a single cut free net
S\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.50003pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}A
:
this is because the partitions
\uparrow^{i}\sigma S

for some switching of
S
,
only depend on the
\otimes
– and
\invamp
–link
of
S
,
which is the same for any cut free net
U\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.50003pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}A
.

We now fix a special propositional variable
that we denote
\mathbf{V}
.
A formula which contains only the
\mathbf{V}

propositional variable is called a
formula pattern
.

Definition 160
.

An hypergraph
M
which contains only
\otimes
– or
\invamp
–links represents
a formula pattern
F
, denoted
M\equiv_{\mathcal{F}}F
whenever:

•

If
F=\mathbf{V}
is the propositional variable
\mathbf{V}

then
M
consists of
(\{p\},\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset)

for some position
p
.

•

If
F=A\otimes B

then
M
is equal to

M_{A}+M_{B}+\mathopen{\langle}M_{A}(1),M_{B}(1)\vartriangleright_{\otimes}p\mathclose{\rangle}

where
p
is a fresh position
and
M_{A}\equiv_{\mathcal{F}}A

while
M_{B}\equiv_{\mathcal{F}}B
.

•

If
F=A\invamp B

then
M
is equal to

M_{A}+M_{B}+\mathopen{\langle}M_{A}(1),M_{B}(1)\vartriangleright_{\invamp}p\mathclose{\rangle}

where
p
is a fresh position
and
M_{A}\equiv_{\mathcal{F}}A

while
M_{B}\equiv_{\mathcal{F}}B
.

Proposition 161
.

Given an hypergraph
M

and a formula pattern
A
.
If
M\equiv_{\mathcal{F}}A

then
M
is target–surjective and source surjective,
and
M
has a single conclusion.

Proof.

By doing a simple induction on
A
.
∎

Notation 162
.

Given a formula pattern
A
and a position
p

we denote by
\underline{A}(p)

a hypergraph which represents
the formula
A
and has for conclusion
p
.
Furthermore we denote
by
A\langle p_{1},\dots,p_{n}\triangleright p\rangle

a hypergraph
M
of conclusion
p

representing
A

and such that

p_{1},\dots,p_{n}
are the
input positions
13
13

13

that is positions which are the target of no link in
M
.

of the hypergraph
ordered by lexicographical order on
\{l,r\}^{*}

for the addresses of the positions
i.e. their addresses
\mathsf{adr}(p_{i})
in
M
.

Lemma 163

(Decomposition Lemma)
.

Given a cut–free net
S

with
n
conclusion
there exists a unique sequence of
n
formula patterns
A_{1},\dots,A_{n}

such that:

S=S^{\maltese}+\sum_{1\leq i\leq n}\underline{A_{i}}(S(i)).

Proof.

One proceeds by induction
on the number of connective links of
S
.
∎

We prove that test of
A

can be defined relatively to any single net
S\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.50003pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}A
:
the next proposition
ensures that the test of a formula
may be described using any single net
S\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.50003pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}A
:
this is because the switchings only depend on the
\invamp
–
and
\otimes
–links
of
S
which are the same for all the nets
U\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.50003pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}A
.

Proposition 164
.

Given a formula
A
and a net
S\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.62222pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}A
.
For any net
T
,

T
is a test of
A

iff

T\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.62222pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}A^{\bot}

and for some switching
\sigma S
of
S

we have

\mathbf{Nat}_{T}(\mathsf{P}_{\maltese}(T))=\mathbf{Nat}_{S}(\uparrow^{i}\sigma S)
.

Proof.

2\Rightarrow 1.
It is the obvious direction.

1\Rightarrow 2.

S
is a cut–free net atomically testable by
A
,
consider any
U
which is also cut–free and atomically testable by
A
:
we show that for any switching
\sigma S
of
S

there exists a switching
\tau U
of
U
such that
the partitions

\mathbf{Nat}_{S}(\uparrow^{i}\sigma S)

and

\mathbf{Nat}_{U}(\uparrow^{i}\tau U)

are the same.

S
and
U
can be both written
as
S^{\maltese}+A\langle\vec{u}\triangleright p\rangle

and
U^{\maltese}+A\langle\vec{v}\triangleright q\rangle
.
The nets
S^{\maltese}

and
U^{\maltese}
are normal for the switching rewriting.
Observe that the sum of two normal nets for the switching rewriting
is still normal for the switching rewriting;
therefore
one can show that
S^{\maltese}+A\langle\vec{u}\triangleright p\rangle\rightarrow_{\invamp}S^{\prime}

implies
A\langle\vec{u}\triangleright p\rangle\rightarrow_{\invamp}R
(by showing the contraposition).
Indeed this will also be true for the net
U
.

Therefore we derive the following:

•

A switching of
S
is a net of the form

S^{\maltese}+R_{S}
where
R_{S}
is a normal form of
A\langle\vec{v}\triangleright q\rangle
.

•

Similarly, a switching of
U
is a net of the form

U^{\maltese}+R_{U}
where
R_{U}
is a normal form of
A\langle\vec{v}\triangleright q\rangle
.

•

Finally observe that two cut–free nets
U^{\maltese}+R=V^{\maltese}+R

where
R
is made of connective links only
then
\mathbf{Nat}_{U}(\uparrow^{i}U)=\mathbf{Nat}_{V}(\uparrow^{i}V)
.

Consider then any two switchings of
S
and
U
with the same reduct
R

and denote them respectively
\sigma S
and
\tau U
:
it follows that

\mathbf{Nat}_{S}(\uparrow^{i}\sigma S)

equals
\mathbf{Nat}_{T}(\uparrow^{i}\sigma T)
.

To conclude, fix the net
S\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.50003pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}A
.
A test
T
of
A
is such that

T\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.50003pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}A^{\bot}

and for some
U\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.50003pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}A
and some of its switching
\tau U

\mathbf{Nat}_{T}(\mathsf{P}_{\maltese}(T))=\mathbf{Nat}_{U}(\uparrow^{i}\tau U)
.
By the previous considerations
there exists a switching
\sigma S
of
S
such that

\mathbf{Nat}_{S}(\uparrow^{i}\sigma S)

equals
\mathbf{Nat}_{U}(\uparrow^{i}\tau U)

therefore

\mathbf{Nat}_{T}(\mathsf{P}_{\maltese}(T))=\mathbf{Nat}_{S}(\uparrow^{i}\sigma S)
:
this allows us to conclude.
∎

F.4
Orthogonality with tests – proof of
75

and
Theorem 76

Using the previous proposition and the well–known
result of Danos Regnier (
Theorem 40
)
we obtain the following proposition of
section 5
.
See
75

Proof.

Consider a test
T
of
\mathsf{tests}({A})

then
T\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.50003pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}A^{\bot}

and
\mathbf{Nat}_{T}(\mathsf{P}_{\maltese}(T))=\mathbf{Nat}_{S}(\uparrow^{i}\sigma S)

for some switching
\sigma S
of
S
(
164
).
By assumption
S
and
T
are orthogonal thus equivalently

\mathbf{Nat}_{S}(\mathsf{P}_{\maltese}(S))
and
\mathbf{Nat}_{T}(\mathsf{P}_{\maltese}(T))

are orthogonal (
73
).
Then

\mathbf{Nat}_{S}(\mathsf{P}_{\maltese}(S))
is orthogonal to
each
\mathbf{Nat}_{S}(\uparrow^{i}\sigma S)
.
we then
conclude that

\mathsf{P}_{\maltese}(S)
and
\uparrow^{i}\sigma S
are orthogonal.
Since this holds for any
switching
\sigma S
thus
calling theorem
40

we conclude that
S
is a proof net.
The other direction of the equivalence
is obtained using the same argument
in the opposite direction.
∎

We can indeed generalise this result the case where
S

has multiple conclusions,
as usual we do this by transformin
the net with multiple conclusions
in a net with one conclusion by adding a bunch of
\invamp
–links.

Definition 165

(General connectives)
.

A
generalised
\invamp
–link

on the positions
p_{1},\dots,p_{n}

is a module
denoted
\mathopen{\langle}p_{0},\dots,p_{n}\vartriangleright_{\invamp^{n}}p\mathclose{\rangle}

and defined by the following induction;

•

\mathopen{\langle}p_{0},p_{1}\vartriangleright_{\invamp^{1}}p\mathclose{\rangle}=\mathopen{\langle}p_{0},p_{1}\vartriangleright_{\invamp}p^{1}\mathclose{\rangle}
.

•

For any
n>0

we defined

\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},\dots,p_{n+2}\vartriangleright_{\invamp^{n+1}}p\mathclose{\rangle}=\mathopen{\langle}p^{n-1},p_{n}\vartriangleright_{\invamp^{n}}p\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p^{n},p_{n+1}\vartriangleright_{\invamp}p^{n+1}\mathclose{\rangle}
.

Similarly we defined
the generalised tensor links
\mathopen{\langle}p_{0},\dots,p_{n}\vartriangleright_{\otimes^{n}}p\mathclose{\rangle}
.

Proposition 166
.

Let
S
be a net with conclusions

p_{0},\dots,p_{n},q_{1},\dots,q_{k}
.
Let
S_{1},\dots,S_{n},T_{1},\dots,T_{k}

be
n
nets with one conclusion
the assertions are equivalent;

1.

S\perp S_{1}\parallel\dots\parallel S_{n}\parallel T_{1}\parallel\dots\parallel T_{k}
.

2.

S+\mathopen{\langle}p_{0},\dots,p_{n}\vartriangleright_{\invamp^{n}}p\mathclose{\rangle}\perp S_{1}+\dots+S_{n}+\mathopen{\langle}S_{1}(1)\dots,S_{n}(1)\vartriangleright_{\otimes^{n}}q\mathclose{\rangle}\parallel T_{1}\parallel\dots\parallel T_{k}

Proof.

By a simple induction
of the size of the generalised
\invamp
connective.
∎

To derive the generalised theorem
one must observe that the tests
of
\invamp
–formulas
are tensors of the tests of the subformulas.

Proposition 167
.

A test
T
of
A\invamp B

is of the form

T_{A}\parallel T_{B}+\mathopen{\langle}T_{A}(1),T_{B}(1)\vartriangleright_{\otimes}p\mathclose{\rangle}

where
p
a fresh position,

T_{A}
is a test of
A

and

T_{B}
is a test of
B
.

Proof.

This comes down to analysing the partitions
generated by a the switchings of a net
S\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.50003pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}A\invamp B

indeed this these
are partitions
P_{A}\cup P_{B}

where
P_{A}
is a partition of the representation of
A

and
P_{B}
is a partition of the representation of
B
.
∎

This easily leads to a proof of
the Danos Regnier theorem as stated in section 5.
See
76

F.5
Correctness of Tests and counter proofs – proof of
Theorem 77

See
77

We have established the previous theorem
using the counter proof criterion of P.L. Curien
found in
[
4
]
.
Let us clearly state the theorem of counter proofs.

Theorem 168

(Counter Proof Criterion)
.

Given a cut–free net
S\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.62222pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}A

the assertions are equivalent:

•

S
is orthogonal to each net
T\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.62222pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}A^{\bot}
representing a proof of
A^{\bot}
.

•

S\vdash_{\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}}A
.

Remark 169
.

The counter proof criterion
is formulated in
[
4
]

where non–homogeneous cut elimination does not exists.
Because this is not the case in our work
we add the hypothesis
that
S\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.50003pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}A

and the proof–opponents are of the form
T\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.50003pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}A^{\bot}

so that only homogeneous cut will appear and be eliminated.
Also observe that
a proof of
A^{\bot}
such that
T\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.50003pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}A^{\bot}

is an atomic proof of
\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}

that is,
a proof such that its daimon rules introduce
sequents which contains only propositional variables.

One can establish that
the fact that tests of
A

are proofs of
A^{\bot}

is equivalent to an implication of the counter proof criterion.

Proposition 170
.

Given a formula
A

the assertions are equivalent:

1.

Each test of
A

is the representation of a proof of
A^{\bot}
.

2.

For any cut–free net
S\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.62222pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}A

if
S
is orthogonal to each (cut–free)
T\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.62222pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}A^{\bot}
representing a proof of
A^{\bot}

then
S\vdash_{\mathsf{MLL}}^{\maltese}A
.

Proof.

The fact that
2\Rightarrow 1

is the proof of
Theorem 77

(it uses the counter proof criterion
[
4
]
,
Theorem 168
).
To show
1\Rightarrow 2
,
consider a net
S\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.50003pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}A

and assume that each test
t
of
A

represents a proof of
A^{\bot}

and is such that
t\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.50003pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}A^{\bot}
.
If the net
S
is orthogonal to each
net
T\vdash_{\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}}A^{\bot}

with
T\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.50003pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}A^{\bot}

then in particular it is orthogonal to
the set of tests
\mathsf{tests}({A})
:
it follows that
S\vdash_{\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}}A
(
Theorem 76
).
∎

In fact the result of adequacy we have obtained for
\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}

(
Theorem 64
)
allows to generalise the counter proof criterion of P.L. curien
[
4
]

in presence of non homogeneous cuts.
More precisely,
having an adequate basis implies
a “soundness” result of a counter proof criterion,
i.e. any proof of
A
is orthogonal to any proof of
A^{\bot}
.

Proposition 171
.

The first assertion implies the second:

1.

There exists an adequate basis.

2.

Any
S\vdash_{\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}}A

is orthogonal to any
T\vdash_{\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}}A^{\bot}
.

Proof.

1\Rightarrow 2.

Say
\mathcal{B}
is an adequate
basis then for any formula
A
we have

\lBrace A:\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}\rBrace\subseteq\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}
.
Now since
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}
equals
\llbracket A^{{}^{\bot\bot}}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

we derive
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}\subseteq\llbracket A^{\bot}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}
(
58
).
This means that any realiser of
A
is a orthogonal to any realiser of
A^{\bot}
,
since by adequacy a net
S\vdash_{\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}}A
realises
A

and a net
T\vdash_{\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}}A^{\bot}
realises
A^{\bot}
we conclude.
∎

Then we can obtain a general form of counter proof criterion
namely when we don’t have the restriction that
the proofs are atomic i.e.
S\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.50003pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}A
and
T\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.50003pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}A^{\bot}
.

Proposition 172

(Generalised Counter Proof criterion)
.

Given a formula
A
and
S
a cut–free net
the assertions are equivalent:

1.

S
is orthogonal to each net
T
representing a proof of
A^{\bot}
.

2.

S\vdash_{\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}}A
.

Proof.

1\Rightarrow 2.

Assume that
S\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.50003pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}A_{0}
with
A_{0}
such that there exists
\theta

with
\theta A_{0}=A

and that

S
is orthogonal to each proof nets
T\vdash_{\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}}A^{\bot}
.
In particular any net

T\vdash_{\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}}A_{0}^{\bot}

with
T\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.50003pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}A_{0}^{\bot}

is such that

T\vdash_{\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}}A^{\bot}

(
33
).

As a consequence:

S
is orthogonal to each
T\vdash_{\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}}A_{0}^{\bot}

with
T\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.50003pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}A_{0}^{\bot}
.
by the counter proof criterion
[
4
]

we derive
S\vdash_{\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}}A_{0}

and thus
S\vdash_{\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}}A
(again using
33
).

2\Rightarrow 1.

We aim at showing that
any proof
S\vdash_{\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}}A

is orthogonal to any proof
T\vdash_{\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}}A^{\bot}

which may have a different syntax tree (and here lies the novelty with respect to
P-L. Curien’s theorem
[
4
]
).
Showing this implication
by analyzing the rewriting rules of cut–elimination
can be delicate,
however
in the language of realisability this becomes a trivial consequence
of the adequacy theorem (
171
).
Hence because adequacy hold (
Theorem 64
), we conclude.
∎

F.6
On Substitutions and Testability – Proof of
82

Recall that a
substitution

is a map
\theta
which maps
propositional variables to formulas.
Naturally substitutions can be lifted
by induction to formulas and to sequents;

\theta(A\square B)\triangleq\theta A\square\theta B

and
\theta(\Gamma,A)=\theta(\Gamma),\theta A
.
A sequent
\Gamma
is an
instance

of a sequent
\Delta
whenever there exists
a substitution
\theta

such that
\theta\Delta=\Gamma
.
In that case we denote
\Delta\leq\Gamma
.

Remark 173
.

Given two representation of sequents

\Gamma=A_{1},\dots,A_{n}
and
\Delta=B_{1},\dots,B_{n}

are such that
\Delta\leq\Gamma

this implies
that for each index
1\leq i\leq n

we have
B_{i}\leq A_{i}

specifically

A_{i}[X_{1}\mapsto F_{1},\dots,X_{k}\mapsto F_{k}]=B_{i}
.

Remark 174
.

Whenever a cut–free net
S
is such that

S\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq}\Gamma

then there exists a substitution
\theta

and a sequent
\Delta

such that
S\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.50003pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}\Delta

and
\theta\Delta
.

Let us provide a proof of the previous remark.

Proposition 175
.

Given a cut–free net
S
and a sequent
\Gamma
.
If
S\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq}\Gamma

then there exists a sequent
\Delta

and a substitution
\theta

such that
\theta\Delta=\Gamma

and
S\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.62222pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}\Delta
.

Proof.

If
S\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.50003pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}\Gamma
we can calready conclude.
Otherwise
S\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq}\Gamma

and let us denote
p_{1},\dots,p_{n}
the initial positions of
S
,
and
\tau
the formula labelling witnessing
S\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq}\Gamma
.
Because formula labellings are total functions
each
p_{i}
as a formula
\tau(p_{i})=F_{i}
associated with it
consider then
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
a family of distinct propositional variables
and the substitution
\theta[X_{1}\mapsto F_{1};\dots;X_{n}\mapsto F_{n}]

then consider the (unique) atomic formula labelling
such that
\tau^{\prime}(p_{i})=X_{i}

then
S\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.50003pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}\tau^{\prime}(S(1)),\dots,\tau^{\prime}(S(n))
.
Indeed then applying
\theta

to the sequent
\tau^{\prime}(S(1)),\dots,\tau^{\prime}(S(n))

will result in
\tau(S(1)),\dots,\tau(S(n))

which is
\Gamma
.
∎

See
82

Proof.

Using remark
81

and the fact that the tests of
a formula
B
in
\Delta

are proofs of
B^{\bot}

and by proposition
33

are proofs of
A^{\bot}
.
∎

Appendix G
Complements to
section 6

G.1
Decomposition

Proposition 176

(Decomposition)
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be an interpretation basis,

\mathcal{H}
be an hypersequent,
A,B
two formulas
and
S
be a multiplicative net.

•

If

S=S_{0}+l
has a terminal
\invamp
link
l
above its last conclusion
and
S\in\llbracket\mathcal{H},A\invamp B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}
;
then
S_{0}
belongs to
\llbracket\mathcal{H},A,B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

•

If

S=S_{0}+l
has a terminal
\invamp
link
l
above its last conclusion
and
S\in\llbracket\mathcal{H}\parallel A\invamp B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}
;
then
S_{0}
belongs to
\llbracket\mathcal{H}\parallel(A,B)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

•

If

S=S_{0}+l
has a terminal
\otimes
link
l
above its last conclusion
and
S\in\llbracket\mathcal{H},A\otimes B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}
;
then
S_{0}
belongs to
\llbracket\mathcal{H},(A\parallel B)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

•

If

S=S_{0}+l
has a terminal
\otimes
link
l
above its last conclusion
and
S\in\llbracket\mathcal{H}\parallel A\otimes B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}
;
then
S_{0}
belongs to
\llbracket\mathcal{H}\parallel A\parallel B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

Proof.

•

Consider
S
a net with a terminal
\invamp
–link
l

decomposing
S=S_{0}+l
such that
l
outputs the only conclusion of
S
.
Say
S
belongs to
\llbracket A\invamp B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

equivalently

S
is orthogonal to
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}\otimes\llbracket B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}
,
since a multiplicative cut can always be performed first
this implies
that
S_{0}
is orthogonal to
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}\parallel\llbracket B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}

thus
S_{0}
belongs to
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}\Yright\llbracket B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}
.
By allowing types
to contain cuts,
this arguments easily adapts to
\llbracket\mathcal{H},A,B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}
.

•

The reasonment for a net in
\llbracket A\otimes B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

is similar and also easily adapts to
the case
\llbracket\mathcal{H},A\otimes B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}
.

•

Consider on the other hand a
net
S
in
\llbracket\mathcal{H}\parallel A\otimes B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}
,
such that
S=S_{0}+l
where
l
is a tensor link and outputs the last conclusion of
S
.
Equivalently
S
is a net orthogonal to

\llbracket\mathcal{H}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}\Yright\llbracket A\otimes B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}

i.e.
\llbracket\mathcal{H}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}\Yright\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}\invamp\llbracket B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}
.
Since the multiplicative cut commute to the left (
103
)
we derive
that

S_{0}
is orthogonal to

\llbracket\mathcal{H}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}\Yright\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}\Yright\llbracket B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}
.
Equivalently (by duality)

S_{0}
belongs to

\llbracket\mathcal{H}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}\parallel\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}\parallel\llbracket B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}
.

•

Similarly we treat the case
\llbracket\mathcal{H}\parallel A\invamp B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}
.
Consider on the other hand a
net
S
in
\llbracket\mathcal{H}\parallel A\invamp B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}
,
such that
S=S_{0}+l
where
l
is a parr link and outputs the last conclusion of
S
.

Equivalently
S
is a net orthogonal to

\llbracket\mathcal{H}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}\Yright\llbracket A\invamp B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}

i.e.
\llbracket\mathcal{H}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}\Yright\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}\otimes\llbracket B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}
.
Because multiplicative cuts can always be performed
first in a reduction sequence (
103
)
is follows that

S_{0}
is ortogonal to

\llbracket\mathcal{H}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}\Yright\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}\parallel\llbracket B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}
.
Equivalently

S_{0}
belongs to
\llbracket\mathcal{H},A\parallel B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}
.

∎

G.2
The merge construction belongs to the composition

From
127

we can derive easily
that an explicitly defined
type
(called merge and denoted
\mathbf{A}\bowtie\mathbf{B}
) belongs to
the composition of two types

\mathbf{A}\Yright\mathbf{B}
.
This will be used to show that
the functional composition
(\Yright)
and
the parallel composition
(\parallel)

of full types remains full
(that is the
181

of
Appendix G
)
therefore by duality this will imply
that the parallel and functional composition
of daimon types is a daimon type.

Definition 177
.

Given
A
and
B
two sets of nets
we denote by
A\bowtie B
the following set:

\{a\bowtie^{(d,d^{\prime})}b\mid a\in A,b\in B,\quad d\in E_{a},\ell_{a}(d)=\maltese,\quad d\in E_{b},\ell_{b}(d)=\maltese\}.

Proposition 178

(Merges belongs to the compositional construction)
.

Given
\mathbf{A}

and
\mathbf{B}

two types;

(\mathbf{A}\bowtie\mathbf{B})^{\bot\bot}\subseteq\mathbf{A}\Yright\mathbf{B}.

Proof.

Consider
S

a net in
A\bowtie B
.

S
may therefore be written as

a\bowtie^{(d_{a},d_{b})}b
for two elements
a\in\mathbf{A}

and
b\in\mathbf{B}

with
d_{a}
a daimon of
a

and
d_{b}
a daimon of
b
.
We can thus decompose

b
as
d_{b}+b_{0}

and

a\bowtie^{(d_{a},d_{b})}b
is equal to

(a\bowtie^{(d_{a}),d_{b}}d_{b})+b_{0}
(
123
).

Now consider an element

\overline{a}
of
\mathbf{A}
,
then

a
and
\overline{a}
are orthogonal,
therefore

(a\bowtie^{(d_{a}),d_{b}}d_{b})::\overline{a}
reduces to
d_{b}
(
127
).
Furthermore

(a\bowtie^{(d_{a}),d_{b}}d_{b})+b_{0}::\overline{a}

is equal to

((a\bowtie^{(d_{a}),d_{b}}d_{b})::\overline{a})+b_{0}

because
\overline{a}
only interacts with the conclusions of
a

thus that net reduces to

d_{b}+b_{0}
which is exactly
b

and so belongs to
\mathbf{B}
.
Thus it follows that
the net

((a\bowtie^{(d_{a}),d_{b}}d_{b})+b_{0})::\overline{a}

that is
a\bowtie^{(d_{a},d_{b})}b_{0}::\overline{a}
belongs to
\mathbf{B}

for any
\overline{a}\in\mathbf{A}

thus that it belongs to
\mathbf{A}\Yright\mathbf{B}
.

We have shown

\mathbf{A}\bowtie\mathbf{B}\subseteq\mathbf{A}\Yright\mathbf{B}

and thus since inclusion is stable under bi orthogonal,

(\mathbf{A}\bowtie\mathbf{B})^{{}^{\bot\bot}}\subseteq\mathbf{A}\Yright\mathbf{B}

∎

G.3
Full Types and daimon types

We introduce some terminology regarding types
in order to identify
types which contain only nets
whose conclusions are outputs of daimon links.
Such basis are for instance daimon basis,
and, one can easily see that
\mathbf{1}
is a daimon basis.

Definition 179
.

A type
\mathbf{A}

is
full

when for each
1\leq i\leq\#\mathbf{A}

there exists a net
S
(resp.
T
)
such that
its conclusion
S(i)
(resp.
T(i)
)
is the output of a
\invamp
–link (resp.
\otimes
–link).

Remark 180
.

Observe that in a daimon type
\mathbf{A}

each conclusion of a net
S\in\mathbf{A}

is the output of a daimon link (
185
):
as a consequence there exists an atomic (that is, containing only propositional variable)
sequent
\Gamma

made of
\#\mathbf{A}
formulas
such that any cut free net
S
of
\mathbf{A}

is testable by
\Gamma

i.e.

S\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.50003pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}\Gamma

Proposition 181
.

Given two full types

\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
;

•

Their functional composition

\mathbf{A}\Yright\mathbf{B}
is still full.

•

Their parallel composition

\mathbf{A}\parallel\mathbf{B}
is still full.

Given two daimon types

\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
;

•

Their functional composition

\mathbf{A}\Yright\mathbf{B}
is still a daimon type.

•

Their parallel composition

\mathbf{A}\parallel\mathbf{B}
is still a daimon type.

Proof.

Indeed the parallel
\mathbf{A}\parallel\mathbf{B}

remains full since it contains in particular
the nets of the form
a\parallel b

with
a\in\mathbf{A}

and
b\in\mathbf{B}
.
Similarly this remains true for
\mathbf{A}\Yright\mathbf{B}

since it contains the merge
\mathbf{A}\bowtie\mathbf{B}

(
178
).

For the daimon types
we conclude using the duality results
(
51
).
∎

Proposition 182
.

Let
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{A}^{\bot}

be two orthogonal and non–empty types,
the assertions are equivalent:

•

\mathbf{A}
is a daimon type.

•

Each conclusion of a net
S
of
\mathbf{A}

is the output of a daimon link.

Proof.

\mathbf{1\Rightarrow 2.}

This is already discussed in
185
.
A net
S
in
\mathbf{A}

is orthogonal to any nets in
\mathbf{A}^{\bot}
,
and because
\mathbf{A}^{\bot}
is full
the conclusions of
S
cannot be the outputs of connectives
otherwise a clashing cut will
occur in some interaction
S::T

for some
T
in
\mathbf{A}^{\bot}
.

\mathbf{2\Rightarrow 1.}

This requires a much more detailed analysis
of the rewriting rules of cut elimination.
We will not give the detail here
because this implication is not used in this work
and the proof
requires the addition of a lot
of content in order to be obtained.
∎

G.4
Daimon Basis are Compact – proof of
83

The object of this section
is to provide a proof of
Theorem 187
.
Let us define
compact basis

as basis which satisfy the first item of
82
;
such a basis if also enjoys adequacy, will obviously
be complete for
\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}
(using
82
).

Definition 183
.

A basis
\mathcal{B}

is
compact

if for any
\Gamma

and any net
S
;

S\Vdash_{\mathcal{B}}\Gamma\Rightarrow S\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.50003pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}\Gamma
.

To obtain
\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}
–completeness (for cut–free nets)

82

ensure that it is enough
to find a basis
that is approximable and compact:
such kind of basis are
daimon
basis (
Theorem 187
),
for instance the basis mapping
each propositional variable to
\smash{\{\maltese_{1}\}^{{}^{\bot\bot}}}
.
Technically this is where
it is useful to interpret hypersequents;
a basis is compact is equivalently
a basis such that for any hypersequent
\mathcal{H}
which is
\invamp-
and
\otimes
–free
the interpretation
\llbracket\mathcal{H}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

contain only nets of whose conclusions are targets of daimon links.

Definition 184

(Daimon type)
.

A type
\mathbf{A}
is a

daimon type

whenever for each
1\leq i\leq\#\mathbf{A}

its dual
\mathbf{A}^{\bot}

contains
a net
S
with a terminal

\invamp
–link which outputs
S(i)

and a net
T
with a terminal
\otimes
–link
which outputs
T(i)
.
A basis
\mathcal{B}
is a

daimon basis

when for each propositional variable
X

its interpretation
\llbracket X\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

is a daimon type.

Remark 185
.

The conclusions
of a net which belong to a daimon type
\mathbf{A}

must be the outputs of daimon links (
46
).

A daimon basis is indeed
a compact basis;
the elements of a (non–empty) daimon type
can only have conclusions which are outputs of a daimon link
otherwise clashing cuts occur (
46
),
then an induction on the formulas show compactedness of the basis.
First we must show a more general result which holds on hypersequents.

There is a simple inductive process to associate
a sequent
\downarrow\mathcal{H}
to an hypersequent
\mathcal{H}
;

\downarrow A\triangleq A

while

\downarrow\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2}\triangleq\downarrow\mathcal{H}_{1},\downarrow\mathcal{H}_{2}

and

\downarrow\mathcal{H}_{1}\parallel\mathcal{H}_{2}\triangleq\downarrow\mathcal{H}_{1},\downarrow\mathcal{H}_{2}
.

Lemma 186

(Daimon Basis Property)
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a daimon basis.
For any cut–free net
S
;

S\in\llbracket\mathcal{H}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}\Rightarrow S\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.62222pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}\downarrow{\mathcal{H}}.

Proof.

of lemma
186
.
By induction on the hypersequent
using the measure
(c,n)

where
c
is the number of connectives in the hypersequent
and
n
is the size of the hypersequent.

•

If the hypersequent is made of one atomic formula
X

then
S\in\llbracket X\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

implies that
S
belongs to a daimon type,
thus the outputs of
S
are outputs of a daimon (
185
).
This by definition means
S\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.50003pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}X

and therefore
S\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.50003pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}X
.

•

Say the hypersequent is made only of atomic formulas.
The hypersequent may be of the form

\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2}

then
\llbracket\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}=(\llbracket\mathcal{H}_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}\parallel\llbracket\mathcal{H}_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot})^{\bot}

since,

\llbracket\mathcal{H}_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}

and
\llbracket\mathcal{H}_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}

are open types their parallel composition
remains an open type.

\llbracket\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

is the orthogonal of an full type hence
it is a daimon type.
Thus any sequent in that type
as outputs which comes from a daimon link
and thus is the approximation of any sequent of size
n

in particular
S\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.50003pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}\downarrow\mathcal{H}
.
We do a similar reasonment when
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{1}\parallel\mathcal{H}_{2}
.

•

Case of non–atomic hypersequent
with a virgula as main connective.
Assume that
S
has a terminal connective link,
say the hypersequent is of the form
\mathcal{H},A\invamp B

such that
S=S_{0}+l

where
l
is a
\invamp
–link and
is the last conclusion of
S
.
By proposition
176

it follows that
S_{0}

belongs to
\llbracket\mathcal{H},A,B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

the measure of that hypersequent as decreased and
so we apply the induction hypothesis;

S_{0}\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.50003pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}\downarrow(\mathcal{H},A,B)

indeed it follows that
S\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.50003pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}\downarrow(\mathcal{H},A\invamp B)
.
A similar argument works for an hypersequent of the form
\mathcal{H},A\otimes B
.

•

Case of non–atomic hypersequent
with a parallel as main connective.
On the other hand say

S
belongs to the interpretation
\llbracket\mathcal{H}\parallel A\invamp B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

then it is orthogonal to
\llbracket\mathcal{H}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}\Yright\llbracket A\invamp B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}

and thus
S_{0}
is orthogonal to
\llbracket\mathcal{H}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}\Yright\llbracket A,B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}
.
Equivalently using proposition
176

S_{0}
belongs to
\llbracket\mathcal{H}\parallel A,B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

the size of the hypersequent as decreased and so we can apply the induction hypothesis;

S_{0}\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.50003pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}\downarrow(\mathcal{H},A,B)

and thus
S\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.50003pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}\downarrow(\mathcal{H},A\invamp B)
.
A similar argument works for an hypersequent of the form
\mathcal{H}\parallel A\otimes B
.
Similarly we treat the case of
\mathcal{H},A\otimes B
.

∎

Indeed the previous lemma is a more general form of
Theorem 187

as stated in this subsection.
One can now easily derive it:

Theorem 187
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be an approximable daimon basis,

\Gamma
a sequent,
and
S
a cut–free net;

S\Vdash_{\mathcal{B}}\Gamma\Rightarrow S\vdash_{\mathsf{MLL}}^{\maltese}\Gamma
.

From this theorem
the fact that the base
\mathbf{1}
is compact easily follows,
because one can show that
\mathbf{1}
is a daimon basis.

See
83

Proof.

Observe that the type
\{\maltese_{1}\}^{{}^{\bot\bot}}

is indeed a daimon type
(
47
),
therefore the basis mapping each
propositional variable
X
to
\{\maltese_{1}\}^{{}^{\bot\bot}}

is a daimon basis.
We conclude using
Theorem 187
.
∎

G.5
Proof of
Theorem 85

See
85

Proof.

As for (1)
note that if
S
realises
\Gamma

for any basis
\mathcal{B}

then in particular it realises
\Gamma
for
\mathbf{1}
.
Thus applying
83

S
must be testable by
\Gamma
,
furthermore
\mathbf{1}
is approximable
hence applying
82

we conclude
S\vdash_{\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}}\Gamma
.

As for (2)
to have the implication that
S\in\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

yields
S\vdash_{\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}}\Gamma

simply use the same argument as for point (1)

\mathbf{1}
is an approximable basis.
To show that

is exactly the theorem of adequacy (

).

,

The
