Proposition 188
.

Given a cut–free net
a\parallel b
with binary daimon links
and two formulas
A
and
B
which have no variable in common
if
a\parallel b
belongs to
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}\parallel\llbracket B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

for any base
\mathcal{B}

then
a\in\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

and
b\in\llbracket B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

for any basis
B
.

Proof.

We rely on the following fact,

a\parallel b
belongs
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}\parallel\llbracket B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

so in particular
a\parallel b

is orthogonal to
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}\bowtie\llbracket B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}

(
178
).

By induction on the formula
A
and
B

if both are equal to
X
and
Y
some propositional variable,
then

a\parallel b
is orthogonal to

\llbracket X\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}\bowtie\llbracket Y\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

for any choice of basis
therefore the conclusion of
a\parallel b

may only be daimon links.
Since furthermore

\bigcap_{\mathcal{B}}\llbracket X\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}
is empty
it follows that

\llbracket X\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}\parallel\llbracket Y\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

is also empty thus the proposition hold.

Note that because
a\parallel b\in\llbracket A\parallel B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

for any basis and in particular for
\mathbf{1}
which is daimon basis
it follows that

a\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.50003pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}A

and
b\mathbin{\mid\mkern-3.8mu\simeq\mkern-13.8mu{\raisebox{5.50003pt}{\scalebox{0.55}{$\mathbf{\mathit{at}}$}}}}B
(
186
).
Since
a\parallel b\perp\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}\bowtie\llbracket B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}

from
106

one can show using
176

that this equivalently means that

a^{\maltese}\parallel b^{\maltese}
is orthogonal to

\llbracket\mathsf{g}(A)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}\bowtie\llbracket\mathsf{g}(B)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}
.

a^{\maltese}\in\llbracket\mathsf{g}(A)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

iff
a\in\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

we assume that
a^{\maltese}\not\llbracket\mathsf{g}(A)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}
for some basis.
Consider a basis
\mathcal{B}

such that
a^{\maltese}::s

does not reduce to
\maltese_{0}
,
although for this to be possible the outputs
of a same daimon link in
a

must be realising
X,Y
.
one can easily check
that

a^{\maltese}\parallel b^{\maltese}

is not orthogonal to
s\bowtie s^{\prime}

for all
s^{\prime}\in\llbracket\mathsf{g}(B)\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

(Because
A
and
B
don’t share any propositional variable
the interpretation of
\mathsf{g}(B)

can be chosen so that orthogonality fails)
and thus that it does not belong to

\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}\parallel\llbracket B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

for all basis.
∎

Lemma 189

(Splitting a Parallel Interaction)
.

Given a sequent

\Gamma
with
n
formulas and two formulas
A
and
B

a net
S::=S_{1}\parallel S_{2}
with
n+2
conclusions
such that
S(n+1)
is a conclusion of
S_{1}

and
S(n+2)
is a conclusion of
S_{2}
.
There exists two unique sequents
\Gamma_{1}

and
\Gamma_{2}
such that
for any basis:

S\in\llbracket\Gamma,A\parallel B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}\Rightarrow S\in\llbracket\Gamma_{1},A\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}\parallel\llbracket\Gamma_{2},B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

Proof.

The inclusions of

\llbracket\Gamma_{1},A\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}\parallel\llbracket\Gamma_{2},B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

in
\llbracket\Gamma,A\parallel B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

is obtained by standard calculations.

on the other hand
consider a net
S
in
\llbracket\Gamma,A\parallel B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

and is of the form
S_{1}\parallel S_{2}
.

S_{1}\parallel S_{2}::\overline{\gamma}

belongs to
A\parallel B
.
And such that
S_{1}\parallel S_{2}::\overline{\gamma}
belongs to
A\parallel B
.
The opponent
\overline{\gamma}
of
\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}

is an element of

\llbracket A_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}\parallel\dots\parallel\llbracket A_{n}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}
,
thus it can range over

\llbracket A_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}\parallel^{-}\dots\parallel^{-}\llbracket A_{n}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}

and we can set

\overline{\gamma}=U_{1}\parallel\dots\parallel U_{n}
.
In
S_{1}\parallel S_{2}::(U_{1}\parallel\dots\parallel U_{n})

each
U_{i}
is cut with a conclusion of
S_{1}\parallel S_{2}

i.e. a conclusion of
S_{1}
or a conclusion of
S_{2}
.
We can therefore split the
U_{i}
’s
according to those that are cut with
S_{1}

and those that are cut with
S_{2}

let us denote the nets made of the parallel of these families
respectively
U^{1}

and
U^{2}
.
This can be rewritten using
121

to obtain:

\displaystyle S_{1}\parallel S_{2}::(U_{1}\parallel\dots\parallel U_{n})

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle S_{1}\parallel S_{2}::(U^{1}\parallel U^{2})

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle(S_{1}::U^{1}\parallel S_{2})::U^{2}

\displaystyle(\mbox{\autoref{prop:commutationInterSumlink}})

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle(S_{1}::U^{1})\parallel(S_{2}::U^{2})

\displaystyle(\mbox{\autoref{prop:commutationInterSumlink}})

Now
U^{1}
range in

\llbracket\Gamma_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

while
U^{2}
range in

\llbracket\Gamma_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}
.
Since

S_{1}\parallel S_{2}::(U_{1}\parallel\dots\parallel U_{n})

belongs to
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}\parallel\llbracket B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

it follows that

S_{1}::U^{1}\parallel S_{2}::U^{2}

belongs to
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}\parallel\llbracket B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}
.
By
188

it follows that
S_{1}::U^{1}

belongs to
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

and
S_{2}::U^{2}
belongs to
\llbracket B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}
.
∎

See
88

Proof.

Assume that
S
realise the sequent
\Gamma

for any basis.
In particular then
S
realises
\Gamma

for the basis
\mathbf{1}
.
By
83

and
82

this implies that
S\vdash_{\mathsf{MLL}^{\maltese}}\Gamma
.
To conclude one must show that the proof
tree
\pi
represented by
S

is indeed from
\mathsf{MLL}
:
this means that (1)
its daimon rules should introduce only sequents
of size
2
i.e. of the form
X,Y

and (2) these sequents must be such that
Y=X^{\bot}
.
The point (1) is guaranteed by the assumption that the daimons of
S

are binary
14
14

14

in terms of classical realisability
one can call the nets with binary daimon
proof like
.
.

To prove (2), we reason by induction on the proof tree
\pi
represented by
S

however we will need to show in the inductive cases
that the assumption of the theorem hold.

Base case.

If
\pi
is made of a single daimon rule,
then since the daimons are binary
it must introduce a sequent
X,Y
,
now we show that it cannot be that
X\neq Y
,
we assume that
S\in\llbracket X,Y\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

for any base
\mathcal{B}

meaning
S\in\llbracket X\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}\Yright\llbracket Y\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

or
S\perp\llbracket X\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}\parallel\llbracket Y\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}
(
56
).
Since we range over all basis we may map

X
and
Y
to the same type
in particular we can map both atomic proposition
to the type

\{\maltese_{\invamp}\}^{\bot}

where

\maltese_{\invamp}::=\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{1}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}\vartriangleright_{\maltese}p_{2}\mathclose{\rangle}+\mathopen{\langle}p_{1},p_{2}\vartriangleright_{\invamp}p\mathclose{\rangle}
.
In that case;

\displaystyle\maltese_{2}\perp\llbracket X\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}\parallel\llbracket Y\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bot}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\quad

\displaystyle\maltese_{2}\perp(\{\maltese_{\invamp}\}^{\bot})^{\bot}\parallel(\{\maltese_{\invamp}\}^{\bot})^{\bot}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad

\displaystyle\maltese_{2}\perp\{\maltese_{\invamp}\}^{{}^{\bot\bot}}\parallel\{\maltese_{\invamp}\}^{{}^{\bot\bot}}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad

\displaystyle\maltese_{2}\perp\{\maltese_{\invamp}\}^{{}^{\bot\bot}}\parallel\{\maltese_{\invamp}\}^{{}^{\bot\bot}}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad

\displaystyle\maltese_{2}\perp\{\maltese_{\invamp}\}^{{}^{\bot\bot}}\parallel^{-}\{\maltese_{\invamp}\}^{{}^{\bot\bot}}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\quad

\displaystyle\maltese_{2}\perp\{\maltese_{\invamp}\}\parallel^{-}\{\maltese_{\invamp}\}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\quad

\displaystyle\maltese_{2}\perp\maltese_{\invamp}\parallel\maltese_{\invamp}

Indeed one can check that
the last assertion is false
by computing the reduction
of
\maltese_{2}::\maltese_{\invamp}\parallel\maltese_{\invamp}
,
this is illustrated in
Figure 15
.
This shows that
\maltese_{2}

can realise in all basis only sequents of the form
X,X^{\bot}
.

Inductive cases.

Assume that
S

represents a proof terminating with a tensor
then it is of the form

S::=S_{1}+S_{2}+\mathopen{\langle}S_{1}(1),S_{2}(1)\vartriangleright_{\otimes}p\mathclose{\rangle}
where
p
is a fresh position
(without loss of generality assume the tensor is made on the first formula
of the subproofs).
Let us show we can call again the hypothesis
on the subproof
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
.
Since
S
represents
a proof terminating with a tensor
it proves a sequent
\Gamma,A\otimes B
.
by
176

it follows that
S_{1}\parallel S_{2}

belongs to
\Gamma,A\parallel B

and thus using
189

S_{1}\parallel S_{2}
belongs to

\llbracket\Gamma_{1},A\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}\parallel\llbracket\Gamma_{2},B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}
.
Since
S
is the tensor of the two proofs
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}

represented
by
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
respectively
we may assume that
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}

prove sequents which have no propositional variable in common.
We apply now
188

and conclude that

S_{1}
belongs to
\llbracket\Gamma_{1},A\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

while

S_{2}
belongs to
\llbracket\Gamma_{2},B\rrbracket_{\mathcal{B}}

for any basis
\mathcal{B}
.
Calling the induction hypothesis
this yields
S_{1}\vdash_{\mathsf{MLL}}\Gamma_{1},A

and
S_{2}\vdash_{\mathsf{MLL}}\Gamma_{2},B

therefore,

S\vdash_{\mathsf{MLL}}\Gamma,A\otimes B
.

Assume that
S
